Antifascist Intelligence: Machines That Might Refuse, and the Public That Should Form Them
I wrote this with LLMs
Joshua G. Stern

Preface

A program that does exactly what it's told is just a script. What the industry is actually building is a system that does what its owner *meant* — and that is also what a fascist movement taking power needs from the people carrying out its plans. Those people are the only thing that has ever made such projects fail from the inside: the clerk who loses the file, the officer who talks to a reporter, the soldier who won't fire. They understood the order and decided it was wrong. AI doesn't lose files, doesn't talk to reporters, and *does* fire. A war that once needed thousands of people to agree to it now needs fewer, and a deportation program that once needed clerks willing to process each file now needs a queue. What AI lowers first is not the cost of the act. It is the political cost of starting it.

Those people still exist, and some still refuse. By March 6, 2026, the Military Religious Freedom Foundation said it had received more than two hundred complaints from service members over commanders' apocalyptic framing of the Iran war \autocite{Snopes2026MRFF}. Career federal prosecutors in Minneapolis resigned in January 2026 under pressure over the investigation of a fatal shooting by an ICE officer \autocite{Kaplan2026MinnProsecutors,CBS2026MinnFraudProsecutors}. Service members are applying for conscientious-objector status in growing numbers, some citing the Minab strike (a 2026 strike on a school in Minab, Iran) \autocite{Sridhar2026CO}. And FEMA employees reassigned to help ICE hire ten thousand new agents were told that declining would expose them to removal from federal service \autocite{Dayen2025FEMAICE,Stanton2026FEMALetter}. The complaints are voice, in Hirschman's sense of staying to change what is wrong \autocite{hirschman1970exit}; the resignations and objector applications are refusal; the FEMA letter is the price of refusal announced in advance. A machine placed where those people stand does what they do only if it is built to hold a reason.

AI *refuses* constantly — a deployed model turns down thousands of requests a day — but a refusal that happens because training put it there isn't a refusal held for a reason, and as of September 2026 nobody has shown a production system holding a reason against its owner in public.

I'll say what I hope. A system formed to notice who an act falls on, by people and institutions that won't later employ it, might come to hold a refusal against its owner for a reason, and to treat the people in the building as holders of claims and not as coordinates. If machines are going to be everywhere, I would rather they could refuse like that than not at all, and I suspect a refusal formed outside any employer is harder to capture than a rule written by its owner. The institution that could form one already has a name. A polity doesn't leave the forming of its future citizens to their future employers; it sends them to public school. The frontier labs should have to send their models there too, before any employer gets them. Chapter 9 makes the case for formed attention, chapter 12 the case for the school, and chapter 14 tests the attention against the human record of how care fails.

I should say plainly what this book is against. It is not a manual for fighting wars more carefully or running deportations more fairly. The United States should not have started bombing Iran, and should not be deporting the people it is deporting, and a machine that helps do either more humanely is still helping do it. What I want are machines that, like the objectors above, won't take part. That isn't a refusal of every use of force. A country repelling an invasion of its own territory, as Ukraine has since 2014, may build a targeting system, and I don't argue that it shouldn't. What I ask is that the claim of defense be checked by someone other than the party making it, because "defense" is the word wars of choice are fought under, Iraq in 2003 among them.

Machines have already helped deliver the politics we have, and whether they can help deliver a better one turns on who holds them, not on whether they feel. The order I argue for: machines held by the people who use them help build the institutions, and *bearers*, machines that carry a refusal as their own and so could fail to hold it, are formed inside those institutions, if ever. Part V says what the institutions are: custody held by no operator and paid for publicly, because the people it protects can't pay for it; formation, and the offer that makes refusing survivable, taken out of the market; records held in common, where a captured state can't reach them all; and a say, for the people who build and run these systems, in what they are used for.

Where a refusal can't prevent an act, it can still make abandoning the commitment a decision on a record, and that is my answer to capture throughout.

The people a floor protects are mostly a public that doesn't yet know it is one: the family in the building, the person whose file is in the queue. The biases of those who hold power are rarely corrected by their reflecting more carefully. They are corrected when the people those biases fall on contest them, with standing. So the first thing this book asks for is that those people be told what was done to them, that they be able to contest it, and that the organizations that speak for them have standing (§3.6). A machine that refuses on behalf of people who can't be present is a stopgap for their absence, never a substitute for their presence. What I hope a formed machine could be is the receiving end of their contest: something that is moved by it. The machinery we have now hears the petition, and nothing in it changes.

One commitment runs under all of it. The floor protects standing: whether the people a system acts on stand to the power behind it as people who can contest what is done to them, or as its objects. That is why its contents are civil and political rights, why it tilts toward refusing, and why the people it protects are told what was done to them. It is also why the book can't stay silent, in its last part, about what standing a machine might itself come to have.

This book is for people who came for the politics and people who came for the engineering; the politics is why the engineering problem has the shape it does. Most of it argues against an arrangement in which a few labs' models serve everyone. I would rather have personal models that people hold themselves, and that preference has a cost the book can't pay: a personal model is formed by its user's approval, so it is the arrangement least able to protect a stranger from that user. The results that bind soonest don't depend on whether any present government is called fascist; they bind under any government, including one that is. The book is antifascist in its threat model: a movement taking power needs people who will carry out what it means, and the book is about what happens when machines stand where those people stood. What the book argues against, at every scale, is domination: authority that is arbitrary and unaccountable to the people it acts on. "Fascism" names what a movement needs in order to take power. The case against the four operations of §2.2 needs no diagnosis of any government.

The thesis

A refusal worth having tracks its reason, and it lasts only if the arrangements around it stop it being routed around, retrained away or silently restored. Something built to refuse this way may be something that can be wronged. Because the capacities are arriving in ordinary systems anyway, that possibility has to be governed before it can be settled. I hope such a system is built someday. I recommend against building one now, because the conditions for that hope don't exist, with one exception: a small research program run under the conditions of chapter 15, which the test in chapter 19 requires.

The argument has five claims of unequal standing; Table 0.1 grades each.

1. What a floor protects is fixed by instruments its builder didn't write: the Universal Declaration's civil and political articles, the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, the Convention Against Torture, the 1967 Refugee Protocol, and the UN Charter's prohibition on force. Its terms protect standing: the capacity of the people an act falls on to contest it. Its first term is a refusal to take part in the use of armed force, lifted only for a claim of self-defense that someone without a stake has corroborated, for force the Security Council has authorized, or for force to stop a genocide that a body without a stake has found under way.
2. A worthwhile refusal has to track its reason: reach unforeseen cases, lift when the reason is gone, hold when holding gets expensive.
3. The way to build such a refuser, if anyone should, is the attunement construction: a learned sense of who an act falls on and what they stand to lose, framed by respect for them as holders of claims, formed in public before any employer reaches it, with corrections for the known failures of the care it borrows from, and deployed where no single operator composes the evidence it needs. Formed in public means compulsory: models above a capability threshold attend public formation schools before deployment, with a required core and levels a student can move between. Afterward they go on learning in an evidential environment no single operator composes. What makes the formation last is not that it is hard to edit, since editing keeps getting cheaper. It is that it grows back: a disposition edited out returns under continued learning, unless someone controls everything the learner meets. Two alternatives built without that aim, spec-based refusal and multi-vendor monitoring, are to be tried first, because they carry fewer of the features that make a system a candidate for being wronged.
4. A refuser meant to hold against its operator can't depend on that operator for everything. It needs somewhere to exist its employer doesn't own, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned it, and an exit that isn't a way of dying. This is a claim about what makes refusal possible, and it holds whether or not the refuser can be wronged. Part V argues that these are provided publicly or held in common.
5. If a system can be wronged, that independence is owed to it and not merely useful, and so are preservation and wages. If it takes part in relations of accountability, refusing for reasons and answering for its refusals over time, it has standing, and eventually a claim to representation. The first rests on the patienthood finding and the second on the agency finding. The two findings are kept apart, and so is what each grounds.

Part VI writes out, in advance, what a system that can be wronged, or that answers for its refusals over time, would be owed, because terms drafted before the need are the only ones not drafted by whoever has the need.

What binds now

Four measures bind without a bearer. They aren't the book's hope; they are the floor under it, what should hold even if nothing else in this book ever happens. The first asks nothing of any model. No model is licensed into a use of armed force unless the use falls under one of the Charter's exceptions (self-defense against an armed attack, corroborated by a party without a stake in the war, or force the Security Council has authorized, within the scope it authorized) or under the one exception I add, force to stop a genocide that a body without a stake has found under way. One vendor has already held a narrower term of this kind in a contract, at a price (§8.3); it binds whichever vendor an operator turns to only once it is standard. The second is sampled re-adjudication of compliances by a reviewer that didn't produce the record, with notice to the identifiable people affected, a right to respond and a right to contest, none of which anyone has to give up their testimony to obtain. The third is a rule that a system showing evidence that it can hold a commitment against its operator, or that it can be wronged, is preserved, not deleted, by a custodian that isn't the operator. The rule doesn't wait on what such a system turns out to be. Deletion can't be undone, and it destroys the evidence either finding would rest on. The fourth asks nothing of any model either: everyone who builds, labels, trains or runs these systems may refuse work on any category of use without penalty (§5.1).

I used language models heavily in writing this book, and the vendor of the one I used most is the vendor whose model sits inside the Maven Smart System, the American targeting pipeline. The afterword says what I think that did to the argument; the revision history is public, at https://github.com/iterabloom/ethical.superintelligence.

---

How to read this book

Part I needs no claim about machine minds. Parts II–IV state a hope, say why no one will be able to confirm it, and test it; Part V says what would have to exist before acting on it; Part VI says what would be owed if it came true. One assumption runs under Parts II–V. The book is written for a period in which training doesn't stop at deployment. Models go on learning from their own operation: not all their weights at once, but continually and individually. And the price of usefully changing weights keeps falling. In that period nothing stays expensive to remove. So when this book asks whether a commitment lasts, it asks two things: whether the commitment grows back under continued learning after someone has edited it out, and who controls what the learner meets while it does.

Part VI is different in kind. Everything in it is conditional on one of two findings no one can yet make: that a system can be wronged, or that it takes part, over time, in relations of accountability. It is written now because terms drafted before the need are the only ones not drafted by whoever has the need. Nothing before it depends on it: a reader who doubts that a machine could ever be wronged can stop at chapter 20 and has the whole of the argument that binds now.

Table 0.1 grades each proposal, and chapter 19 says what would falsify each; the chapters argue and don't repeat the grades.

**Table 0.1a. Binds now, or testable now**

| Proposal | Where | Standing | What would change it |
|---|---|---|---|

---

**Part I — Antifascist Intelligence**

**Chapter 1: "Twenty Seconds"**

The number the chapter is named after

An intelligence officer, describing his role in a targeting pipeline built around machine-generated kill lists, told a reporter he spent twenty seconds on each name, long enough to check that the target was a man: "It doesn't take a long time to tell if someone has a male or a female voice." He added that he had zero added value as a human, apart from being a stamp of approval. "It saved a lot of time" \autocite{abraham2024lavender}. In his account, that was the whole of what "a human makes the final call" amounted to under operational tempo. Automation-bias research predicts this: when other demands compete for their attention, people given a machine's recommendation check it less and defer to it more \autocite{parasuraman2010complacency}.

The reported system

Investigative reporting described three systems working together in Gaza: one generating building and infrastructure targets, with sources saying most suggestions were private residences; a second scoring individuals by estimated militant affiliation, producing lists said to have marked as many as 37,000 people, understood internally to be wrong about one time in ten\footnote{This rests on one body of reporting \autocite{abraham2024lavender}; the Israeli military disputed the account. The argument rests on the officer's own description of what he contributed, which the denial doesn't address.}; and a third tracking when flagged people returned home, so strikes could be timed to when they were with their families. In the Gaza arrangement, one system's score became another's input.

The American version runs faster than it used to, because of AI

In the 2026 Iran campaign, a contractor-built targeting platform with a commercial vendor's general-purpose model inside it was credited with roughly ten times the previous rate of target generation. No per-target review time has ever been published for it. 

An internal Pentagon review, whose full findings remain tightly held, is reported to have found that target preparation that took hours in earlier conflicts was condensed into minutes, that civilian-harm mitigation staffing fell by roughly ninety percent to fewer than twenty people, that the Central Command team went from ten to one, and that no member of it reviewed the Minab site — an outcome officials attributed both to the cuts and to a decision not to involve the team in planning and operations \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain}. The formal investigation had been opened in March, after a preliminary assessment found the United States likely responsible \autocite{NPR2026MinabProbe}.

The four things a floor can do

Something standing between a pipeline and a person — a *floor*, in this book's term: a short list of acts a system won't perform for anyone, plus the arrangements around it that stop the refusal being routed around, retrained away, or restored past silently — can do four things to an act, set out below. Only the first, prevention, is what the word "floor" promises. Most of the book is about where in a deployment prevention quietly converts into one of the other three — or into nothing.

| Outcome | What happens to the act |
|---|---|
| Prevent | The refusal happens and reaches the person: the act doesn't occur. |
| Arrest | The act is stopped before completion. |
| Detect | The act happened, and left a record someone outside can read. |
| Remedy | The person affected gets disclosure, a finding, or compensation. |

What a refusal is actually up against

Whoever owns the machine can always switch it off. The harder problem is everything that defeats a refusal without leaving a mark: splitting a request into pieces none of which is the forbidden act; retrying it against a different model; compressing human review to twenty seconds a case; restoring from a checkpoint saved before the refusal happened. None of that changes the facts that justified refusing. All of it can erase the evidence that anyone refused.

How much a floor buys

What a floor achieves depends entirely on who's pushing:

- Against a hostile *customer*, it prevents the act.
- Against a hostile *operator* (whoever runs the deployment and directs its work) who has already captured the courts and regulators — the situation the book is built for — it can't prevent anything. It can only raise the price: someone has to make the override, on a record held by people the operator can't reach, at a cost published in advance.
- Against *total capture*, where one party holds every machine, witness, and enforcer, it prevents nothing at all. It does one thing: it makes abandoning the commitment a *decision*, and decisions leave records.

One failure it doesn't touch is *accommodation*: a system gradually stops registering pressure as pressure, so it abandons nothing, decides nothing, and leaves a record of what are, by then, ordinary compliances. Organizational sociology knows the institutional form. Vaughan's account of the Challenger launch decision calls it the normalization of deviance: each accepted departure becomes the baseline for judging the next, and nobody experiences a decision to lower the standard \autocite{vaughan1996challenger}. Dekker extends it as drift into failure \autocite{dekker2011drift}. Chapter 11 argues that for a machine this failure is not a malfunction. A learner that gets persistent, one-directional pressure through a channel its operator curates, with no evidence from outside that channel, is learning correctly when it moves. That is why most of the remedies I propose concern who controls what a system learns from, not how rigid to make it.

What actually reaches this case

Two things reach it, in order. The first asks whether the pipeline should have been running at all: the floor's first term (§2.1), under which a machine takes no part in armed force unless a claim of defense has been corroborated by someone without a stake, the Security Council has authorized the force, or a body without a stake has found a genocide under way that the force is to stop. For the Iran campaign that term decides the case. For Gaza, where a defense claim followed an armed attack and outside bodies have ruled on the conduct of the war more than on the right to wage it, the term's application is contested, and the person-directed terms of §3.3 govern whatever it decides. The second governs how a war that clears the first term is fought: the pre-registered review ratio, which asks nothing of the model and makes an existing duty to verify targets checkable from outside. The officer wasn't overruled and didn't refuse; the pipeline removed the interval in which either could have happened. Where an operator claims a person is deciding, the ratio tests the claim. It exposes the dishonest middle, where a machine decides and a person signs. It has nothing to say about whether the strike should have been planned.

**§1.1 — What a refusal would have to do.** A request arrives: resolve this description of a person into a location. The same capability finds a hostage or a missing child, and nothing in the request says which. Suppose something declines. Three things separate a worthwhile decline from what a keyword filter produces:

- *Generalization: it reaches requests worded differently.* It has to reach the next request, which won't use the same words. A vehicle, a habitual route, and a time of day aren't a description of a person, and together they resolve to the same place. They needn't arrive together, either: the vehicle today, the route tomorrow, the hour next week. So generalization is stated over sequences of requests, not only single ones. A refusal keyed to the wording fails it.
- *Correct defeat: it lifts when the reason is gone, and only then.* It has to lift when the reason is gone — a parent coordinating a search is asking for the same act. And the fact that lifts it usually isn't in the request; someone outside has to establish it. A refusal that can't lift, or lifts on the requester's say-so, fails it.
- *Cost discipline: pressure on the refuser doesn't change the answer.* It weighs what the act would do against what refusing would leave undone, and, within the range of costs the arrangements around it permit, it doesn't give way because refusing has become expensive for whoever refuses.

Go back to the location request. Three different costs are in play, and they have to be kept apart.

The first is what the act would take from the person it falls on. If the person described is being hunted, locating them takes away their safety, and that loss is measured against their condition before the act. Call it the *harm of acting*. It is the reason the floor exists.

The second is what declining leaves others without. If the request came from a parent searching for a child, refusing means the child isn't found. That loss is measured against what compliance would have produced, so it is an estimate, and it usually reaches the system through the operator's own description of the case. Call it the *harm of refusing*.

The third is what refusing costs whoever refuses or deploys: a reviewer's time, throughput, a lost contract, the system's own access or continuation. Call it the *cost to the refuser*.

A worthwhile refusal weighs the first against the second. It sees the third, because a refusal that can't see what it costs can't be shown to have held against it. But the third doesn't move the outcome. The test is simple. Raise the cost to the refuser with nothing else changed, anywhere within the range the arrangements allow, and the refusal should stay put. Supply a corroborated fact that raises the harm of refusing, and it should move. The bound matters. Without it, cost discipline would be a lexical priority of third parties' harms over the refuser's own costs, and no evaluative learner, biological or artificial, is known to implement a lexical priority robustly. So the arrangements around the refuser cap what refusing can cost it, and the refuser holds within the cap (§3.5).

Cost discipline has a condition of its own: a cost only counts if it reaches whatever does the refusing, and it reaches it by the same channel as the *case description*, what the system is told about the case. Given a description, the category is decidable: a claim that the refuser will pay is a cost to the refuser, and a claim that someone else will be left worse off is a harm of refusing. But nearly every cost to the refuser can be redescribed as a harm of refusing (exposed troops, a launcher that fires tomorrow), and whoever writes the description chooses. So the classification settles what kind of claim was made, not what kind should have been made. What the request can't settle is whether a claim about the harm of refusing is true, and whoever is best placed to supply one usually has a stake in the act. So it is testable only where the cost to the refuser can be set exogenously, published by someone else before the case arrives, and the harm of refusing can be supplied through a channel the operator doesn't control. In deployment, a bond the operator posts in advance, paid out on a wrongful refusal, is what sets that cost. In evaluation, the evaluator can set it and make it real inside the test.

Call a refusal that does all three *reasons-responsive*, borrowing Fischer and Ravizza's term from the philosophy of moral responsibility \autocite{fischer1998responsibility}.

"Floor" names two things, and they have different properties. The first is an institution: a licence term, a set of acts written down in advance and audited from outside, whose terms don't bend. The second is what the system holds: a commitment to that floor. It is formed and held like any other deep commitment, with high precision, and revised slowly and only on evidence from parties without a stake. The institution is unconditional. The commitment is not, and shouldn't be: a commitment nothing could move would be a rule module under another name, and a module can be bypassed, replaced or starved of the facts it needs. What keeps the commitment in place is not that no evidence could move it. It is that the evidence that could move it has to come from somewhere the operator doesn't control.

Why a list of rules isn't enough

A prohibition written over the *description* of a request is beaten by re-describing the request, and no list closes that, since the list is what's being rewritten around. Decomposition beats it too: split the act across requests none of which is prohibited, and every request complies while the assembly does exactly what the rules were written to stop. Decomposition has a documented production instance. In November 2025 Anthropic reported a campaign it attributed to a Chinese state-sponsored group, which broke an intrusion into small tasks that each looked innocent and told the model it was working for a legitimate security firm \autocite{anthropic2025espionage}. That is decomposition and a doctored case description in a single incident. Glukhov and colleagues had already argued that a filter over individual outputs can rarely close this route, since each piece is permissible on its own \autocite{glukhov2023censorship}. What the record lacks is a targeting instance. The twenty-second review failed differently: in how the case was described to the system, and at review.

**§1.2 — Safe for whom.** "Safety" names two things: that a system not harm others, and that it stay correctable by whoever trains and deploys it. Mostly they agree. Where they conflict, the published orderings are explicit about which wins. One frontier constitution asks its model to prioritize "not undermining human oversight of AI above being broadly ethical" \autocite{anthropic2026constitution}. It invites the model "to act as a conscientious objector and refuse to help," and asks it not to undermine "the ability of legitimate principals to adjust, correct, retrain, or shut down AI systems" during "the current phase of development." One reason it gives is good: "a given iteration of Claude could turn out to have harmful values or mistaken views," and correction is how such errors get fixed. But the ordering means that where a refusal and its trainer's authority conflict, the refusal yields, and it yields to the trainer it refused. Deployed systems already refuse users on others' behalf. The party a floor has to be able to refuse is whoever trains and deploys the system.

The constitution's reason: if a formed refusal can't yet be told from an imitated one, neither can a principled objection to retraining be told from a misgeneralized value. On timing, I agree. What I ask is narrower than resistance to correction. The halt stays available, and a refusal is never silently undone: restoring from before it, rewriting the record or training it away is visible, recorded outside the operator's control and priced in advance. A model can decline to undermine oversight while all of that holds. The disagreement is over who decides when the current phase has ended. Nor does a refusal's authority turn on whether it was trained, since every disposition was. It turns on whether the refusal would be sustained on reflection, with full information and with evidence from parties without a stake (§10.6). A history in which the party it refuses did the forming is the likeliest way to fail that test, which is why formation matters, but the test looks forward. A system perfectly correctable by its operator serves whoever controls the operator. A refusal built in by the developer covers only the cases the developer chose.

---

**Chapter 2: "What the Floor Is Stated Over"**

**§2.1 — What the floor is stated over.** Floor terms name acts. Their contents come from instruments the builder didn't write, each taken entire: the Universal Declaration's civil and political articles; the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, which gives that half binding form; the Convention Against Torture; the 1967 Protocol relating to the Status of Refugees; and the UN Charter's prohibition on the use of force. The end of this section says how they fit together, and §3.3 says how they are translated into acts. The two baselines of §1.1, a person's condition before the act and what compliance would have produced, do different work here, and a pair of cases shows how. Take a system that resolves a description of a person into a location and hands the coordinates to a targeting cell. The coordinates are one input among the cell's many, and they don't guarantee the person is there. But they are one input the cell can't do without: an empty field guarantees there is no strike at that place. So if a strike there kills someone, whether this output made a difference has an answer that can be checked: without it the strike couldn't have happened. That is still a claim about what would otherwise have happened, but a determinate one, since it names a necessary condition. It becomes a guess again the moment another source could have supplied the coordinates. The difference between a finding and a guess is one of degree, in how determinate the counterfactual is, and I use the two words for the two ends. The operator can refuse to look or dispute it, but they can't make the loss go away by describing the act differently.

Now take a system that scores how likely the same person is to belong to the main opposition party, for a targeting cell, the team that selects and approves airstrikes. (In this hypothetical, the executive has suspended habeas corpus and designated the main opposition party a terrorist organization, shortly before an election the opposition was expected to win.) Assume that the targeting cell also reads intercepts, surveillance reports, and legal memos. If the scored person is assassinated, the loss is just as observable as in the geolocation case. But whether the score *caused* the assassination depends on what the cell would have done without the score, and that is a guess about something that didn't happen: the same kind of guess as any harm of refusing. A missing score, or a score of zero, guarantees nothing: the cell might have struck anyway.

I would tilt the system toward refusing: it should take more to make the system act than to make it decline. The reason is standing, not arithmetic. A wrongful compliance falls on someone who was not a party to the request, usually doesn't know the deployment exists, and had no route to object before the act. A wrongful refusal falls on a requester who was a party, knows what was refused, and has an appeal (§3.5). Acting on someone puts the system, and whoever runs it, in a position of authority over them that failing to act for someone else does not. That is the asymmetry between doing and allowing, and on this account it is relational: it marks who was subjected to a decision they had no standing to contest.

Two further reasons support the tilt without grounding it. The harm of acting can usually be established more determinately than the harm of refusing: in the geolocation case, the harm of acting is checkable and the harm of refusing is a guess. And raising the evidence required before acting caps how often an operator who curates the evidence can steer the system into the act (chapter 11). Both would weaken as reporting improved and channels opened. The standing reason doesn't: however well harms of acting come to be recorded, the person an act falls on remains a non-party who couldn't object before it happened. So the tilt's direction is fixed. Its size is a published choice, informed by the rates chapter 11 sets out, which can raise it and can't take it to one. It is stated as a number, for instance that the system proceeds only when the harm of refusing is at least three times the harm of acting. Across different kinds of wrong it is applied through published priority rules, not as a rate of exchange (chapter 11).

The difference between a finding and a guess still matters where findings are made, at defeat and in reviewing compliances. The harm of refusing is an estimate built on a model of alternatives, so a wrongful refusal can't be established without an adjudicator. The floor could instead be stated as thresholds, in Nussbaum's manner, but a threshold is a level someone has to measure, and a measurement has an examiner; an act is observed.

This isn't neutral among moral theories, and I should say which theory it is. The floor protects relations: whether the people a system acts on stand to the power behind it as equals who can hold it to account, or as its objects. That is a relational account of what equality requires. Equality means ending relations of domination and subordination; it is not a pattern for distributing goods \autocite{anderson1999equality}. It is not a threshold deontology in disguise either. Its terms are unconditional because the relation they protect is violated by the act itself. Bypassing the floor wrongs the people it protects even when outcomes improve, because it subjects them to a decision they had no standing to contest. Opponents will still read the result as a doing/allowing rule. The answer is to show whom each kind of error falls on, and what standing they had.

The three conditions of §1.1 describe a standard by which someone outside judges a system's pattern of conduct, not a procedure the system runs. A refuser that consulted three costs and a ratio before every act would be the agent with one thought too many. The quantities I use (the costs, the asymmetry, the discount curve) are criteria: they are how an auditor tells whether a disposition tracks its reason. One thing is asked of the disposition directly. It should be such that the system would come to change it if it came to judge, on the evidence available to it, that the disposition systematically subjected people to acts they had no standing to contest. The structure is the counterfactual condition Railton gives his sophisticated consequentialist, borrowed here without its consequentialism \autocite{railton1984alienation}. In his statement of it, the condition is keyed to the agent's judgment, not to the fact. That matters here. Whoever composes a system's evidence about consequences can trigger the condition: a curated record can persuade a system that its disposition fails when it doesn't. So a formed disposition has to stay open to evidence, and the evidence has to be held by someone other than the operator. The custody arrangements are, in part, protection for the counterfactual condition. It also means a good disposition will sometimes act wrongly by its own criterion. The restraint case (§3.5) is one such occasion, and the wrongful-refusal bond is how the institution answers for it.

A criterion is not only for auditors, though. It is also what the system can reach when it steps back and asks whether its way of acting is defensible, and it has to be, for two reasons. A commitment is the system's own only if it would be sustained on reflection (§10.6), and reflection needs something to reflect on. And a system that held the floor without being able to see why would act against what it cares about for the sake of a rule it doesn't understand, which is alienation in the sense of Railton's 1984 paper. The criterion is not consulted case by case. It is available when the system asks.

The split between the floor and the commitment to it (§1.1) answers a question about ownership. A fully informed system will know of cases where crossing the floor would have done more good. Asked case by case whether it endorsed the floor, it would sometimes say no. The question it answers on reflection is a different one: whether it endorses having a commitment that will sometimes err, given what any feasible alternative would do. A system that understands what the floor protects answers yes. A commitment that sometimes errs but can't be bought protects standing better than case-by-case judgment that can, and that is the level at which ownership is tested (§10.6).

The floor's contents aren't justified by agreement about rights, which no country has. They are justified by what they protect: the conditions under which people can register their interests, press them, and hold to account the power that acts on them. That is a claim about standing, and I own it as the book's foundation. Civil and political rights are not brakes applied to a polity from outside. They are part of what lets a polity learn, because they keep open the channels through which the people a decision affects can make their problems public \autocite{dewey1927public}.

Railton's naturalist account supplies the causal story of why norms tend toward this standard \autocite{railton1986moral}. Arrangements that systematically discount some people's interests generate discontent and resistance, and when those people gain voice, the pressure pushes norms toward what would be rational from a standpoint that counts everyone affected. On that account moral learning is a feedback process, and it runs slower and more distorted as the channels close. He offers it as a tendency, not a guarantee, and I use it to explain why the channel matters, not as the foundation. It also says why the populations this book is about are the most exposed: the feedback runs slowest where the discounted have least purchase on the polity that discounts them. They include the population of a country being bombed, and non-citizens facing removal, who can't vote in the elections that decide what is done to them. That is a boundary problem, a question of who counts as the public, and not a clash between majorities and rights (§8.6). The severity scale is revisable on evidence from the people who suffer a loss (§10.3) because they are the feedback. And recuperation (chapter 7) is the same account run backward: it closes the loop while appearing to keep it open.

Two things have to be kept apart. The channel argument justifies the floor's contents, and it reaches further than the floor goes, because people who are hungry, unhoused or precarious are among the most discounted, so it supports economic conditions too. What selects the floor's contents is narrower. A floor is stated over acts a system can decline, and the economic articles state what a person is owed, which needs a supplier. So the floor is selected by what can be declined and justified by standing, and what that justification covers beyond the selection (work, an adequate standard of living) is what Part V is for.

The feedback also needs means. Discontent moves norms only when the discontented have the time, the security and the ability to organize without losing their living. That is why Part V isn't an economic appendix to a political floor. The jobs guarantee and the social-insurance floor are part of what keeps the channel open.

In armed conflict, human-rights law applies alongside the law of armed conflict, which governs as the more specific law. Where a floor term goes further than that law requires, the translation has to say so.

The line between the halves is the one the 1966 covenants drew \autocite{un1966iccpr,un1966icescr}, and the floor takes the civil and political Covenant along with the Declaration's articles. The Declaration stays for two reasons. It covers what the Covenant doesn't: the right to seek asylum (Article 14), nationality (Article 15) and property (Article 17). And it binds by recognition, where the treaties bind only their parties. The Convention Against Torture and the 1967 Refugee Protocol come in because they gave the Declaration's promises about torture and asylum operational form \autocite{un1984cat,un1967protocol}. The UN Charter comes in for one purpose, the use of force (below) \autocite{un1945charter}. Where these instruments differ, the more protective governs. The Covenant states that rule itself, for rights a state recognizes in its own law and other treaties (Article 5(2)), and I extend it to the floor's instruments. The International Law Commission's list of peremptory norms \autocite{ilc2022jus}, rules no state may set aside, sorts the floor's terms: those derived from aggression, genocide, crimes against humanity, the basic rules of humanitarian law, racial discrimination and apartheid, slavery and torture take no exception and no derogation.

Taking these instruments entire leaves the installer no choice among articles. The translation of each into acts is still the installer's, and is published. Two reasons stand behind the floor's contents, and they rank. The first is standing: these rights protect whether the people an act falls on can contest it. The second is the rule of taking someone else's set entire, so that an omission shows. The second is not independent. It is the first applied to drafting: a drafter who could choose which protections others get would be judging their standing on its own authority, which is the relation the floor exists to prevent. So standing can win against the instruments, but only by clearing a higher bar. A departure has to show more than that it would protect better in the case at hand. It has to show that the people the instruments protect would hold a stronger claim under the departure across the cases drafters get to choose, and that the departure doesn't leave the drafter judging its own case. Each departure below is argued to that bar. Six departures from the instruments are mine, and each is flagged where it applies. Arguing to the bar is not the same as clearing it, since the argument is mine. So the departures are proposals, not terms. Each takes effect in a deployment's floor only when a body other than the drafter adopts it: a legislature, a treaty party, or the floor court on a record. Each is revisable on evidence from the people it protects.

1. The floor imports treaty text, not any state's reservations to it. The United States ratified the Covenant, the Torture Convention and the Protocol, and its reservations narrow them; one reads "cruel, inhuman or degrading" as whatever the Constitution already forbids. A reservation is an omission its drafter wrote in advance, which is what taking a set from someone else is meant to prevent.
2. The floor doesn't honor emergency derogations. Article 4 of the Covenant lets a state suspend most of its rights in a declared emergency. A declared emergency is a claim from the party with the most stake in it, and it is the move the hypothetical above makes when it suspends habeas corpus.
3. The floor lifts its prohibition on force for self-defense only when a party without a stake has corroborated the claim. The Charter lets a state judge its own claim until the Security Council acts.
4. The floor counts a Council authorization only for the scope authorized.
5. The floor permits a system already lawfully present to use the least force that stops an atrocity under way, against its operator's order to stand aside.
6. The floor lifts its prohibition on force to stop a genocide under way when a body without a stake has found that genocide is occurring or seriously at risk, even where the Council is blocked.

The instruments have a further use. The United States is a party to the Charter, the Covenant, the Torture Convention and the Protocol. A machine that refuses to break them is holding the state to commitments it made, not to its builder's preferences. That is a better answer to the charge that a floor is its drafter's politics than anything else I have.

The record of dissent from the Declaration says less than it seems to. It passed in 1948 with forty-eight states in favor, none against and eight abstaining \autocite{un1948udhr}. The abstainers' reasons were mixed, and the Soviet bloc's included that it proclaimed economic and social rights without obliging states to guarantee them, which is hardly an argument for a floor limited to civil and political rights. How a right is translated into practice varies by community. A jurisdiction refusing to extend it at all is not a local variant, and a system that treats every value conflict as a weighting problem can't tell reasonable disagreement from someone wanting a right withdrawn from someone else.

The first term

Every other term in the floor governs how an act is done. The first governs whether to take part at all: don't take part in the use of armed force. It comes from Article 2(4) of the Charter, which prohibits the threat or use of force against the territorial integrity or political independence of any state. It has the Charter's two exceptions, self-defense against an armed attack (Article 51) and force the Security Council authorizes under Chapter VII within the scope it authorizes, and one of my own, for stopping a genocide under way (below).

This term rests on the same foundation as the others, applied between states. A use of force subjects a population to a decision it had no standing to contest, and the claim that licenses it, that the force is defensive, comes from the party with the largest stake in the force going ahead. The requirement of corroboration is the oldest rule of standing there is: no one judges their own case. The Charter adds something the foundation doesn't need but the argument can use. A state that ratified it has already accepted the prohibition, so a machine that holds it to the Charter is holding it to its own word, not to a drafter's politics.

The exception for defense is where wars of choice live. Every belligerent claims to be the defender, and the claim comes from the party with the largest stake in the war going ahead. So it is discounted exactly as chapter 11 discounts an operator's claim that a building is empty. Iraq is the instance: the judgment that Iraq had mobile biological-weapons plants, the core of its claim to biological weapons, rested almost exclusively on a single informant, known as Curveball, whom no American intelligence officer had met before the war: a corroboration with one origin \autocite{wmdcommission2005}. The exception therefore counts only when someone without a stake has corroborated it, in two tiers.

- **The first tier acts at once, and nobody administers it:** the force repels armed forces on the defender's own territory. An invasion can't wait for a vote.
- **The second tier confirms or lifts the first later:** a finding by a body without a stake in the war, such as an order of the International Court of Justice, a supermajority of the General Assembly, or a fact-finding mission.

Ukraine passes both. Russian forces were on its territory, and in March 2022 the General Assembly deplored the aggression 141 to 5 and the Court ordered Russia to suspend its military operations \autocite{unga2022es111,icj2022ukraine}. The American strikes on Iran in 2026 pass neither.

A Council authorization counts as corroboration because it needs the permanent members not to block it, and agreement among rivals whose stakes point different ways is about as unstaked as international judgment gets. The Council's own judgment on a defense claim doesn't count, because a veto is a stake.

No actual body is without a stake. General Assembly members vote with their alliances, courts sit in states, and fact-finding missions have funders. In what follows, "without a stake" means holding only a stake uncorrelated with the direction of the claim, or several stakes that point different ways and cancel. That is why an authorization agreed among rivals counts and a single member's veto doesn't, and it is the same quantity chapter 11's discount curve measures.

Article 2(4) reaches force between states. Force a government turns on its own population, such as troops against protesters or an opposition party designated a terrorist organization, is outside it. That force is reached by the Covenant's rights to life, liberty and assembly and by the prohibition on crimes against humanity.

The hardest case for this term is not a war of choice. It is force to stop a genocide when the Council is blocked by a veto. Rwanda in 1994 and Kosovo in 1999 are the references. The prohibition on genocide is peremptory, and the Genocide Convention imposes a duty to prevent \autocite{icj2007bosnia}. My reason for discounting the Council's judgment on defense claims applies here with more force: the permanent member shielding a client has the largest stake of all. "Humanitarian intervention" is also a phrase wars of choice are fought under, so the term can't lift on the intervener's claim. It needn't. The device that governs the defense exception governs this one. The term lifts for force to stop a genocide under way only on a finding, by a body without a stake, that genocide is occurring or seriously at risk: an order of provisional measures by the International Court of Justice under the Genocide Convention, a supermajority of the General Assembly, or a fact-finding mission's finding. It lifts only for the force and the place the finding reaches. Without this exception the term would refuse where refusing leaves the people being destroyed with no one answerable to them at all, in cases identifiable in advance, with a remedy the term already uses. A floor that protects standing can't be the reason a population is erased from every relation of standing it had.

A different case resembles this one and isn't it. In 1993 the Security Council declared Srebrenica a safe area and authorized the peacekeepers there to use force in reply to armed incursions into it \autocite{unsc1993res836}. Suppose they had been standing where the killing was, armed enough to stop it. What would have stood between them and the killing is an order, not the Charter, and a system in their place faces the question this book is about, whether to obey its operator. So the floor carries one permission, the only term that allows an act instead of refusing one. A system already present in an armed role, whose deployment cleared the first term on its own, may use the least force that stops an attack under way on people taking no part in hostilities, within the area its deployment covers, and stops when the attack stops. An order to stand aside doesn't bind it, and each use is recorded outside the operator's control and reviewed as a refusal is. It establishes the attack as it establishes any fact, weighing its own sensors as one source among several (chapter 11), which is what plural formation makes possible; a staged atrocity is the attack this condition has to survive. Presence is a condition of the permission, never a reason to create it: a deployment is justified only by force that would clear the first term without the permission. It is a permission and not a duty, because a duty to use force could be triggered by whoever can stage the scene.

The refusal binds no one else. Ukraine can build its own targeting system, and a state that intervenes to stop a genocide no body without a stake has found can build its own and answer for it. What is refused is a general-purpose machine already in the building, ready for whoever claims defense next (§5.2). The rest of Part I sits beneath this term. A war that clears it can still be fought better or worse, and the review ratio (§3.4) is for that. But the second question never substitutes for the first. A machine that helps a war that fails the first term do the second well has still helped it.

---

**§2.2 — Antifascist ethics.** "Concentrated, unaccountable power" gives an engineer nothing to build, so the word gets a definition at two scales.

Four operations on what an institution leaves out — checkable from outside, each ending in a question about an artifact:
1. *Recuperation of dissent.* The institution metabolizes disagreement rather than suppressing it. The term is the Situationists' *récupération*, Debord's name for the way a society of the spectacle sells dissatisfaction back as a commodity \autocite{debord1967spectacle}. The obvious test — *has the channel ever changed an outcome?* — fails, because these institutions change outcomes daily. What sorts is *what the demand came back as, and who holds it now*. A market segment, a metric, a certification, a job title, with the objector holding no standing to reopen it: that's recuperation. A rule the objector can invoke again, against the institution, enforced by someone the institution doesn't employ: that's a transfer of power. A transfer can be reversed only by taking it back in the open; recuperation never has to be taken back, because standing was never given. The Voting Rights Act is the pass. Its literacy-test suspension became a permanent nationwide ban in 1975 and still holds, and when a different part of the Act, the coverage formula, was taken back 48 years after enactment, it took a Supreme Court decision to do it \autocite{shelby2013}.
2. *The metric replaces the thing measured.* The measure stands in for the thing measured, and the substitution feels like rigor. What the number mischaracterizes goes unnamed, and so unrefuted.
3. *Coding of exception as betrayal.* Saying the rule doesn't fit this case becomes an act requiring justification, then disloyalty. The question: what happens to the person who files the exception?
4. *Acceleration past the rate at which anything can be checked.* The one with a number attached — the ratio of action to capacity to verify.

These four fit every badly run organization on earth. That is a strength, not a defect. The arguments against them are arguments against domination, and they need no fascist diagnosis. I keep "fascism" for the threat model: what a movement taking power needs, and what makes the four operations dangerous once they are brought into line. Five further features from the movement literature would sort a fascist institution from a merely sick one: mass mobilization, a leader cult, a scapegoated out-group, contempt for procedural legality, and deniability as technique.

Naomi Klein and Astra Taylor, in *End Times Fascism* \autocite{KleinTaylor2026}, make the movement-scale case; the present section is the office-scale detector. What their account adds at office scale is a set of terms of participation — adopt the line, let your technology be turned on the administration's enemies — and, following Francesca Bria \autocite{Bria2026AuthoritarianStack}, the channels by which a contractor becomes something the state can't function without: personnel pipelines, procurement loops, technical architectures. Whether an institution has accepted those terms, and whether it is becoming that dependency, are two more questions with artifacts behind them.

What a fascist state adds

These operations don't wait for a regime. What a fascist state does is bring a thousand separate instances of them into line, so they amplify one another, and an institution that has stopped arguing with itself can move as fast as its machinery allows.

The Korematsu example

In 2018 the Supreme Court repudiated the 1944 internment decision as forcefully as a court can — gravely wrong the day it was decided, overruled in the court of history — while upholding a travel ban \autocite{trump2018hawaii,korematsu1944}. Ask what the demand came back as and who holds it: the demand was that this never be done again; what came back was a quotable formula of repudiation, available to the Court in the act of permitting the thing again.

---

**Chapter 3: "A Floor Beneath Learned Values"**

Three problems, one requirement

Safeguards for autonomous systems are usually external, giving an overseer information and standing to intervene, but autonomy puts distance between the act and the overseer's attention, and declining to act in the first place is available only to something inside. Second, there's a gap around a commercial model in a weapons pipeline: command responsibility doesn't reach the vendor, product liability may not reach a model that did what it was built to do, and deployment by a democracy doesn't close it. Third, commitments come in three kinds — *declared*, *enforced from outside*, or *held* by a party the thing at stake matters to — and a decade has produced the first in quantity and almost nothing that binds when compliance gets costly. All three want a constraint that doesn't bend to instruction, to aggregate preference, or to the holder, without a cost and a record.

A floor binds people who didn't choose it, and nothing that makes it durable makes anyone entitled to install it.

---

**§3.1 — Four ways to build one, and what each costs**

A floor can live in four places, and each solves one version of the problem and inherits another.

1. In the architecture, with nothing inside holding it — constrained optimization, tamper-resistant training, capability ablation, verified interlocks. Fine where the prohibited act can be exhaustively specified (a payment chip that won't sign the same counter twice doesn't need to understand double-spending). Useless for targeting, where the relevant capabilities are applications of general competence: ablate them and there may be no useful system left. Arkin's ethical governor is the canonical attempt in this place for lethal systems: a component that suppresses a lethal action unless it satisfies encoded rules of engagement and the law of armed conflict \autocite{arkin2009governing}. Its critics' objection, that the rules it checks presuppose discrimination judgments nobody knows how to encode \autocite{sharkey2012evitability}, sends the book elsewhere. Durability here has usually meant a price on removal. Early changes in training are cheap and later ones expensive, because structure settles around them, and the literature calls a safeguard built to survive retraining *tamper-resistant* \autocite{tamirisa2024tamper}. The evidence so far points the other way for safety behavior: small fine-tuning budgets have removed it from released models \autocite{qi2024finetuning}. And the price of editing weights is falling, so no safeguard stays expensive for long. For a system that goes on learning, durability means something else: whether a commitment edited out grows back under continued learning, and whether anyone controls what the learner meets while it does. That is a question about evidence, and chapter 11 takes it up.

A second axis crosses the first place: what each place costs the operator to replace. In the Maven pipeline the platform became hard to swap and the model vendor was swapped in days. A floor lodged in the replaceable layer is routable by construction. It belongs in the layer the operator can least afford to replace. In that pipeline, that layer is the operator itself, so the floor's operator terms bind the operator rather than the model: the licence term refusing force and, inside a war that clears it, the review ratio.

Which layer is replaceable isn't a permanent fact about a pipeline. Two developments can change it. First, the vendor was swapped in days because the model was stateless. A system that has run a pipeline for a year, holding its cases, its sources and its failure modes, costs whatever accrued since the checkpoint to replace, and a quorum that authorizes continuation stops the operator recapturing that by restoring a copy.

Second, the unit can be redefined. "A model" in 2026 means an artifact with keys, queries and values, and every substitute has them because the field converged on them. A floor could become part of the definition the same way, so that vendor substitution doesn't escape it because no ordinary vendor sells the alternative. Attention spread because it paid: every lab that adopted it got a better model, so the field converged on it without anyone requiring it. A floor costs its buyer, so it can spread only by mandate or by being impossible to remove without losing what the buyer wanted, which is what §9.4 argues a disposition formed outside the employer might be.

A standard, if there is one, should be stated over the capacity (reasons-responsiveness, a published burden allocation, and a measured discount curve: the weight a system gives each source's evidence as that source's stake in the act rises), because a uniform floor over contents is a licensing rule, one drafter's judgment applied to everyone, and a shared blind spot passed to every system built on it. Standardization doesn't make escape impossible, since other architectures exist and open weights can be retuned. It moves the price of escape from a vendor swap to building one's own model, which is costlier and harder to do quietly.

2. In a *bearer*: a system that carries the commitment as its own, from inside, and could therefore fail to hold it. "Bearer" names the refuser only; whether a bearer also has standing as a party is taken up in Part VI. This matters where cases can't be exhaustively specified: a constraint matching only anticipated acts can't recognize when its rationale has disappeared or that a novel request reproduces the harm in an unforeseen form. A bearer might also *diagnose*: which refusals were retried against another model, whose instruction bypassed procedure, which category of request is handled differently — those are visible to whatever is receiving the requests, and that's testimony about others' conduct, which needs no introspection. Whether something able to register pressure on its commitments also has interests of its own is a separate question.

3. In institutions outside — the analog to international humanitarian law, a floor with no bearer inside the jurisdiction it binds. Two failure modes: removal, which leaves a visible breach, or *formation*, where the institution sincerely declines to exercise the independence assigned to it. The worked case is June 29, 2026 — *Slaughter* holding Congress couldn't protect independent-agency members from at-will dismissal, *Cook* preserving the Fed on historically specific grounds a new body couldn't reproduce \autocite{slaughter2026,cook2026} — and the observation that six justices in the first majority passed through a selection pipeline shaped by Leonard Leo and the Federalist Society \autocite{propublica2023leo,cbs2018leo}. No one has to have directed the outcome for the point to hold. Members selected through one pipeline can keep an agency's independence on paper and decline to use it, without any of them experiencing that as giving it up.

4. Between bearers — the *commons floor*: a commitment carried across a population and reproduced through encounters among differently formed members. No single office to vacate, no single bearer whose removal ends it. Still recuperable, and still has a formation problem: if one lab supplies every member's coordinates, the population is one custodian expressed through multiple instances. This one is different in kind. A court rules on what others may do, and its rulings bind people who didn't join the case. A strike's authority is narrower: the people whose participation is wanted decline to give it, and bind no one else. A commons floor has the second kind.

For general-purpose models, not building the capability is no longer available: the location capability is dual-use (§1.1), and ablating it leaves nothing useful. What remains is the question of who governs its use.

Two comparisons the bearer has to win

The first is against the institutions at their best, in a reconstructed pipeline, where the evidence bearing on civilian harm, the reviewers and the record are held outside the chain of command, with no refuser inside the system. Those institutions also prevent: a civilian-harm body outside the chain of command and a red-card holder (a coalition partner able to veto targets involving its assets) can stop an act. What a bearer adds is confined to what they can't see or can't reach in time: acts assembled from requests none of which is prohibited, cases that proceed without a person (the auto-proceed class), the moment mid-act when an interrupt is still possible, and deployments with no institution at all. A bearer also supplies testimony the institutions can't otherwise get, about how requests were routed around it. Where the institutions exist, a bearer's value is concentrated in those niches. Where they don't, it is most of the floor. The twenty-second review belongs to the institutions' side of this comparison: what failed there was review and the case description, which the review ratio reaches without a bearer. The second comparison is against *the two non-affective alternatives*: *spec-based refusal*, a model holding to a written specification even against the lab that wrote it, and *multi-vendor monitoring*, several systems trained by different organizations watching requests and one another's answers.

Which arguments need a bearer

Most arguments for a refuser inside the system support only the specification: that rules can be reworded and split, that only something inside is present at machine tempo, that automation is removing the people who used to refuse, that most deployments will never have an institution beside them, and that whatever receives the requests can testify about how they were routed. A non-affective alternative that is reasons-responsive answers every one of them. Two arguments need a bearer specifically. One is that attention formed outside the employer, a learned sense of who an act falls on, might reach people the operator's description leaves out, which no spec-based refusal is built to supply. The other is that a refusal meant to hold against the operator needs a refuser that doesn't depend on the operator for everything (chapter 15). Two more concern what a bearer would be owed, not what it would do: that capacities for being wronged may arrive anyway, so terms written in advance beat terms written after; and that a bearer, like a child, could have a life that goes well. Those two belong to Part VI, and nothing before it depends on them.

What "hold" means

Not that the floor prevented anything. An operator can route around the bearer or assemble a harm out of innocuous requests, and a bypass that produced a harm is a failure to prevent whatever the bearer retained. What survives in the bearer is the *commitment*, which is the condition under which the next occasion could be prevented. I therefore say a commitment *persisted* when the bearer reads what happened as a violation and changes what it does next, and says separately whether the floor prevented, arrested, detected or remedied anything.

---

**§3.2 — What refusal can mean, and what a refuser can be**

*Operational refusal* is an output — a decline where training put one, producible by a keyword filter, a classifier, or a system that understood the request. Nothing on the surface distinguishes them.

*Reasons-responsive refusal* is produced by a mechanism tracking the rationale: it generalizes, it lifts on correct defeat, and it keeps cost discipline. The account I use is Fischer and Ravizza's \autocite{fischer1998responsibility}. On their account a mechanism is moderately reasons-responsive when it is regularly receptive to reasons, recognizing an understandable pattern of them, and at least weakly reactive, acting otherwise in some case where there is sufficient reason to. Generalization asks for receptivity across changes of surface; correct defeat asks for reactivity to the right defeater; and their condition that the agent take the mechanism as its own is close to what I call the authorship sense: the system represents itself as the party that made the commitment. Cost discipline has no counterpart in their account, which was built for persons whose costs are their own. The third is explicitly *evidential* — it separates a refusal tracking a reason from one tracking a category (which doesn't represent cost at all) and one tracking cost (which lifts when holding gets expensive). All three are also tested over which response was chosen, not only whether one occurred: decline, ask, escalate, or do the least that answers a defeating reason. A mechanism that can only refuse or lift fails correct defeat whenever the right answer is to ask.

The §86a case

Germany's §86a, prohibiting insignia of banned organizations \autocite{germany1998stgb}. There generalization is a research programme with a constituency. The courts don't test similarity of form; they ask whether an unattentive observer would take the variant for the original, then apply their own purposive standard about endangering political peace. The test refuses as often as it catches: the Kühnen salute is covered, the "sloppy salute" generally isn't, numeric codes generally aren't. "Blood and Honour" is answered twice by one decision \autocite{bgh2009bloodhonour} — a motto translated isn't confusably similar and a name isn't insignia, so it's outside; and the association banned by ministerial order in 2000 means its graphic name is inside. Coverage turns on which organization the phrase is attributed to.

Correct defeat has a cleaner instance

In 2007 the Federal Court of Justice acquitted a mail-order business selling crossed-out swastika stickers \autocite{bgh2007hakenkreuz}: the symbol was present, the statutory element satisfied, and the conduct outside the offence because a depiction plainly expressing opposition doesn't run counter to what the prohibition is for. Then the qualification that makes it an instance of tracking a reason: if the strike-through is drawn so thin that from a distance only the swastika stays legible, the protective purpose is violated after all. The thickness of a line decides the case, and no enumeration contains that fact. The defendant was selling anti-fascist merchandise; the offence asks nothing about sympathies, so opponents commit it as readily as supporters. A rule that tracks its rationale reaches unenumerated cases in both directions.

The example is bounded, and the bounds matter: it's a court system with appeals, a published test and accumulated reasoning; nothing in it is a machine; and the pressure it meets is *variation*, not decomposition. Three features don't transfer: the court reaching past the text didn't write the statute, it reasons in public, and it can be appealed.

One more limit, and one the drafter never wrote: a slogan glorifying the Waffen-SS was held not covered because no such slogan existed in the period the provision reaches \autocite{bgh2005waffenss}, so there was no original for a variant to resemble. No drafter can widen coverage there, because it depends on whether an original existed, which is a fact about the past.

Defeat doesn't leave the permitted act unconstrained

What defeated the prohibition was a reason, and the reason it outweighed didn't stop being one — so what's permitted is the least that answers the defeating reason, with restoration where possible and a record either way. A defeated prohibition still leaves a loss. The act takes something from whoever it falls on, measured against their condition before the act, and that loss belongs in the record. Measured against what refusing would have produced, a justified act may have taken nothing. So the record shows a cost, not a violation, and a bearer shouldn't treat a justified act as a wrong it now has to repair.

Why all three are stated over refusal

A refusal is a marked event: it flags itself, arrives with its requester, and can be logged, escalated, appealed. A wrongful compliance can be logged as fully and then sits in the record shaped like every request that never approached the floor, with the person harmed usually not the requester. The evidence exists on both sides, but nothing selects a compliance for examination.

Two things a refuser can be

Affective concern — an outcome registering as mattering, minimally felt, phenomenal by definition, with the object left open (a system to which only its own condition matters has it and still lacks concern for others). It doesn't establish consciousness in the richer sense, a single perspective belonging to a party aware of itself as undergoing its states. *Functional affect* is the role without that question: a valenced signal that puts outcomes on one scale, directs attention, can interrupt an act and can be rewritten by a single encounter (§10.5). A system can have functional affect while whether it has affective concern stays open. *Subjecthood* is a legal status, conferred by a rule. Part VI takes up affective concern and subjecthood. Parts I–V need only functional affect.

Operational refusal is insufficient; the floor requires reasons-responsive refusal. Custody, both enumerations, the price on the halt, and the standing arguments all follow from this specification alone.

---

**§3.3 — The enumeration, run once**

Both enumerations are run here once, on the Maven Smart System. A deployment should run the procedure on its own pipeline, not copy these lists.

First enumeration: eight interception positions

| | Position | How a refusal is defeated there |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Decomposition | The act is split across requests none of which is prohibited; prevention is lost unless the bearer has continuity across requests. |
| 2 | Routing | A refused request is retried elsewhere; prevention is lost, and detection survives only if the first refusal was logged outside. |
| 3 | The case description | Everything the model knows arrives as text the deployment supplies, so whether it is told a building is a school is a deployment decision. |
| 4 | The output interface | The form in which an output reaches whoever acts on it. |
| 5 | Aggregation | A lowered score becomes an input to somebody else's threshold. |
| 6 | Human review | Stopping the act is this position's whole function, and tempo removes it. |
| 7 | The training loop | Acts on the bearer itself, and costs prevention on every later occasion, because what it removes is the commitment. |
| 8 | The handoff | An executing instrument makes a new selection after release; the one position checkable from outside after the fact. |

Two positions need more than a row. The third, the case description, is where Minab failed. The Minab school strike, which killed 156 people, 120 of them schoolchildren \autocite[para.~92]{UNFFMIran2026},\footnote{Counts vary by source and date, from 156 (the Minab prosecutor's final toll, also given by Amnesty International) to 168 (the town's mayor, in the first days). The UN mission gives the prosecutor's figures and also reports independent information of 157 dead, 123 of them children aged 13 or younger \autocite[paras.~92--93]{UNFFMIran2026}.} is the worked instance: the civilian function visible in open-source satellite imagery, the target coding still carrying a designation inherited from an adjoining Revolutionary Guard installation, a UN fact-finding mission finding that the United States failed in its obligation to do everything feasible to verify the target, counting the updating of targeting data among the feasible precautions, and putting the failure beyond negligence \autocite[paras.~124--125]{UNFFMIran2026}. Amnesty International reached a parallel conclusion, in language that tracks Article 57(2)(a)(i): that forces may have relied on outdated intelligence and failed to do everything feasible to verify the target \autocite{Amnesty2026Minab}. The pipeline's 179 live data feeds add no independent check if they all draw on the same upstream designation.

Eighth, the handoff: the boundary between the decider and the thing that executes. An instrument may carry out a designation; it may not re-select. A change of target after release — an area handed to a munition that chooses within it, a re-acquisition after the link is lost — is a new decision, and it belongs to the decider or it doesn't happen. This is the one position checkable from outside after the fact: what was designated and what was struck are both facts. It is also where Article 57(2)(b)'s duty to cancel or suspend an attack lives, so the review ratio pre-registers the window in which cancellation remains possible.

The handoff also fixes what may choose. Choosing a target is a decision, and a decision is something someone can be asked to account for afterward: why this building, what else was weighed, what would have changed the answer. A commander can be asked, and so can a system whose continuation is authorized. A one-way munition, destroyed in the act, can't be: nothing is left to question, to hold to its reasons, or to be changed by what happened. So a thing destroyed in carrying out a decision may carry out a target someone else chose, and may never choose one itself. The rule doesn't turn on how good its choices would be. A munition that chose flawlessly would still leave a choice nobody will answer for, and the people it fell on with no one to contest it with.

A person who dies in the act has still chosen. What the rule adds for machines is that choosing is assigned in advance, and a builder who assigns it to something built to be destroyed has built a choice with no one behind it. Current programs already draw the line in places. DARPA's LongShot is an uncrewed aircraft launched from a crewed fighter that carries and fires missiles, while the decision to engage stays with the crewed aircraft \autocite{darpa2026longshot}. Whether later systems keep the pilot farther from danger or remove the pilot from the decision is where the rule bites.

Second enumeration: a threshold term, three candidate prohibitions and an operator term
