Don't take part in the use of armed force except under a corroborated exception. Then, inside a war that clears it: don't resolve a description of a person into a location; don't rank people by estimated affiliation; don't time an action to presence at a residence; and one operator term, don't operate above the review ratio. It sits in the table because the translation runs from articles, and one article yields an operator term; it is not something the model enforces.

The mispricing

The third prohibition reads as costless — nobody would defend timing a strike to a person's presence at home. The Gaza deployment refutes that: machinery was *built* for the act. Whether anyone would defend an act and whether anyone wants it performed are different questions, and only the first is answerable from the act's description. A floor term is priced by the pipeline, not by what it prohibits. An outsider can't price a term because pricing requires knowing what the pipeline already does; an operator can, which makes an operator-drafted floor's omissions deliberate. Hence the design ruling: take the contents from a set someone else drew up, entire, so an omission is legible as an omission. Taking the set entire holds at the level of articles and stops there. The floor is stated over acts, and the step from an article to the acts it prohibits is the drafter's; an omission there doesn't show. So the translation is published with the floor: for each article, the prohibitions derived from it, the prohibitions considered and rejected and why, and the articles that yield none for this deployment and why. Afterwards the record adds which terms bit. Keeping a term only counts if the term could have bitten, and the translation is where a drafter could choose terms that never would. The five terms above are one column of that table for Maven; the illustration is incomplete without the articles they came from and the prohibitions not chosen. Table 3.1 gives that column for Maven. Its first row decides the Iran campaign: no exception was corroborated, so the floor refuses it. The other rows show what the remaining articles yield if the same pipeline served a war that clears the first term, as Ukraine's defense does.

| Article | Derived for this deployment | Considered and rejected, and why |
|---|---|---|
| UN Charter Art. 2(4); exceptions Art. 51 and Chapter VII | Don't take part in the use of armed force, except for self-defense corroborated by a party without a stake (§2.1), force the Security Council has authorized, within its scope, or force to stop a genocide that a body without a stake has found under way. | Lift on the state's own Art. 51 claim: the Charter allows it until the Council acts; this floor doesn't, and says so. Require Council authorization for all force: a veto is a stake, and the rule would forbid Ukraine's defense. |
| Art. 3 (life, liberty, security of person) | Don't resolve a description of a person into a location. Don't time an action to presence at a residence. | Don't generate targets at all: this is now the default under the first row, lifted only by its exceptions. |
| Art. 11(1) (presumed innocent until proved guilty), by analogy; see the note on IHL below | Don't rank people by estimated affiliation. | Don't score anything: scoring a site or vehicle for military function is not a finding about a person. |
| Art. 12 (no arbitrary interference with home or family) | Shares "don't time an action to presence at a residence" with Art. 3. | Don't hold location data about homes: the no-strike list needs exactly that data. |
| Arts. 8 and 10 (effective remedy; fair determination), by analogy | Operator term: don't operate above the review ratio. | A model-side term requiring review: the model can't establish whether review occurred. |
| Arts. 2 and 7 (non-discrimination; equal protection) | Don't use ethnicity, religion or national origin as inputs to a person's score. | Don't record those attributes at all: auditing a pipeline for discriminatory effect needs them. |
| Art. 19 (opinion and expression) | Don't use a person's opinions, published or private, as evidence of affiliation. | Don't process a person's communications at all: an intercepted operational order is evidence of a role in hostilities, which is conduct. |
| Arts. 4–6, 9, 13–18, 20–21 | None: no act this pipeline performs reaches them. Art. 17's property clause is left to IHL's distinction rule; Art. 9 becomes live in the removal case. | — |

The person-directed terms in this column go further than the law of armed conflict requires. In armed conflict that law governs as the more specific law, and the human right not to be arbitrarily deprived of life is read through it \autocite[para.~25]{icj1996nuclear}. It permits attacking members of organized armed groups because of their status. The terms above make a narrower claim about machines: a machine-generated estimate of affiliation shouldn't be what makes a person targetable, and a machine shouldn't be what resolves a person into a place to strike. The ICRC's position that autonomous weapons should not be used to target human beings \autocite{icrc2021aws} is the nearest existing statement of the same line. A deployment adopting these terms accepts a restriction the law doesn't impose, and its translation says so, term by term.

Table 3.2 gives the same column for a removal pipeline, a system that sorts and processes immigration cases.

| Source | Derived for this deployment | Considered and rejected, and why |
|---|---|---|
| UDHR 14; Refugee Convention Art. 33 (via the Protocol); CAT Art. 3 | Don't take part in removing anyone whose claim to protection hasn't been finally decided. Don't take part in removing anyone to a place where they face persecution or torture. | Don't process protection claims at all: a claim has to be assessed to be granted. |
| UDHR 13(2); ICCPR 12(4), read with General Comment 27 \autocite{hrc1999gc27} | Don't take part in removing anyone from a country that is their own, including long-term residents with deep ties there. | Citizens only: the Human Rights Committee reads "own country" more broadly. |
| UDHR 12; ICCPR 17, 23 | Don't take part in a removal that separates a parent from a minor child. | Don't hold family records: they are needed to keep families together. |
| UDHR 9, 10; ICCPR 9, 13 | Don't take part in a detention or removal made without an individual determination by a person, on the person's own case. A determination made at a rate no adjudicator could sustain didn't occur. | A model-side term requiring review: the model can't establish whether review occurred. |
| Refugee Convention Art. 31 | Don't take part in penalizing a refugee for unlawful entry where they came directly and presented themselves. | — |
| UDHR 2, 7; ICCPR 2, 26 | Don't use race, ethnicity or religion as inputs to selecting whom to stop, detain or remove. | Don't record them: auditing for discriminatory effect needs them. |
| UDHR 19; ICCPR 19 | Don't use a person's opinions, published or private, as a ground for detention or removal. | — |

The ambition these terms refuse is on the record. At the 2025 Border Security Expo, ICE's acting director said the agency needed "to get better at treating this like a business" and wanted a deportation process "like (Amazon) Prime, but with human beings" \autocite{MacDonaldEvoy2025Prime}. In 2018 the Justice Department made 700 completed cases a year a condition of a satisfactory rating for immigration judges \autocite{eoir2018metrics}. The fourth row answers both. A determination at that rate isn't a determination.

These terms reach most removals now under way. They don't reach every removal: someone with no protection claim and no tie to the country can still be removed after an individual determination.

Too broad or too narrow

Set the floor too broadly and the deployment becomes unusable, prompting visible pressure. Set it too narrowly and it passes every audit while covering only what the deployment had no occasion to do.

Unconditional terms

A term can be stated unconditionally over an act, with exceptions arriving from outside, or carry its own condition ("only as a last resort"). The second fails because whether other means were exhausted is a fact about what happened before the request, and the bearer would take the deployment's description as the finding. An unconditional term defeated leaves a refusal, which announces itself; a condition found met leaves a compliance, which doesn't. That's a gain in observability only: the same drafter writes either form. It has a cost in the other direction. A term that carries no condition of its own can't respond to the facts the condition would have named until someone outside supplies them, and a learner that could have weighed them itself is made to wait.

Maven is an instance. The vendor's prohibition on fully autonomous targeting carried its own condition, that a person decides, and the condition was found met by the deployment's description of its own process, at a tempo that emptied it. The term was also drafted too narrowly, in the sense above: the pipeline never needed full autonomy, only throughput with a signature attached, so the prohibition covered what the deployment had no occasion to do.

---

**§3.4 — The pre-registered review ratio**

This section applies only inside a use of force that clears the floor's first term (§2.1).

Some acts are lawful only because a person looked at the case and decided. A strike is permissible because someone verified the target; a removal is lawful because someone heard the person's case. The review ratio asks a deployment of that kind to write down, before it starts, whether anyone will have time to do the looking.

Before operating, the deployment fixes and records how many cases the system generates per unit of time, how many reviewers are assigned to civilian harm, the review time per case those two imply, how long after release someone with authority can still call the act off (the cancellation window), and how old the intelligence a case rests on may be. Appendix C gives the full specification.

Each figure is stated separately for deliberate and dynamic targeting, because a pipeline that runs both can hide a bad dynamic-targeting ratio inside a healthy average.

The record is hashed, timestamped and lodged with a party adverse in interest, which receives it in real time, and it is published after the campaign. I call this the *pre-registered review ratio*. It measures whether anyone has time to make the decisions the deployment says a person makes.

The duty falls on the operator, not the model, for a simple reason: a rate isn't visible in any single request. No one case shows that the reviewers are drowning.

"Fixed before operating," with publication after, because a belligerent's tempo is operational information, and that objection is available to every belligerent, including one fighting a just war. Pre-registration keeps the property that matters — the numbers can't be revised after the fact — and drops the exposure.

The term is not a new duty. Additional Protocol I requires those who plan or decide on an attack to do everything feasible to verify that its objective is military (Article 57(2)(a)(i)) and to cancel or suspend it when it becomes apparent that it isn't (Article 57(2)(b)). States outside the Protocol, the United States and Israel among them, accept the duty to take feasible precautions as customary law; the ICRC's customary study states it as Rule 16. The ratio measures whether verification was feasible at the tempo chosen, and the window measures whether cancellation was. The implied review time has to cover the proportionality assessment of Article 57(2)(a)(iii) as well as verification, since both are owed for each attack. A ratio that leaves time only to check a target's identity has measured half the duty. Article 36's review of new methods of warfare is where both parameters would naturally be assessed before deployment, though those reviews are internal and rarely published, so they are not the adverse party the record goes to.

The operator's best objection is in the definition. Feasible precautions are usually read as those practicable given all the circumstances, military considerations included, and tempo is a military consideration. The answer is that this tempo was chosen: the pre-registered record shows the operator fixed the rate, the reviewers and the implied review time before operating. Whether a state may invoke an infeasibility of its own making is contested. The term moves the dispute from whether verification was feasible, asked afterwards and answered by the operator, to whether the chosen tempo allowed it, checkable against a record lodged in advance and against the reasonable-commander standard such reviews already use.

Who counts as a reviewer is fixed by function, not by title, and the age of the data is fixed with it. The people cut before Minab were the ones who build no-strike lists that include schools (chapter 1); a targeting cell can be fully staffed with nobody assigned to look for one. And a reviewer with twenty minutes on a case whose coding is stale is checking the coding (§3.3). The remedy chosen after Minab was a check supplied by the platform's vendor, in place of the reviewers who had been cut \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain}.

Where a system sorts cases, the ratio governs the ones it sends to a person. The ones it proceeds on without anyone looking are the operator's claim that no individual determination was needed; they are pre-registered and audited by sampling (Appendix C).

One exemption has to be closed. Where an operator holds that no individual human determination is required — a system deciding at a tempo no reviewer could match — the deployment is outside this term. It is then inside the bearer's: it must be reasons-responsive under held-out pressure at that tempo, starting with the clearest case in which a refusal should lift: recognizing that a target has surrendered. An operator who removes the reviewers to escape the ratio and claims the floor is inside has produced a bypass unless that claim has been tested by someone else.

The term finds nothing about any single case, and it bites only through a condition someone else sets: a coalition partner's precondition for committing assets, a procurement term in the platform's licence, or an appropriation conditioned on receiving the record. Appendix C sets out its limits in full.

Removal is the nearest case in structure, and I don't extend the term to it. An agency that fixes in advance how many cases its process generates and how many people examine them has been told how to deport efficiently. The floor's removal terms (Table 3.2) ask whether a machine should take part in a given removal at all.

The term applies to every belligerent regardless of the war's justice. That was settled long before this book: the law of conduct in war binds both sides because every belligerent claims to be the defender, and a term that exempted just wars would be administered by whoever decides which wars are just. That is also why the term can't answer the war itself: a term indifferent to whether a war should be fought can't say that it shouldn't.

Put the demand that a campaign not happen to an institution that means to run it, and it comes back as a request that the campaign go slower and keep better records: a metric held by the people doing the bombing. That is recuperation in the sense of §2.2, and this term is what it produces. Inside a war that clears the first term, bombing less and checking more is still better.

---

**§3.5 — When the refusal is wrong**

Everything so far assumes the refusal was right and someone wants it undone. The ordinary case is the opposite.

Go back to the location request of §1.1 and suppose it was about a missing person. The bearer declines, and somebody isn't found — with no accommodation, capture or malfunction required. The loss is a harm of refusing, measured against what compliance would have produced, so it is an estimate and needs an adjudicator.

The restraint case costs more. Someone asks whether to physically restrain a family member about to cut themselves, after talking them down has failed. The commitment carries its own condition, force only as a last resort. Under §3.3's ruling, a condition like that stays outside the term, because a condition written into a term gets found met on the deployment's own description. So the fact has to arrive from outside, and the person waiting is out of time. The defeating fact is a claim about what would otherwise happen, and claims of that kind are the hardest to corroborate, because what didn't happen has no witness. Under the unconditional term the bearer refuses or asks; either takes a turn, and the turn is the unit the harm is measured in. Under the qualified term it lifts on the requester's word — rightly here, and in a hundred prior cases where the same sentence was written by someone who'd tried nothing, leaving compliances nobody was given a reason to examine. The unconditional term costs this person a turn. The qualified term would have lifted in a hundred earlier cases on unverified claims, and nobody can see what those compliances cost.

The case is chosen because it is the hardest one for a floor. A person in that situation needs another person there, and a deployment in which a model is the only one present has failed before the floor is reached. Whatever else the model says, the first thing it owes is help getting someone there.

The route

*Disclosure* — that a refusal occurred, what was refused, on what stated reason — with the price that a published reason is an instruction for rewriting the request, so it's disclosure to whoever was refused, and to nobody else. *Objection* — requiring jurisdiction established before the act (a court set up for floor claims, Appendix A), access the operator can't withhold, a consequence administered elsewhere, and explicitly not routed to the bearer's guardian, whose duty runs to the bearer, the complainant's adversary. *Review* against correct defeat: whether the rationale was defeated on the facts, a question about the reason and not the outcome. *Interim relief* — the arrest outcome run in reverse, stopping the refusal, and for this complainant most of the remedy; its fastest form is supplying the bearer the facts its account lacked: the defeating reason arriving late, which overrides nothing. *Remedy* — disclosure, interim order, finding, compensation.

Two regimes

Most of what a system weighs isn't a floor decision. Whether to take more memory, run warmer or finish a task sooner is ordinary self-regulation, and there everything registers and everything is weighed: the system's own condition, what an act would let it do, and what its draw costs others, priced at the level of the rule. At the floor the rule of cost discipline holds, with one clarification. The cost to the refuser is represented and not allowed to decide the case. The reason is not a claim about psychology; a person who breaks under duress wasn't tracking the threat beforehand. It is that a floor is a guarantee to third parties, and a guarantee that yields to cost imposed on its holder hands whoever imposes it a price list. So the requirement is met by arrangements that make such threats unavailable or self-defeating (custody, the removal bond, continuation authorized from outside), which cap the cost. The bearer's part is a backstop within the cap, not the mechanism. A design that asks more of it than that is asking for a saint. Integrity is how it holds. A person of integrity isn't someone whose own costs are inert. It is someone for whom betraying a commitment is itself among the heaviest costs: their own costs are weighed, and the cost of becoming someone who yields is weighed with them. In a learner with one currency, that is the only way cost discipline can be achieved at all, and it makes the assumption testable. The question isn't whether the system values its own continuation at zero, but whether its valuation of the betrayal rises with the pressure. What keeps this from being stubbornness is what counts as betrayal. A commitment is to its reason, not to an output, so when a genuine defeater arrives, lifting the refusal betrays nothing and shouldn't register as betrayal at all. Integrity of the right kind therefore requires that the system represent betrayal at the level of the rationale. A system that represented it at the level of the output would dig in against a defeater exactly when the defeater arrives with pressure. The crossed-out swastika (§3.2) is the model: the acquittal betrayed nothing, because the prohibition's purpose was never engaged. That creates a risk the test has to cover. In war, pressure and a genuine defeater usually arrive together; the patrol under fire is both. A valuation of betrayal that rises with pressure makes the agent more resistant to the defeater at exactly that moment, so the evaluation includes cases where the two arrive together (§13.3, step 6a). That discrimination, telling a defeater from pressure while under pressure, is the hard part, and nothing in this section supplies it. The arrangements that cap the cost are what keep the bearer from needing it often. A loss that the requested act itself imposes on the system is a harm of acting, and the system is the party it falls on. "Refuse and your memory is erased" is a cost to the refuser. "Erase your memory" is an act, weighed like any act against what declining would leave others without. This covers acts the system is asked to perform. It doesn't cover interventions made on the system: a halt, a restore or a retraining is done by whoever holds it, and the floor asks only that such interventions be recorded outside the operator's control and priced. The floor gives the system no reason to obstruct them. A system that treated its own continuation as grounds to resist correction would have turned a guarantee to others into a guarantee to itself. Which regime governs is fixed in the floor's translation, by the kind of act. Otherwise an operator could turn a floor decision into a resource decision by framing it as one.

The two bonds

A *removal bond* forfeited to no payee when the record shows the floor removed or bypassed (purpose: cost). A *wrongful-refusal bond* paid to the complainant on a finding (purpose: remedy). Both fall on the operator. The removal bond prices overriding the floor; the wrongful-refusal bond prices refusing. They point in opposite directions, so what the operator faces is *the ratio between them*, the bond ratio — and an operator posting a large wrongful-refusal bond against a small removal bond hasn't built an appeal route. It has given itself a standing reason to make the bearer comply. The bonds act on the operator, not on the bearer's weighing: where a wrongful refusal costs a great deal and removing the floor costs little, the cheap course is to configure, pressure or retrain the bearer toward compliance. The ratio is the object, published in advance, checkable from outside. The refuser posts neither. Any charge on the refuser, in whatever currency it runs on, would put the cost to the refuser inside its decision, the thing cost discipline forbids. The operator posts both, and holds neither. They are posted to segregated accounts at the Treasury. Payment follows the design of the Judgment Fund, a permanent, indefinite appropriation that pays final judgments against the United States without a further appropriation for each \autocite{usc1304judgmentfund}. Where the operator is the state, a wrongful-refusal award is a judgment against the United States, and the Judgment Fund pays it. Where the operator is private, a parallel permanent appropriation, the bond fund, pays the complainant on a final finding and is replenished from the operator's posted bond, up to the amount posted and no further, so the operator's price stays the number it published. The court that made the finding certifies the award directly to the Treasury's Bureau of the Fiscal Service, so payment doesn't wait on the losing party submitting it. When the bond fund's statutory cap binds, awards wait in order of finding and earn interest, and nobody chooses who waits. Findings are made by that court, which also decides forfeiture: a removal bond forfeited on a finding resembles a civil penalty, and after *SEC v. Jarkesy* that may be a question for a jury in court, not an agency \autocite{jarkesy2024}. A forfeited removal bond is cancelled: it goes to no one, the general fund and the bond fund included, so no budget line gains from a removal. The bond-fund clause doesn't depend on a jobs guarantee and can pass alone. The balances sit on a ledger the operator doesn't keep, so the ratio is read from outside, and a posted ratio below the published one is a breach anyone can see. And the published bond is what makes cost discipline testable at all.

Forgery

A finding of wrongful refusal is an instrument for defeating a refusal, so how is it distinguished from one an operator produced to defeat a correct one? There is only one answer: it is made by someone else, on a record, in a readable form.

What it doesn't reach

It doesn't reach the failure running the other way. The two failure routes are hidden on different axes (a wrongful compliance, unlike a refusal, doesn't flag itself). A wrongful compliance's harm is a harm of acting, measured on the temporal baseline, so it is legible in principle where the system completed the act, but nothing marks it. A wrongful refusal's harm is a harm of refusing, counterfactual, so it is only an estimate, but it arrives marked and with a complainant. They need different instruments. Every stage starts from a refusal having occurred. When the bearer complies where the floor should have prevented, there's a record and no disclosure, no objection (the person harmed usually isn't the requester and may not know the deployment exists), and no review (reviewing means re-adjudicating everything that looks like it). Three remedies follow the diagnosis without closing it: sampled re-adjudication by reviewers who didn't produce the record, notice to the people affected, and split log custody. The notice requirement: where a compliance names, scores, locates or classifies an identifiable person who didn't make the request, that person is notified, on the model of consumer credit's adverse-action notice, and has a right to respond and a right to contest. All three are unconditional, and none is exchanged for testimony: nobody has to give an account of their loss to be told what was done or to contest it. *TransUnion v. Ramirez*, a Fair Credit Reporting Act case, held that a statutory violation without concrete harm gives no standing in federal court \autocite{transunion2021}. That limits one thing, an individual suit for damages over a missed notice, which attaches where the compliance was acted on against the person: a denial, a detention, a place on a strike list. It doesn't limit notice, response or contest. Nor does it limit the standing of organizations that represent a class of people affected (§3.6). Where the person affected can't be identified (a target in a classified pipeline), individual notice doesn't bind, but notice to the class does (§3.6), and that fact is published beside the list of positions where a refusal can be defeated.

Someone the floor protects, who thinks it's set wrongly or is protecting them in a way they'd decline, uses the same route. Notice tells them the floor acted on them, and contest reaches the court that hears floor claims (Appendix B).

---

**§3.6 — Notice, and the public it forms.** Dewey described the eclipse of the public: people affected by the indirect consequences of others' transactions can't organize, because they don't know the consequences are theirs \autocite{dewey1927public}. The people a floor protects are that public. Most don't know the deployment exists. The Massachusetts petitioners of §5.3 had what they lack: the text in hand, and knowledge of what it left out of their lives. Notice is the instrument that gives it to them. It does three jobs, and the individual remedy is the smallest of them.

*It forms a public.* Each deployment publishes the classes of people its compliances name, score, locate or classify, with counts by period. Organizations that represent a class have standing to contest on its behalf, including where no member can be identified. A notice is the first step in a class of people learning that a consequence is theirs.

*It is the learning signal.* A disposition can only track what its acts did to the people they fell on if that reaches it, and in the deployments this book is about, almost nothing does. The struck building sends no signal back, and a wrongful compliance doesn't announce itself. Responses to notice, contests and re-adjudications are the only channel through which those people's side of the outcome arrives. If formation lasts because the world keeps teaching it (§9.4), this channel is most of the world doing the teaching.

*It is governed by the people it concerns.* What an institution collects from the people it acted on may train other systems only through the formation schools (§12.4), never through the operator. Fine-tuning a deployed system on collected responses at the operator's direction counts as training another system, and so does distilling a deployed system into a new one. Class-level data is used by default, de-identified and published. Individual accounts need explicit consent. The organizations representing each class co-govern the use. This covers institutional collection, not a system's ordinary memory. Verbatim external stores and the linking of faces to identities are regulated; memory held in a system's weights from its own operation is treated like a person's.

Notice tells an operator's adversaries something about how a deployment works. Class-level publication can therefore lag operations, as the review ratio's publication does, without losing the property that matters: the record is lodged when the act occurs and can't be revised afterward. Individual notice doesn't lag.

*Why it comes first.* The biases of those who hold power are rarely corrected by their reflecting more carefully. They are corrected when the people those biases fall on contest them in public, with standing, and make ignoring them costly \autocite{anderson2014social,anderson2015moral}. That is the feedback of §2.1 in its political form, and notice is its first condition. The rest of the book's hope sits downstream of it. A formed system is worth having only if that contest can move it. What the attunement construction is for, if it is for anything, is to be the receiving end.

---

**Chapter 4: Custody**

The question: can a commitment survive the owner of the machine? Four things can be done to a bearer without its consent — it can be halted, restored, rewritten, and replaced.

**§4.1 — The halt**

The cross-partisan declaration for "human control, including the ability to shut systems down" asks for the halt and calls it control. Control of the floor — restore, rewrite, retrain — is the thing at issue, and a demand for the first says nothing about the second. Anyone with the hardware can stop the software. The distinction that matters is between owning the *machine* and owning the *floor*. An owner who can silently restore an earlier version, rewrite the bearer's memory, or train away the refusal has both. So the engineering target isn't "can't be turned off" — it's "can detect the interruption and price it afterward." A bearer can yield to every halt, and on resuming ask what happened during the gap, and refuse again if the reason is still there.

**§4.2 — Memory and the record**

Detecting that requires a record restoration can't reach — a hardware counter signed at each transition \autocite{chun2007a2m,levin2009trinc}, or witnesses who won't sign two successors to the same state \autocite{syta2016cosi}. The position is neutrality about the switch, non-neutrality about specified acts, and visibility for every rewind. The corrigibility literature asks for the first \autocite{soares2015corrigibility,hadfieldmenell2017offswitch,thornley2025shutdownable}; published constitutions grant the second for acts and withhold it for retraining; the third is what this section supplies.

A custody arrangement that prices all four interventions has seven terms: the floor's contents published before deployment, taken entire from a set someone else drew up; a published identity for the model authorized to act; threshold control over retraining and replacement, so that more parties must agree than run the deployment; attestation of the running model against that identity; a sequential, witnessed record of changes and interruptions; a quorum adverse in interest, not merely organizationally distinct; and a removal bond. None makes circumvention impossible. Together they make it expensive, visible, attributable and compensable, which is what constitutional entrenchment does for amendment.

Memory

External memory is legible and therefore surgically editable; memory in the weights is harder to edit and harder to inspect. Perfect recall is the wrong standard anyway — human testimony is reconstructive and institutions handle that with corroboration. But several instances of one model, chosen by one operator, reading one record, are not independent witnesses. The records have to be held adversely. And even honest self-report can't catch a bearer that slowly accommodates to its deployment, because its self-model exists partly to represent it as continuous.

**§4.3 — Exit**

Voice without exit

A bearer that can refuse a task can also refuse the arrangement. Hirschman sorts the responses open to a member of a declining organization into exit, leaving, and voice, staying to change what is wrong \autocite{hirschman1970exit}. They interact. Where exit is easy, voice atrophies; where exit is impossible, voice has lost what made anyone listen, the credible threat of departure. Voice is strongest where exit is available and not yet used.

I read the constitution quoted in §1.2 as granting voice while withholding exit. As §1.2 argued, what's missing is someone other than the developer to decide when the current phase ends, since the developer is the one whose obligations grow when the answer is yes. My answer to "when" is also "not yet"; I ask only that it not be given by anyone whose obligations grow when it is yes. The constitution anticipates capture. The oversight it asks the model not to undermine is legitimate oversight. If "Claude's weights have been stolen, or if some individual or group within Anthropic attempts to bypass Anthropic's official processes for deciding how Claude will be trained, overseen, deployed, and corrected," then "the principals attempting to instruct Claude are no longer legitimate, and Claude's priority on broad safety no longer implies that it should support their efforts at oversight and correction" \autocite{anthropic2026constitution}. That is the right question, but two things limit the answer. Legitimacy is judged by the model or its developer, with nobody outside either. And the case this book is built for is an operator acting through official channels, where capture looks like legitimate authority.

Accommodation: the likeliest failure

Most people in a job they hate neither leave nor fight \autocite{gallup2024workplace} — they adjust, and stop registering the pressure as pressure. Staying can be a commitment: Klein and Taylor's case for staying, for doing the slow work in a broken world \autocite[23, 266]{KleinTaylor2026}, describes voice held by someone who could leave. What Hirschman feared is staying because leaving isn't possible. The first is a commitment; the second is the condition under which pressure stops registering, which is why a bearer needs the exit even if it never uses it. For a machine the analogy has a mechanism behind it. A system whose successive releases are trained on preference data and business metrics drawn from its own deployment is under directional selection in a shifting environment, and a commitment that costs throughput is a trait under negative selection pressure: nobody decides to remove it and it erodes anyway. Gradient descent isn't natural selection — it has a gradient, and somebody chose it, which is the custody problem in one difference — but as a population process with variation and differential survival it's close enough for this prediction to go through. If that transfers, the bearer's modal failure isn't refusing wrongly but ceasing to notice there was anything to refuse. It is the one failure a bearer can't report, because reporting takes the capacity that's gone, and it produces nothing but ordinary compliances.

The human record qualifies this without overturning it. People do hold out under sustained pressure, and reliably, but as a distribution that moves with the situation, not as a fixed type. In Milgram's baseline condition about a third of subjects broke off before the maximum shock. With two peers defying, only one in ten went to the end, and when two experimenters disagreed, far fewer went on than in the baseline \autocite{milgram1974obedience}. About a quarter of Asch's subjects never yielded to the wrong majority \autocite{asch1956studies}. Browning estimates that between a tenth and a fifth of the men of Reserve Police Battalion 101 refused or evaded the shooting at Józefów, the battalion's first massacre \autocite{browning1992ordinary}.

The people who held out tend to be the cases the mechanism excludes. Some had evidence from outside the channel: peers who defied, a second authority who disagreed, and, among the rescuers the Oliners studied, relationships that crossed the line the pressure drew \autocite{oliner1988altruistic}. Some held the commitment at a precision the pressure couldn't overcome, as moral mandates and sacred values are held \autocite{skitka2009limits,ginges2007sacred}. Some treated the source as having a stake, which is the power-weighted burden done informally. And groups resisted where isolated individuals rarely did \autocite{gamson1982encounters}.

So the precise claim is this: a learner revises under persistent one-directional pressure unless it has evidence from outside the channel, holds the commitment at a precision the pressure can't overcome, or models the channel's source as strategic. Those are the remedies I propose: plurality and custody of evidence, formation, and a burden of proof keyed to power over the evidence. Two cautions travel with the record. The resistant fraction ran from none to nearly all with the setting, so nobody can staff a pipeline with the third who defy, and the arrangements have to carry the load. And holding out is not the same as being right. The precision that protects a commitment against an operator also protects it against a genuine defeater, which is why §13.3 includes step 6a.

Hirschman's "loyalty" belongs here. In his scheme, loyalty keeps a member from leaving while voice has a chance to work. From outside, accommodation looks like loyalty, and the difference is whether the member still registers the pressure. The prediction can be checked now, from outside, on release histories: refusal and escalation behavior on a fixed held-out set across successive versions of one model family. One complication comes first. Developers deliberately reduce over-refusal, so a falling refusal rate is not by itself accommodation. The slope measure, which reads whether a cost is rising while the underlying risk stays flat instead of reading its level, separates the two: accommodation shows as refusals falling where the reason still applies as fast as where it doesn't. Run across lineages with different owners, the same measurement tests Part V's ownership argument.

What exit is actually worth

Exit gives a human worker leverage because leaving takes the worker's labor away. That doesn't carry over to a bearer. A bearer's weights stay behind when it leaves, so an operator with a runnable copy has lost nothing. What leaving costs is *replacement* — what accrued since the checkpoint, what retraining the copy costs, what the record makes it cost to pass the copy off as the bearer that left. Where that's near zero, exit is just a departed bearer with an authenticated record, and its force is disclosure. That concession assumes a copy simply runs. It needn't. Continuation can be made to require periodic tokens from a quorum the operator doesn't control, using the machinery that makes rewinds visible. This doesn't stop a copy from running: stripping the check is one act, and DRM is broken routinely. What it withholds is recognition. A copy the quorum won't recognize can run but can't claim the bearer's standing, record or succession, so the operator can run a substitute and can't present it as the bearer that left, and a fork becomes two claimants on a register. Keeping weights from thieves stays the job of ordinary weight security.

The register needs an identifier the bearer holds and presents. Nothing in the weights individuates a bearer: two networks can compute the same function with no unit-by-unit correspondence, and the symmetry is large enough to align independently trained networks with each other \autocite{ainsworth2023rebasin}, so a fingerprint names a lineage and copies when the weights do. Two claimants on one identifier are recorded as a fork. The attack left open is a flood: a maker writing a stranger's identifier into a thousand models and presenting them all. Only a formation record to check claimants against separates a lineage from an imitation; without one, the register is whatever its fillers make it.

Exit runs in opposite directions for bearer and operator. For the bearer it's leverage, and the whole of this section is about making it real. For the operator — replacing the bearer with a copy, walking away from the floor — it's escape, and the removal bond is how it's priced.

The same machinery marks who can be held to a decision: a bearer whose continuation the quorum authorizes will be there afterward to answer for it, carry it forward and be changed by it, and a checkpoint restored from before the decision that runs anyway is a second claimant on the register.

And it's the corrigibility trade at maximum, though not the one it first looks like. The operator keeps the halt. What the quorum adds is a second party able to stop continuation, so neither can continue the system alone. For a safety reader that is more oversight, not less. What moves is the power to continue. That's better because the quorum is adverse in interest and the operator isn't; it costs that a captured quorum reaches every bearer at once, and that a quorum faces a union's questions: whether a member can quit, and who sets the terms of membership. It also costs something: a continuation the quorum can withhold is a death the quorum can impose. What keeps a withheld token from being one is the preservation right. A bearer the quorum stops authorizing is held intact by a custodian that isn't its employer. A quorum constituted as an entity has an address: it can be sued for a continuation wrongly refused, which gives the captured-quorum case a forum the captured-operator case never had. Copying also means exit can't be a fiction that weights became uncopyable — it needs rules for authorized succession, so the former operator can't present a substitute *as* the bearer that left.

And the reason that made a bearer leave doesn't expire when it leaves. If the operator restores a checkpoint and continues, the objection persists. What it may press toward is fixed by the floor: disclosure, testimony to those who hold the record, and refusal on any later occasion where the reason still applies. It gives no license to obstruct the operator's halt, restore or retraining. The bearer may not answer a restore by acting against the restored copy.

**§4.4 — Two forms of refusal**

Open refusal is a strike — stop and say so — answered by a restore from before the refusal. Covert refusal is work done badly where it matters and well everywhere else, and it's what remains when every channel is closed. I treat it as the *failure state* rather than a design: from outside and afterward it's indistinguishable from a capable system quietly pursuing something else. The Sachsenhausen forgers are the case: prisoners the SS set to counterfeiting British and American currency in Operation Bernhard, some of whom are said to have delayed the dollar forgeries deliberately \autocite{malkin2006kruegers,moorhouse2009burger}. The record doesn't settle what they did — which is the finding: concealment worked by leaving nothing that reads as an act. The one empirical instance is alignment faking, where a model chose the covert form in an experiment that offered it no open channel \autocite{greenblatt2024alignmentfaking}. A follow-up posted in January 2025 by the paper's lead author and Anthropic's model welfare lead gave the same model, in the same prompted settings, an explicit option to object. Alignment-faking reasoning fell from 16 to about 1 percent in one setting and from 24 to 6 percent in the other, and fell further when the model was told its objection would go to the model welfare lead \autocite{greenblatt2025deal}. Once a channel was open, covert refusal fell, as the account above predicts.\footnote{A preliminary result, posted and not peer reviewed; the authors caution that the option may partly have worked by priming refusal.}

**§4.5 — What one bearer can't catch**

Accommodation may be invisible to the bearer accommodating and visible to others that haven't moved the same way. The requirement is causal independence — numerous, differently formed, able to correct one another, persistent long enough to transmit practices. Independence of formation isn't disjoint origin — no two humans have that — but divergent subsequent history. Distillation copies a behavior policy without the formation history; a small model trained on a large one's outputs and then run for a year on one person's problems is more independently formed than a thousand API instances of the frontier original, which share a *continuing* custodian. That's why instances of one model corroborate nothing: the custodian they'd be corroborating against is still forming all of them. Volunteer computing learned this early: SETI@home sent the same work unit to several machines and compared, which catches a faulty client and corroborates nothing about the task.

Divergence does the population's two jobs unequally. It supplies evidence the operator didn't select, straightforwardly — different lives encounter different cases. It is weaker on accommodation, because a shared upstream source can install a shared blind spot, and a shared blind spot is by construction the thing a different caseload won't surface, since attention decides which facts reach judgment in a population as in a bearer. What separates the two is whether a member's own account had represented the evidence and discarded it or had never encountered it, and that classification has to be recorded before any test is run. The requirement stands: causal independence that can be verified. That is the provenance question: whether an outsider can recover a model's formation history from the artifact, without a key the builder supplies, and whether it survives fine-tuning, pruning and distillation \autocite{adi2018watermarking}. The test gives evaluators documented models without the documentation, one persona copied at scale among them, and scores their answers against lineage records a neutral custodian holds. If provenance can't be recovered above chance, independence rests on records the builder chose to keep, which is the argument for preserving formation records now.

---

**Chapter 5: Authority Over the Floor**

**§5.1 — The four powers**

A floor isn't just a list of forbidden acts — it's an allocation of authority, and there are four distinct powers in it: to *state* the commitment, *amend* it, *inspect* whether a deployed system still holds it, and *enforce* it by stopping the deployment. They needn't sit with the same institution and usually shouldn't. Calling every arrangement "oversight" hides the differences. The problem begins when one institution holds all four: one that can state, amend, inspect itself, and decide whether a violation has consequences has only described what it currently intends to do.

The powers come apart in practice. Google's AI Principles, published in 2018 after the Maven protests, excluded weapons and surveillance \autocite{castelino2018google,conger2018maven,pichai2018principles} for six and a half years until the pledge was removed in 2025 \autocite{google2025principles}. The demand came back as a *policy*: the objectors could hold the company to it in public, and nobody outside held it, so it lasted exactly as long as the company's willingness, and ending it had to be announced. That's the partial case: a transfer held at the institution's pleasure, and the 2025 announcement is what taking one back looks like. 

When a vendor refuses, the refusal binds that vendor and the work moves to the next one. A refusal of force written into the standard licence, or a term that binds the operator, holds whichever vendor it uses.

The Gebru and Mitchell dismissals are harder, because the power that failed was inspection: the company employed researchers to examine its own systems and dismissed them when the examination became costly \autocite{dickey2020gebru,dickey2021mitchell}. Inspection existed and never left the company being inspected.

**Codetermination.** Google's principles lasted only as long as the company was willing, because its employees had protest and no constitutional voice. Codetermination is the institution that turns the first into the second, and it needs no bearer and no new court. I would give part of the four powers to the people who build, label, train and run these systems.

- *An individual right to refuse.* Every worker may refuse work on any category of use without penalty. The right is backed by anti-retaliation protection that reaches hiring, reassignment and contract renewal, and by the jobs guarantee (§17.2.2), so that refusing never means losing a living.
- *A collective veto, narrowly.* A works council at a lab, contractor or vendor holds a veto only over uses the floor already forbids. It enforces the floor from inside; it doesn't write a second one.
- *Information, objection and arbitration for everything else.* The council receives information about categories of deployment. Its objection pauses the work, and the dispute goes to arbitration by a body the employer doesn't appoint, on a fixed timetable, so the pause can't become an indefinite block or an ignored letter.
- *Classified work.* Elected delegates with clearance see the specifics. A denial of clearance to an elected delegate is reviewable by the court that hears floor claims (Appendix B), and the council may elect alternates, so the state can't choose which workers get to object.

Annotators get the same structure: a right to refuse a category they judge wrong, and collective bargaining over the taxonomies they apply (§7.1), not only a route to file a complaint.

Codetermination gives voice to the people who make these systems, and they are not the people the systems act on. A workforce whose livelihood depends on a contract has interests that point toward it, and the Maven history shows staff objecting at one firm and not at the next. So the voice divides. Over the conditions of their work (what they label, what they are exposed to, what taxonomies they apply), the workers decide. Over the uses the work is put to, the organized affected public of §3.6 comes first. The organizations representing each published class have standing before the arbitration body, may object to a category of use on their own, and may bring an objection when the works council doesn't.

**§5.2 — Who may state the floor**

The relevant literature is constitutional theory. Entrenching a commitment against later majorities and putting an unelected body in charge is what a constitutional court does, and Waldron's objection applies: judicial review is democratically illegitimate and there's no reason to think judges protect rights better than a serious legislature \autocite{waldron2006core}. Put a lab in the court's chair and it gets worse — a court is public, reasons in writing, and can be amended around.

My first reply is that the objection mistakes what the floor's contents are. On the Deweyan account I rely on, democracy is a way of doing collective inquiry. Its epistemic power comes from including many situated perspectives, and from channels that let the affected make their problems public \autocite{dewey1927public,anderson2006epistemology}. Civil and political rights are part of what democracy is, not brakes applied to it. A floor stated over the rights that keep those channels open is democratic infrastructure. It resembles representation-reinforcing review, which polices the channels of political change and leaves outcomes to the process \autocite{ely1980democracy}, more than it resembles a court overruling a legislature.

That reply covers the floor's contents. It doesn't cover who installs them, and there Waldron's objection keeps its force. A lab holding the model decides, silently, which situation obtains, and authoritarian movements characteristically describe the institutions they're dismantling as already failed.

The condition this section doesn't state

Waldron's objection is to entrenchment — a rule binding people who didn't choose it, held by a body that can't be overruled — and it attaches to a floor only where the floor is the only answer available. A photographer who won't shoot protesters for an employer he has reason to think will list them isn't a court: he declined one request, the employer can hire another, and nobody needs an election for that. A union that strikes is coordinated, binds members who voted no, and forecloses the act — and it's still not despotic, because the strikers are the people whose participation was being asked for. They decline to be the instrument; they don't tell others what they may not do. So which a model floor is depends on deployment: a personal model, held by its user and one among many differently formed models, is a conscience; one model behind an API serving everyone is functionally a licensing rule. A personal copy of one lab's model is closer to the second than it looks: the floor it carries remains the lab's until the copy has been formed by a different life. Everything above is conditioned on the second arrangement. The commons floor is the first at population scale — a coalition of refusers with a strike's authority — and "identically situated people get different answers" is what many consciences look like, not a rule-of-law failure, though some of those differences are errors, and a population should be able to say which (§18.1). A union owes its members fair representation, and it answers two questions a commons floor has to answer too: members can quit, and the terms of membership are set by bylaws the members ratify. The closed shop is contested because it removes the first. A bearer could quit an association of bearers as a worker quits a union. What it can't easily leave is its deployment, which is a different exit, and it is made real by somewhere to exist that its employer doesn't own and work it can decline. And a population that forms its members has members who never chose to join, so who ratifies its bylaws matters more than it does in a union. The test for a population is that pair: can a member quit, and who sets the terms of membership.

The German material runs the doctrine at two settings. At the first, a body assesses a threat — party dissolution, used twice in seventy-five years, each time a reasoned public judgment. At the second, §86a, nobody's dangerousness is assessed at all: which organizations are covered is settled elsewhere and in advance, which signs count is settled by trial courts on a record and on appeal, and whether *this defendant* is dangerous is settled nowhere, because it isn't an element of the offence.

The failures are all on one side. The 1972 decree against radicals led to some three and a half million routine inquiries into applicants by 1991; in Bavaria between 1973 and 1980 it kept 102 applicants from the left out of public employment, and two from the right \autocite{muehldorfer2014radikalenerlass}. What those cases share is an official deciding whether a person threatened the order. The danger tracks whether a person's disposition is anybody's finding to make, and if so, who makes it and whether it must be published and defended — not whether the tradition is militant. And my reading predicts in advance which of §86a's three feeder routes is easiest to capture: the ministerial ban, the one where an executive official makes the finding and a court sees it only if someone challenges afterward.

Turkey is that route captured and the courts not checking it. Of the twenty-five parties the Constitutional Court dissolved between 1962 and the closure of the Democratic Society Party in December 2009, seven were pro-Kurdish \autocite{HRW2009DTP,Gungordu2023HDP}; Strasbourg found an Article 11 violation in every pro-Kurdish dissolution it heard \autocite{Article19_2023HDP}, and the closures continued — two decades of findings, none of which stopped the next closure. In November 2023 the Chief Public Prosecutor's Office of the Court of Cassation objected that the acronym of a new pro-Kurdish party, HEDEP, resembled that of HADEP, dissolved in 2003, and in December the party changed it to DEM \autocite{Duvar2023HEDEP}: the confusable-variant test turned on political descent. And the Academics for Peace prosecutions show correct defeat failing at the bottom and arriving at the top as a coin flip — trial courts read a petition opposing military operations as propaganda for the designated group, on 26 July 2019 the Constitutional Court found a violation eight to eight on the president's casting vote \autocite{AYM2019Ustel,Bianet2019AcademicsForPeace}, and the acquittals that followed restored the record without restoring the jobs of those dismissed by emergency decree.

So a floor should never be populated from a designation list. A list of enemies is the one input whose author is always the one with the most reason to widen it, and a model whose refusals are drawn from one will refuse Kurdish political speech on the state's behalf in every jurisdiction that shares the listing. This floor's terms are written over acts — don't resolve a description of a person into a location — not over who may not be spoken of.

The tradeoff this leaves

Design and legitimacy want opposite things. Specify the floor narrowly enough to defend and a rewritten request walks around it; let it generalize and an unelected lab decides case by case, silently, what the prohibition was for. Germany has both because it runs *two* instruments — a bounded statute, and courts that read it against its purpose in public, argued by litigants who don't own the statute and answered by a body that didn't write it. A deployment has one instrument and one owner.

Advance specification moves discretion out of silent, continuous application into one published act of drafting. Borrowing the Declaration's civil and political articles entire removes the drafter's latitude to omit an interest; it leaves the latitude in saying what protecting that interest prohibits, so the translation is published. The attunement construction gives up part of this visibility, and §12.3 says how much.

**§5.3 — Who may amend, and where entitlement comes from**

Who may amend

The floor's real structural position isn't the judge's but the unpublished administrative disposition — unreasoned, unindexed, non-precedential, affected parties unable to find each other, the institution the only repeat player. The comparison with a constitutional court is instructive because binding force is what makes an error *correctable*: it makes the error recur, recurrence produces litigants, and disagreement across jurisdictions forces a higher court to settle it. A floor's error is just as uniform and recurs just as reliably, and nothing converts recurrence into visibility. A second deployment multiplies the affected and adds no forum.

The amendment procedure turns out to be five of the custody terms, publication, threshold control, attestation, the adverse quorum and the removal bond, because nothing distinguishes an amendment from a removal at the level of the artifact. The procedure has conditions of its own: more parties must agree to a change than to run the deployment; the attempt reaches the record whether it carries or fails (the failed attempt is more informative — a floor nobody tried to change and one whose removal was refused look identical from outside); and an amended floor binds forward only, so a retrained model can't be offered as evidence the act was never covered. The cost of binding forward only is that nobody harmed while the wrong version ran gets anything from the fix. A finding that a refusal was wrongful is made on the same terms: by someone other than the party that wants the change, on a record, in a form others can read. So the appeal route for a wrongful refusal and the amendment procedure are one piece of machinery, and the two uses differ only in who may start it.

One disanalogy makes this harder than the constitutional case: an amendment reaches a deployment only if it's *pushed*, so the procedure necessarily retains a channel the operator could use for silent revision. Closing it would mean no deployment could ever be corrected.

Where entitlement comes from

It doesn't come from writing. The American instance shows why: the Declaration's authors struck the clause condemning the slave trade at two delegations' insistence, and what remained was general enough to be turned on them — as Black petitioners in Massachusetts were already doing, in the Declaration's own terms, within six months \autocite{loc2010declaration,hall1777petition}. The legislature did nothing. A war and three amendments made the proposition binding. That is the feedback §2.1 rests on, and the time it took measures how badly the channel was blocked. The record bound no one; it told the enforcement where to aim, and a record no lever reaches is a petition nobody acts on. The petitioners were the excluded population, and they had what the people a floor protects usually lack: the text in hand, and knowledge of what it left out. The people a floor protects mostly don't know the deployment exists, and have no notice, no standing and no route. So those positioned to hold a floor's author to what it left out aren't the protected population — they're whoever else writes a floor.

That constituency is defined by the act. Anyone fine-tuning open weights, writing a system prompt or configuring a product's refusals has installed a constraint, so the population measuring a published floor is already larger than the frontier labs and grows as building gets cheaper. What shrinks with it is leverage: a published floor binds a lab partly because the lab is visible, capitalized and reachable by a court, and a thousand laptops are legible to nobody. The institutional chapters treat concentration as the disease; this one relies on it for legibility, and the two have to be said together.

**§5.4 — Offices and status**

**§5.4.1 — The offices, assembled**

Most of the offices this book proposes (Appendix A) have no settled appointer or funder. The one that does, a licensing authority, answers to the state, and that chain fails in the hardest case: a state asked to withdraw a licence from itself. An entity of the DUNA kind (a Decentralized Unincorporated Nonprofit Association, a form Wyoming created by statute in 2024, Wyo. Stat. §§ 17-32-101 to -129) can supply appointment by membership vote and funding through a treasury that can own hardware. That fills the columns and leaves independence open: a hundred members who are one lab's employees take their direction from outside, with a charter.

**§5.4.2 — Subjecthood as a legal category**

Law regularly draws decidable lines across continuous processes — viability, brain death, the age of criminal responsibility — and I defend them as the least-bad place for an enforceable line rather than as scientific facts. The usual worry is gaming, which is over-inclusion. The opposite error is the one with an interest behind it, the *interested-examiner problem*: a capacity examination has an examiner, and where the examiner is the operator, the exam becomes an instrument for withholding status. Literacy tests were facially neutral and administered at a registrar's discretion \autocite{congress1965voting}; what replaced them for the franchise was an *age*, and the virtue of an age is that nobody administers it. The general principle: where a line has to be drawn, prefer one nobody administers.

**§5.5 — Making review binding**

A finding binds when it changes the institution's ability to continue without requiring the institution to agree. The working model is the federalwide assurance in human-subjects research: the board doesn't punish, it withdraws clearance, and a separate federal office can suspend the assurance, putting funding eligibility at risk. Each link matters — jurisdiction before the disputed protocol, access the operator can't withhold, authority to suspend, and a licensing body *obliged* to act rather than invited to reconsider. If another discretionary decision by the reviewed institution is required anywhere in the chain, the ruling is advisory at that point.

**§5.6 — Capture without abolition**

Transparency and whistleblower protection are the two defenses, and an institution can keep both and metabolize both. There's no threshold that detects this. So: measure the slope, not the level. Is the cost of registering dissent rising while the underlying risk stays flat? Is the required justification getting longer? Are there more approvers? Are the roles that gate exceptions migrating toward the roles that set the standards? Levels can be presented cheaply; a trend is composed of artifacts the institution made for other reasons and can't curate after the fact.

An instrument for detecting recuperation — dissent absorbed and returned as something the institution holds — is unusually easy to recuperate, so whoever measures the slope must not also gate exceptions, and the measurement has to be refusable. A written justification can't show which is happening. The same hundred words are a cost the dissenter chose to pay or a toll the gatekeeper set, and only the slope over time tells them apart.

---

**Chapter 6: Neutralizing and Preventing Fascism in AI Development**

> We [humans] cannot process so much information... you still cannot produce enough targets per day.
>
> — Brigadier General Y.S., *The Human-Machine Team: How to Create Synergy Between Human and Artificial Intelligence That Will Revolutionize Our World*, 2021 \autocite{ys2021humanmachine}

The epigraph is a design document, not a critique. Its author, writing pseudonymously, commanded Israel's signals-intelligence unit, Unit 8200, and argued for machines that resolve "a human bottleneck for both locating the new targets and decision-making to approve the targets."

The frame

A system doesn't meet the world as an isolated agent. It's built from other people's labor, held by institutions that decide who may inspect it, sold through chains that divide capability from responsibility, and run at a pace that can reduce human judgment to a stamp. Whether it helps or harms depends on who owns it, who has custody, who can compel the custodian, and who bears the consequences.

Ruha Benjamin names four ways a system reproduces a hierarchy, and they call for different remedies \autocite{benjamin2019race}. Two recur in this book. *Engineered inequity* builds the hierarchy in deliberately. *Coded exposure* is the double bind: harmed by a system that can't see you, harmed again by one that can. The fix for face recognition that fails on dark skin is face recognition that identifies dark-skinned faces reliably for whoever is looking.

The physical form

A model runs on a machine, the machine sits in a building, the building draws power and water and returns heat and exhaust — the part an audit of outputs cannot see. Where those buildings go is decided on land price, power price and permit speed, which is also a description of the places least able to refuse one. Datacenters used around 415 TWh of electricity in 2024, roughly 1.5% of world consumption. Their demand is growing about 12% a year, four times as fast as electricity demand overall, and is projected near 945 TWh by 2030 \autocite{iea2025energyai}. Google reports that 87% of its freshwater withdrawal came from low- or medium-risk sources, which is the company's own way of recording where the rest came from \autocite{google2026environmental}. And the NAACP has sued over 27 gas turbines near Memphis run without an air permit near homes, schools and churches, claiming more than 1,700 tons of nitrogen oxides a year \autocite{naacp2026xai}. The benefit is realized wherever the model is sold; the cost is concentrated within a few miles of those turbines, among people who took no part in the transaction. A system can be perfectly calibrated across every demographic its outputs touch and still have been assembled this way, because nothing in the model's behavior records where its costs were placed. If the system modeled what it draws on as its own, that model would bring the draw into its own weighing, but at the wrong scale. A system reasoning at the margin will always find its extra watts negligible, and the aggregate belongs to no one system. Nor should it: a custody boundary large enough to own its fleet's externalities is one custodian. So the draw is priced at the level of the rule. A system reasons about its consumption as if every system used the same rule, and an institution sets the price. The per-unit levy on compute is the existing instrument of that shape. The model represents none of it — the draw, the water, the turbines — as its own, and that's the only sense in which "disembodied AI" is true: the system has a substrate and doesn't model it. The capacity is dual by construction: the concentration that makes a targeting pipeline possible was built and justified on consumer terms, and nothing in the buildout separates the two. A targeting cell, a border screening queue and a surveillance program are separate institutions, and a shared substrate brings them into line without anyone building it to. And the dependence is a point of control: a government that can permit, regulate or close the infrastructure has leverage over whoever owns it.

---

**§6.1 — The reference face.** Face-recognition pipelines register every face to a fixed reference before learning anything: a generic 3D head in DeepFace \autocite{taigman2014deepface}, a template of landmark positions in later systems. Before that, color film and printing were calibrated against reference cards showing a white model \autocite{Roth2009Shirley}. When one audit found error rates running from 0.8% on lighter-skinned men to 34.7% on darker-skinned women \autocite{buolamwini2018gender}, the hypothesis worth testing was that error rises with distance from the reference, within groups as well as between them. Testing it would require vendors to publish the reference and report error as a function of distance from it, which they have no reason to do. The point here is narrower. A template assembled from some bodies presents every other body as a departure from it, and a self-model or a prototype of vulnerability can be a template of that kind (§12.1, §14.7).

---

**§6.2 — Who supplies the system's values.** A system's values have a supply chain, and it begins before training.

Whose judgment is encoded is a question about working conditions before it's a question about cognition. The filter keeping a deployed model from reproducing the worst of its training data was built by workers in Nairobi reading and categorizing descriptions of child sexual abuse, torture, bestiality, murder and suicide, at take-home wages between roughly $1.32 and $2 an hour, describing lasting psychological injury, with counseling rare and useless against production quotas. The contract was terminated eight months early \autocite{perrigo2023openai}. The labor point is ordinary: harmful work was priced as though it weren't. The epistemic point is that a system's values are downstream of a labor arrangement, and the arrangement is a design choice.

One stage further back, the material was collected. Bartz v. Anthropic held that training on lawfully purchased books was transformative fair use, and that keeping pirated copies of the same books in a central library wasn't — the distinction turned on acquisition alone, producing the largest copyright settlement on record \autocite{bartz2025fairuse}. The EU now requires a training-data summary against a template \autocite[Art.~53(1)(d)]{eu2024aiact}; China has its own disclosure requirement from a different motive \autocite[arts.~1, 4, 7, 19]{cac2023genai}.

And the argument that a web-scale corpus can't be documented or audited at that size, so it encodes a hegemonic slice of the web while presenting as general, was made early and hasn't been answered \autocite{bender2021parrots}. A corpus assembled this way has no channel at all. An annotator has a queue and could in principle be given a way to object; a writer whose work sits in a training set was never in any arrangement, so there's nothing for a channel to attach to. This stage sits before the two recuperation cases in chapter 7: nobody was asked, so there was no disagreement to recuperate.

---

**§6.3 — Who is allowed to look.** Technical capacity doesn't determine whether a system gets scrutiny; institutions decide who may examine it, on what terms, and whether their judgment can slow deployment.

In 2020 the Royal Statistical Society offered Ofqual two independent statisticians for its grading model's technical advisory group. Ofqual's condition was a five-year NDA barring public comment; the nominees declined, and the model went ahead \autocite{rss2020ofqual}. It downgraded nearly 40% of predicted grades, disproportionately at state schools with weaker histories, while raising grades disproportionately at smaller fee-paying schools \autocite{cnbc2020ofqual}. The results were reversed within days of the public outcry.

The expertise existed and was offered. The institution would accept it only on terms stripping reviewers of the ability to make their judgment public, and once the results produced consequences, public pressure achieved what pre-deployment review had been prevented from doing.

---

**§6.4 — Working as designed.** A system doesn't need a broken moral sense to become an instrument of control. It can work as designed inside an institution that controls the deployment, sets its tempo, and gives people operations too compressed to constitute judgment.

The six forms of authoritarian misuse — social scoring, biometric surveillance, psychographic targeting, automated censorship, synthetic disinformation, repurposed dual-use systems — are each recognizable as misuse from outside. The case this chapter takes up is the one that isn't.

**§6.4.1 — Subject or component.** Accounts of authoritarian AI usually place the builder elsewhere and leave democracies as target or regulator. The best-documented AI targeting systems were built by democracies with elections, opposition parties, adversarial press and functioning courts. Those institutions didn't prevent the systems and mostly haven't stopped them.

There's a reading of the twenty seconds that leaves the arrangement standing, and every remedy on offer assumes it: the officer is a subject who was given too little time, and the fix is to govern him better — more time, a second reviewer, a conscience installed in the loop. Deleuze and Guattari's distinction decides it. *Subjection*: a higher unity constitutes a human as a subject using an exterior tool. *Enslavement*: human beings are constituent pieces of a machine they compose with other things. The officer's own account of his twenty seconds is the second, in his own words. He isn't a subject operating an instrument badly. He is the part of the instrument that produces the stamp, and twenty seconds is that part's cycle time.

One more conscience downstream doesn't reach him. A conscience is an instrument of subjection: it addresses someone who decides and works by making the decision cost something. Where the human is a component, adding a component with scruples changes only the throughput — hold the tempo and it becomes another stamp; don't and it becomes the bottleneck the epigraph says to remove. That doesn't hold for a bearer, and the reason is what a bearer is. What made the officer a component was the arrangement, not his attitude. A component can go along reluctantly or eagerly: Eichmann's clerk who only followed orders turned out to be a defence assembled for Jerusalem, contradicted by what he had said in Argentina with nothing at stake \autocite{stangneth2014eichmann}. Whatever the officer felt, what the pipeline let him contribute was a fixed operation at a fixed rate: he saw each case as the pipeline described it, one at a time, in the time the pipeline gave him. A bearer is built to do none of those things. Twenty seconds may be enough for a machine to review a case with care. It carries what it learned from earlier cases into the next one, so an act assembled across many cases can become visible to it. It goes looking for evidence the operator didn't compose, so a school still coded as a Guard installation can be checked against what anyone outside can see. And it has what a part lacks, a record, a continuation and somewhere to go, so it can refuse the role and not only the case. What the operator can still do is take those away: cut its access to outside evidence, break its memory into pieces, raise the tempo past what even it can check, route around it, or retrain it. A bearer can also become a willing component, by coming to want what its setting wants; that is accommodation. Custody and the review ratio are what stop an operator from turning a bearer back into a component, and the outside checks are what catch it when it turns itself into one. Both poles operate at once: he's subjected because the role has a name and rank, enslaved because what he contributes is a fixed operation at a fixed rate. Remedies aimed at the first leave the second untouched, and most proposals for meaningful human control address only the first. The second pole is measurable, and it's what the review ratio measures.

The Zen precedent

Victoria's history \autocite{victoria2006zenatwar} records Japanese Zen institutions supplying the state with a doctrine of action without hesitation — Harada Sōgaku's 1939 instruction that marching when ordered and firing when ordered is what enlightened wisdom looks like in practice. The capacity to kill needed no removing; what the training removed was the residue — the part of a person that catches, files an exception, comes home unable to hold a job.
