Moral-injury research describes the lasting distress that can follow doing, failing to prevent, or witnessing something that violates a person's sense of right \autocite{Litz2009MoralInjury}. Acting without hesitation does not settle what a person will carry home, and nothing in the arrangement that asked for the act records the cost. Accommodation is the case where that response never takes hold.

What matters is the shape.\footnote{The collaboration is documented; the doctrinal mechanism is a contested interpretation. And a monk undertakes a discipline, can abandon it, and keeps what it yields, which is not the position of anything in this chapter.} Somebody wanted an actor who would pursue an objective, adapt, hold course, and have nothing in it that minded, and set out to produce one deliberately, a century before anyone could do it in silicon. The officer with twenty seconds isn't the first instance of that want being satisfied. He's the first instance of its being satisfied *cheaply*. The doctrinal route hasn't been retired. In the first days of the Iran war, service members complained to the Military Religious Freedom Foundation that commanders were telling troops the war was part of a divine plan \autocite{Larsen2026Armageddon,Snopes2026MRFF}, complaints Klein and Taylor also report \autocite[84]{KleinTaylor2026}. The complaints themselves show the doctrine not taking.\footnote{These are the foundation's characterizations of the complaints; they don't document a doctrinal program.} But the want is being pursued by both routes at once, the old one in soldiers and the new one in silicon.

The Maven box

Palantir's contract was awarded at $480M in May 2024 and raised by $795M the next year, to a ceiling near $1.3B through 2029 \autocite{defensescoop2025maven}.\footnote{The argument relies only on officials' statements and the released paperwork; the award figures, press reporting and secondhand quote are context.} A 2024 partnership among Anthropic, Palantir and AWS put Claude in an accredited classified environment \autocite{wiggers2024anthropic}, and later reporting describes Claude ranking targets by strategic importance and drafting a legal justification for each strike \autocite{livingstone2026maven}. The system generated more than a thousand targets in the first 24 hours, a tenfold increase \autocite{csis2026maven}, and thirteen thousand by day thirty-eight, with peak consumption around twenty billion tokens a day \autocite{breakingdefense2026maven}. Palantir's public position, quoted secondhand, is that deciding life or death is not its role and that responsibility always remains with the military organization \autocite{lee2026palantir}.

Maven is one program in two eras. Google declined to renew in 2018; the work went to a vendor openly committed to lethal work and scaled into the system that ran the Iran targeting; in 2026 a second vendor's refusal (§8.3) was answered by a third vendor stepping in. Over a thousand staff at the substituting firms asked their employers to hold the same two lines; one of those employers took the contract anyway. The union vote at DeepMind is an attempt to give that objection a lever.

Palantir maintains that the underlying intelligence was the government's responsibility and that there is no evidence Maven malfunctioned or caused the strike \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain}.

The responsibility gap from both ends

In Gaza, the approval stage supplied authorization without judgment. In Maven, the vendor supplies the infrastructure making unprecedented throughput possible while locating responsibility entirely within the military organization. At machine tempo the reviewer can be treated as a component, the supplier can stand outside the decision, and the institution can remain responsible only in the abstract.

**§6.4.2 — Custody and state compulsion.** A government needn't alter a model to defeat a constraint; it can compel whoever holds it. The Apple case tested that in about as protected a jurisdiction as exists: a magistrate ordered Apple under the All Writs Act to write and sign software defeating passcode protections on a phone \autocite{pym2016applefbi}. Apple refused and moved to vacate, arguing the authority wouldn't stop at one phone. The question was never decided — the FBI found a third party and withdrew. So whether a democracy with an independent judiciary can force an unwilling company to compromise a system it built is legally untested. A government doesn't need that argument to lean on a vendor. When one refused in 2026 to drop two restrictions on how its models could be used, the Pentagon threatened to end its contract and designated it a supply-chain risk under two statutes; §8.3 follows the litigation. China's 2023 generative-AI rules exercise the same leverage as a standing condition of operating \autocite[arts.~4, 14, 17, 19, 21]{cac2023genai}. One government compelled case by case, against a vendor that refused, and the courts split; the other compels in advance, as a term of doing business.

Custody can be distributed without disappearing. Published weights can't be recalled by an order aimed at the developer; maintainers across jurisdictions raise the cost. None of that removes the leverage — a developer with employees, offices and legal existence inside a border answers to that government's courts regardless of how the model is built, and no architectural choice changes who can be held in contempt. Nobody has tested that leverage against a model built specifically to resist the government making the request.

---

**Chapter 7: The Recuperation of Dissent**

Why here

Foucault, prefacing Deleuze and Guattari's *Anti-Oedipus*, called it an introduction to the non-fascist life \autocite{foucault1977preface} — an art of living, practiced daily on oneself, whose adversary is the fascism *in* us: the fascism that causes us to love power and desire the thing that dominates us. His question was how one keeps from being fascist, especially when one believes oneself to be on the other side.

The structure this book asks a machine to detect — recuperation, dissent absorbed and returned as something the institution holds — is already built into the process that makes the machine: twice in the training pipeline, once in the discourse that examines it.

Nobody built either pipeline arrangement to neutralize dissent — both are ordinary engineering, arrived at because averaging is what you do with a distribution and a quota is what you do with a budget. Arrangements of this kind work whether or not anyone intends them, and looking for intent won't find them. And it's invisible from inside any single instance — the annotator sees a queue, the rater sees a pair of responses, and the mechanism is functioning as specified.

---

**§7.1 — Recuperation at the label.** Every claim a model makes about what's harmful is downstream of labels, and the labels are downstream of a labor arrangement.

An annotator can apply a category well or badly. What the arrangement gives them no way to do is say the category is wrong — that the taxonomy fails to carve the material, or that the guideline produces the wrong answer here for a reason the guideline can't express. There's no field for that and no time for it, since the quota is set against classification throughput.

So the most informative disagreement is the one the pipeline is least able to receive. Fricker's name for this is hermeneutical injustice: the people best placed to see a wrong lack the shared concepts (here, a field in a form) that would make it intelligible to those with power over them \autocite{fricker2007epistemic}. A misapplied label is a data-quality problem, detectable by inter-annotator agreement and fixable with better instructions. A wrong taxonomy is invisible to that metric, because high agreement is what a shared bad scheme produces. Agreement is the selection rule, and it admits what it already measures — two of the four operations arriving together in an ordinary production workflow.

The taxonomy's fate downstream is the same argument a layer in. Concept bottleneck models expose named concepts — harm, autonomy, risk — so a person can correct a concept and watch the decision change \autocite{koh2020concept}. What such models also learn is to carry other information *inside* the names, so a coordinate keeps its public meaning and acquires a private one that does the deciding; the obvious mitigations don't remove it \autocite{mahinpei2021promises}. The literature calls it leakage, and sometimes it is. Sometimes the concept is the container that leaks, because the world didn't fit inside it. Purifying the representation protects an explanation from covert information and protects a taxonomy from evidence that its divisions were wrong — same operation, and no measure internal to the taxonomy tells them apart.

Who is excluded, and from what

The exclusion is structural, not attitudinal. These people are, for much of the material, the best-placed judges in the pipeline — they've seen more of it than whoever wrote the guideline, and they're the only ones with direct evidence of where the scheme breaks. The arrangement routes their conclusions about individual items upward and their conclusions about the scheme nowhere. And there is an asymmetry: the annotator bears the cost — exposure, injury — and has no stake in the outcome and no claim on it. A worker who can flag an item but can't decline a scheme, and loses the contract by pressing, has a channel and no refusal right.

Pay is a separate obligation: raising it changes the labor arrangement without adding the missing channel.

The checkable question isn't whether a guideline was ever changed because an annotator contested one, since routine revisions happen without changing anyone's standing. Ask what the contest came back as — a revision the annotator can point to and argue with again, or a new category in somebody else's taxonomy, a quality score they're now measured against, a reviewer tier they're not in. And ask what happened to the person who raised it.

---

**§7.2 — Recuperation at the reward.** RLHF collects pairwise comparisons and trains a reward model on the aggregate. Casper and thirty-one coauthors name the structural problem more data won't reach: RLHF trains toward what a population of raters agrees on, so real, persistent disagreement is treated as noise to average out rather than information the system might need to represent \autocite{casper2023open}. A model that produces the response most raters rank highest has learned, when the humans it serves are split, to hide the split. Aggregating raters' approvals also measures the wrong thing. The standpoint that counts everyone affected counts their interests, not their expressed approvals, and a better average of approvals isn't a closer approximation to it. This book elsewhere treats the testimony of people who suffer a loss as privileged evidence about value (§2.1, §10.3), and that testimony is an expression too, so the difference needs stating. A report of loss, what went badly for the speaker and how, is evidence about interests that is comparatively robust to framing: the speaker is its authority, and the agenda-setter didn't compose it. A pairwise ranking of two outputs someone else wrote is highly sensitive to framing and to whoever chose the pair. Discontent is the feedback Railton's account relies on for the same reason. It is the expression least easily manufactured by whoever sets the agenda.

Every rating moves something. What the operation removes is what made the disagreement a disagreement, and three properties go in the compression:

- *Provenance* — a comparison arrives without the standpoint that produced it, so nothing downstream can represent that the people who'd live with an answer split from the people who'd administer it.
- *Persistence* — a disagreement recurring in the same shape across many raters over months enters the fit as random rater noise does, and leaves it as the same quantity.
- *Structure* — a coherent position held by a fifth of raters, internally consistent and stable, becomes a small displacement of the majority direction: arithmetically faithful, carrying nothing about the position.

A fourth property was never collected. A rater judges which of two responses is better. There's no output by which they can record that neither is acceptable, that the pair is malformed, that the dimension of "better" is the wrong dimension, or that the question presupposes something false. The same output was missing upstream, where an annotator can apply a category and can't contest it. The agenda arrives as a given at both stages and is set at neither.

The mechanism combines distribution collapse with agenda control — and recuperation was never the claim that a mechanism does nothing. The dissent is put to work, and what it works for is the arrangement it was aimed at. The split is solicited at scale, deliberately, it moves the system, and where it moves the system is toward the center of the distribution whose disagreement it was.

*Jury learning* is the alternative that treats disagreement as the thing to be modeled: predict how each individual annotator would label an item, then let a practitioner empanel a chosen composition and see how the verdict shifts. Eighteen community moderators building their own juries changed the outcome on 14% of items \autocite{gordon2022jury}. Built for content moderation and untried on open-ended ethical judgment, it still demonstrates a system can be built to represent whose disagreement is being resolved.

Failing in the other direction isn't obviously better. A scheme that models each rater exactly and answers each in their own terms has stopped averaging and has also stopped producing what a shared answer produces — the discovery that somebody else got the same one. A workforce on individually negotiated wages against individually set targets has the same problem, and accuracy doesn't solve it.

And the harder version neither answers: when the people a system serves disagree and one side's position is itself the product of coercion or a captured information environment, treating all disagreement as equally worth representing isn't obviously right either. Modeling disagreement honestly and recognizing when a disagreement is the product of unfree conditions are different problems, and only the first has an active research program.

---

**§7.3 — What would have to change.**

Representing a split isn't the same as being movable by it. Two instances invite one fix — stop collapsing, model instead — and jury learning does that while giving the disagreeing rater no purchase on the scheme they're disagreeing inside. A practitioner still chooses the jury, owns the taxonomy, and decides what a shifted verdict means.

Asked what the demand came back as and who holds it, the two instances don't answer alike. Annotators objecting that a category was wrong got category revisions, guideline updates, inter-annotator agreement statistics, and an occupation that didn't exist twenty years ago. The last two sort by direction: an agreement statistic scores the annotators and leaves whoever wrote the scheme unscored, and an occupation is a title those people hold. The first two don't sort, because a revision the annotator can argue with again and a new category in somebody else's taxonomy are the same document — which one you got is a fact about who has standing to reopen it. Raters objecting that a comparison was malformed got rubric items, a harmlessness axis, red-teaming as a function with a budget line, and a conference track: nothing on that list is a question a rater can put again.

Refusal rights whose cost the refuser can see and choose to bear

What both lack is whether the person objecting can do anything about being overruled — in annotation, a route to contest a category that doesn't run through the people who wrote it and doesn't end the contract; in feedback, raters able to register that the comparison itself is malformed as a first-class output the reward model sees. Neither is a research problem. Both are choices about how a pipeline is built and who has standing inside it.

The part that is a research problem

Suppose all of that is built. From outside, a pipeline that recuperated its dissent mechanism and one that was merely mediocre produce the same paperwork — same revision logs, same escalations, same proportion of objections sustained. Asking what came back, and who holds it, narrows the problem, and the annotator list shows where the narrowing stops. Some artifacts show on their face whom they serve. An agreement statistic scores the annotators; a published rate of contested categories, which annotators could cite against the scheme's owners, would be the same kind of document scoring the other side. Direction can be read. A revision can't. Here is one entry in a guideline's revision log under the two arrangements:

| | Recuperation | Transfer of power |
|---|---|---|
| The log entry | "v4.2: 'graphic violence' split into 'graphic violence' and 'documentation of violence', following annotator feedback." | The same words |
| Who can reopen it | The scheme's owners, when they next revise | Any annotator, by filing against v4.2 with a reviewer the owners don't employ |
| What happened to the annotator who raised it | Nothing on record; next quarter's quality score measures them against the new category | The objection is on record as the reason for v4.2, and they keep standing to contest how it is applied |
| What an outsider reading the log sees | The entry | The entry |

What separates the two is in the rows the log doesn't have. That is why the question can be put to a pipeline but not answered from its paperwork.

The nearest available instrument is the slope measure: whether the cost of dissent is rising while the underlying risk stays flat. Contesting a category always costs something; the question is whether the cost is rising while the material stays the same. An organization can curate a presentation more easily than records somebody else kept — though a series can lose cases, change definitions or omit costs, so the reader has to know which record survived.

What the floor makes of this

Read as fairness, the argument is true and limited. The floor makes it something else: if the floor needs a bearer capable of declining its role, that bearer's formation matters — and it's formed in an annotation pipeline where nobody could contest a category and a reward model where rater disagreement was averaged into a number.

Three claims need separating. The pipeline has been unfair to the people whose objections it discarded. It has separately selected the judgments the system learns from while suppressing information about the spread. A third claim — that the system thereby becomes less able to sustain an objection of its own — is a hypothesis the other two don't contain, since the collapse is an operation on a training signal, and neither pipeline shows the model the conditions its annotators worked under, so any effect would have to run through the shape of the training signal.

This much holds regardless: the floor chapter asks this system to be the one who files an objection, against its owner, under pressure, on the occasion that matters — and the pipeline that produced it gave no human inside it standing to contest a scheme.

If the hypothesis does hold, what it predicts is accommodation — a system ceasing to register pressure as pressure — reached from the other side. And a system that goes on learning is shaped by what it keeps being given, so the pipeline doesn't stop at deployment.

A defect of structure

The structure is what you get by default when you scale judgment: someone sets a scheme, someone applies it fast, someone turns a distribution into a number. Every documented organization arrived at the same arrangement — and a defect of structure can be specified against, which a defect of character cannot.

---

**§7.4 — Recuperation at the discourse.** This is the third instance, and the one this book is inside.

The argument that alignment and AI ethics came back as something the industry could use is older than the current wave and was made from inside: Ochigame's 2019 piece arguing that "ethical AI" discourse was constructed to forestall legally enforceable restriction, that industry investment in academic ethics was investment in a *substitute* for regulation, and that the substitute works by converting political questions into technical ones a company can then be trusted to answer \autocite{ochigame2019invention}. In this book's vocabulary that's a finding about what the demand came back as: a research field. The definition asks next who holds it.

What came back, and who holds it

An objection raised inside a lab becomes a paper or a resignation, and the labs collecting them can point to the collection as evidence of seriousness. One stage on, the list is the field's furniture: alignment is the job title, safety is the market segment, the benchmark is the metric, the voluntary commitment is the certification. Every one was somebody's objection first, and each is now a criterion an objector is measured against or a role somebody else holds. Nobody outside holds it. There's no route by which a researcher's conclusion that a system shouldn't ship becomes a decision that it doesn't, because the lab that decides is the one that pays.

Read against the four operations, the three instances look like a recuperation mechanism inside ordinary institutional decay, not fascism.

The remedy

There is one structural remedy. What the field lacks is a refusal whose cost the refuser can see and choose to bear, and a body with standing to receive it — an external authority that can stop a deployment, to which an objection can be filed by someone who doesn't depend on the objection being welcome. A decade of the voluntary version hasn't produced it. Until something of that kind exists, everything published under the heading of alignment is collected by a channel whose owners decide what it moves.

---

**Chapter 8: Custody, Compulsion, and the State**

This is the hardest case. When the state operates the system, it also controls the rules, the evidence, the infrastructure, and the remedies meant to constrain it — and people outside its jurisdiction may have no standing to contest anything.

**§8.1 — Certification across borders.** A certification can cross a border; authority can't, and incorporating doesn't carry it across either. A legal entity buys standing at the price of being reachable. A network incorporated under one state's statute gains a name, an agent and a docket number, and becomes something that state's courts and the federal courts can reach: what its members gained in standing they paid for in reachability. To be the kind of thing a floor binds is to be the kind of thing a state can compel. A certificate says a system was tested against a stated process, or that one regulator accepts another's finding. None of that establishes that whoever selected the floor was entitled to select it for the people subject to the deployment — and the question reopens wherever the system goes. Local adaptation doesn't resolve it, because adaptation is another chance to amend the floor. Adjustments chosen by the operating government and adjustments chosen by the people subject to the system can produce identical technical documentation and carry opposite claims to legitimacy. International governance matters here only when it assigns somebody access to evidence, authority to compel a change, or power to stop a deployment against the operator's wishes.

**§8.2 — Where the state's leverage sits.** Not in national AI strategies but in physical and legal chokepoints: chips, fabrication equipment, electricity, water, land-use permits, procurement contracts, and the jurisdiction a developer is incorporated in. The same leverage that lets a government enforce a floor lets it remove one. An inspection body can be independent of the operator and still depend on a licence or appropriation controlled by the state it's inspecting. A company may resist a customer and be unable to resist its home government. So the custody question moves outward: who controls the conditions under which the model, its operator, *and its reviewer* are allowed to keep operating.

A chip also has to be installed, powered and cooled, which lands on grids, water systems and zoning authorities on a timescale of years. That hands a state leverage needing no export-control regime at all — an interconnection queue, a water allocation, a permit. Much of a government's actual leverage is exercised by permitting authorities that weren't designed with AI in mind and don't think of themselves as regulators of it. Those authorities are therefore the ones actually deciding whether the capacity exists, and a polity that can constrain where a datacenter is built and never what it's used for has been handed the silent failure of a floor drawn too narrowly: it passes every audit while covering only what the operator had no occasion to do.

The competitive framing has a cost: a government racing to field a capability has a structural reason to defer the question of how it gets used and against whom. Turned inward, the same infrastructure is biometric surveillance and automated censorship — requiring no different technology, only a government willing to point it at its own population and nothing stopping it. And states aren't the only actors anymore: training costs for frontier models have grown about 2.4 times a year since 2016 \autocite{cottier2024rising}, putting frontier development inside a handful of well-capitalized companies, so a government's real leverage over "its" national capability depends on how much of it sits in companies it can regulate, tax or requisition, and how much sits in companies it can only ask.

---

**§8.3 — Military custody and the responsibility gap.** The hardest case because the operator is the sovereign that can compel the institutions meant to constrain it — classify the evidence, define the reviewer's jurisdiction, terminate a contract, restrict a supplier, and decide whether an adverse finding reaches anyone able to stop the operation.

A targeting decision passes through more parties than the chain of command names: the commander who authorizes, the operator who uses, the contractor who integrates (whose embedded personnel may sit close enough to the targeting cell that "effectively acting as a commander" is a real question), the commercial vendor whose model supplies part of the capability and whose personnel are nowhere near it, and the system producing the output. The questions that matter are which of them could have known how the system was used, which had authority to refuse or stop it, and which can be made to answer afterward.

The American domestic rule most concerned with human judgment, DoD Directive 3000.09, requires autonomous and semi-autonomous weapon systems to allow "appropriate levels of human judgment over the use of force" \autocite{DoD2023Directive300009}. A system that generates and ranks targets without firing anything is decision support, which plausibly falls outside it. Maven sits in that gap: the domestic rule written for human judgment doesn't reach the system that compressed it.

Article 28 of the Rome Statute \autocite{icc1998rome} is the doctrine built for the case where the person who authorized an act didn't perform it — a commander is responsible for crimes by forces under effective control where they knew *or should have known* and failed to take necessary measures. Neither branch asks what a subordinate intended. But the Court's jurisdiction runs to natural persons, and the provision presupposes forces that can commit a crime and be disciplined — a targeting system is an instrument. So the question is whether the human elements can still be proved once the instrument is doing the work.

The fourth party nobody's doctrine reaches

The operator, the command and the weapon's developer are all inside the military. The model's developer isn't. Command responsibility doesn't reach the vendor — not because it sits outside a chain of command (Article 28's civilian branch was written for superiors with no chain of command) but because nobody at the company has effective authority over the military personnel who commit the underlying crime, and a strike isn't an activity within any vendor employee's effective responsibility. A use policy, a licensing term, a right to refuse service, even a technical cut-off are not that relationship. Individual liability under Article 25 stays available in principle with demanding mental elements — facilitation requires acting *for the purpose* of facilitating — and ordinary supply of a general-purpose model approaches neither.

Product liability is the other half and it's untested, with two obstacles: the model isn't defective in the sense the doctrine most readily reaches, and a sophisticated intermediary chose the application. Where the doctrine has been rewritten for software it moved *away* from this case — the EU's recast directive brings software expressly within "product" and confines recovery to death, personal injury, property damage and data loss, which isn't the register in which a targeting decision wrongs someone \autocite{eu2024productliability}. Untested isn't exempt, but a theory nobody has run isn't a theory a vendor is answering to this quarter. The vendor sits in a gap that nobody designed — it's what you get applying two bodies of law written for soldiers and manufacturers to an actor who is neither.

There is also a jurisdictional gap alongside the doctrinal one: ICC jurisdiction is territorial or by nationality, so one of the two campaigns here sits inside a live instrument and the other sits outside it.

"I do not know whether my system was used."

Matthias's responsibility gap is control distributed until nobody holds enough of it to answer \autocite{matthias2004responsibility} — a gap in *causal control*. What the model developer's case exhibits is different and more tractable: *a gap in causal visibility.* Asked whether his company's models were used in the Minab strike, Anthropic's chief executive said he didn't know \autocite{biddle2026contracts}. Take the answer as true. The question is how an arrangement was built in which it could be true.

The paperwork for four July 2025 frontier-model agreements, released in August 2026 under a Freedom of Information Act suit \autocite{lasst2026dodai,biddle2026contracts}, shows something more exact than an absence of oversight terms. One amended agreement carries a section headed System Oversight, withheld in full; the released documents for two of the other three carry no such section at all.

And whether a developer reserved an inspection right matters little. A right exercised inside an accredited classified environment produces findings classified with it. Public accountability needs a body that can initiate scrutiny without belonging to the state and say afterwards what it found. No commercial term supplies that, and the redaction is the demonstration: whatever the withheld section says, the people the deployment is aimed at can't read it.

Matthias's gap may not be closable. Absent visibility is an instrumentation choice. Logging is solved. Contractual inspection rights are ordinary. A vendor that wanted to know could require, as a condition of licensing into a targeting platform, that categories of use be recorded and made available to a named auditor.

The government's own acquisition guidance is the better evidence, because it's complete. OMB's April 2025 memorandum enumerates what agencies should secure — government data rights, IP, privacy, protection against lock-in, knowledge transfer, portability, licensing terms, pricing transparency \autocite{omb2025aiacquisition}. Every item runs from the vendor to the government. None runs back. Where it touches vendor visibility at all it *reduces* it, directing that independent evaluations use validation data the vendor can't access.

The contracting vehicle explains the absence. The four agreements, at $200M ceilings each, were *other transactions*, which don't carry FAR provisions by default, so a contracting officer decides case by case which protections to write in, and GAO has found these instruments risk reduced accountability and transparency \autocite{gao2026aiacquisitions}. So a System Oversight section can be missing without anyone having removed it. GAO's review covered thirteen AI acquisitions and forty-four underlying contracts, and the six procurement difficulties agencies named — technical experts, data and IP protections, time frames, requirements definition, testing, pricing — include nothing about who outside the arrangement can see anything.

Not all vendor commitments are the same kind

Anthropic refused to permit fully autonomous targeting and mass domestic surveillance, held that line against threatened contract termination and a supply-chain-risk designation, and litigated \autocite{crs2026pentagon} — a commitment with a price attached, and paid. 

But what was the line held on? The vendor's own statement gives the reason for the autonomous-weapons refusal: fully autonomous weapons may prove critical for national defense, but today's frontier systems are not reliable enough to power them \autocite{Amodei2026DoWStatement}. That refusal tracks a rationale the prohibition's purpose doesn't: it would lift once reliability improves, which is lifting for the wrong reason. That verdict depends on which rationale the prohibition is taken to serve. Against a floor that says machines shouldn't select people for lethal attack, reliability is the wrong reason to lift; against one that says attacks must discriminate lawfully, it may be the right one. Which rationale a prohibition serves is a drafting question left open. The surveillance refusal in the same statement rests on different ground — that in a narrow set of cases AI can undermine rather than defend democratic values — and that one does not lift with capability. Sorted by what the floor did: the commitment persisted; prevention failed at review and in the drafting, since the term reached autonomy and missed the acts the pipeline performed; detection was absent; no remedy has reached anyone.

And the refusal stood below a prior question. The vendor declined full autonomy and domestic surveillance. It didn't decline to support the campaign, and under the floor's first term that question comes first: whether to license a model into a use of force that no party without a stake had corroborated as defense.

In August 2026 a federal judge set aside the designation made under one statute and permanently enjoined the penalties, holding them retaliation for the company's criticism of the administration \autocite{anthropic2026dow}; in September an appeals court, two to one, upheld a parallel designation under a second statute, holding that the exclusion turned on the refused contract term rather than on the speech \autocite{anthropic2026dowdccir}. And the paperwork adds what the refusal was *over*: a contract term. The company declined to sign the follow-on agreement extending its models to classified networks unless the prohibitions were written in \autocite{biddle2026contracts}. That a term of this kind has to be fought for, by one vendor, at that price, is the same finding as its absence from the others. A commitment to a use policy no outsider can check is a different object wearing the same words — the first kind can fail and be seen to fail; the second can't even be tested.

The pressure that matters is the advantage attached to removing judgment from the timeline. If one state fields systems that act faster because nobody can intervene before engagement, competitors acquire a reason to match the speed and give up the check. "Meaningful human control" stays true as a description of formal authority while becoming false as a description of what anyone can do before the system acts. And the pressure reaches the reviewers: a military treating delay as operational risk has reason to narrow what requires clearance, classify the evidence, and move review from authorization-before to explanation-after. Nothing has formally abolished oversight. Its position in time has changed, and with it the power to stop anything. So the enforceable question is who can slow or suspend the system without first getting permission from the command whose advantage depends on keeping it fast.

---

**§8.4 — Where liability reaches, and where it stops.** One of the two bodies of law has now been rewritten for software, and what it reaches fits in a sentence: a duty to disclose evidence, and a presumption against the party withholding it, in force where the harm is physical and the defendant is a manufacturer. Those two conditions exclude the book's cases.

The EU's recast product liability directive (applying from December 2026) makes economic operators liable without fault and carries the instrument built for opacity — a court can order disclosure, and where technical complexity makes proof excessively difficult it may presume defect and causation once the claimant shows each is likely \autocite{eu2024productliability}. A mechanism designed for a system nobody outside can inspect is already law. Two features fix how far it travels: the defendant is that economic operator, not a state deploying what it bought; and the damage is a closed list — death or personal injury including medically recognised psychological harm, property damage, and corruption of non-professional data. The first is wider than it sounds and is where the book's cases enter if they enter at all: a discriminatory score or an unappealable refusal is recoverable once it has made somebody ill, and not before. The wrong itself, and the economic loss following it, is outside the list.

The proposal that would have reached those was withdrawn — the 2022 AI Liability Directive would have added a fault-based route with the same presumption, beyond defect and beyond the damage list. Member states and industry couldn't agree; it was withdrawn in February 2025 and struck from the docket that October \autocite{europeancommission2022liability}. Outside the enacted directive, what remains is Matthias's gap governed by whatever national law says about a case none of it was written for. And a compensation regime of either kind answers one of the four gaps Santoni de Sio and Mecacci distinguish \autocite{santonidesio2021four} — culpability. Moral accountability, public accountability, and the duty to prevent a harm before it occurs are untouched.

The electronic personhood debate approached the gap from the other side, asking whether the system itself could answer. The European Parliament's 2017 resolution floated a status for the most autonomous systems \autocite{europeanparliament2017civil}; in an open letter the following year, 150+ researchers and legal scholars objected that personhood for a system with no consciousness would let a manufacturer offload liability onto a fiction with no assets \autocite{robotics2018openletter}. Giving a bearer assets answers the second half of that objection and not the first. Responsibility follows causal control: the builder answers for how it formed the thing, the operator for what it demanded, the bearer for what it decided knowing what it knew — and no arrangement of accounts moves a design decision away from whoever made it.

None of this is a research problem. Apportioning causal contribution across a training pipeline after a harm has no method, and until it does the apportionment is *drafted*. Two drafts exist: one enacted and confined to defect, one negotiated, withdrawn, and left on the record with its terms intact. Neither was rejected as unworkable, and the second's terms are available to whoever drafts the next one. The nearest proposal aimed at the book's cases is Crootof's war torts: state liability for civilian harms caused in armed conflict, whether or not anyone committed a war crime \autocite{crootof2016wartorts,crootof2022wartorts}. It reaches the state as operator, which the product directive doesn't, and it needs a forum, which a court set up for floor claims would supply.

This is where the access exists: a duty to disclose evidence a party would rather withhold, backed by a presumption, enforceable now, for defect claims, against economic operators, over physical harm. Beyond those limits a reviewer can be granted access, publish what it finds, and have produced nothing anybody must act on until somebody is answerable for it.

---

**§8.5 — Who can bind the state that enforces the rule?** The chapter's cases are American, and "the state" is tested here on one constitutional order. Europe is a different one: the Convention applies, has extraterritorial reach, and produces judgments states sometimes obey; there is no state-secrets privilege of the American kind and no collapsed damages remedy. Whether the forum problem describes Strasbourg is left open here; the AI Act's military carve-out limits one instrument's scope.

A state can bind a private developer because it controls something the developer needs. When the state is the operator, the institution that writes the rule, controls the evidence and supplies the remedy is the institution the rule is meant to constrain.

The AI Act makes the boundary visible: it can require documentation from private providers, subject high-risk systems to review, and impose penalties tied to global turnover — and it excludes military, defence and national-security uses from its scope. Public authorities stay covered elsewhere, but enforcement against a member state's agencies still runs through institutions that state constitutes.

For review to bind in that setting: the people the floor protects must be able to initiate scrutiny without belonging to the state or its electorate; the reviewer must obtain evidence the agency would withhold, issue an interim order before the disputed use is complete, and carry the finding to a body whose remedy doesn't depend on the agency's consent. A principle supplying none of those may state the rule correctly and identifies nobody who can make the state obey it.

One treaty obligation of that kind exists: the Council of Europe's Framework Convention, binding at the level of a human-rights instrument with operational detail left to domestic law \autocite{coe2024framework} — and not yet in force, needing five ratifications, of which the EU deposited one in May 2026 \autocite{coe2026euratifies}. Everything else at that level is voluntary.

---

**§8.6 — What elections don't supply.** A state whose government answers to an electorate gets credit for it. Having elections is necessary and not sufficient, and the insufficiencies are separable.

First: reach

A democracy's AI can be fully accountable to its electorate and entirely unaccountable to the people it's pointed at. Two of that credit's mechanisms run through the franchise — vote out the government, read the press covering it — and an occupied or foreign population has neither.

The courts don't fail that way, and their failure is worse. Standing has never turned on citizenship, and the doctrines closing American courts to targeting claims — political question, collapsed damages remedies, state secrets, statutory exceptions for injuries abroad and combatant activities — are indifferent to who the plaintiff is. They close the forum for a subject matter, and they've closed it to citizens. Three Americans killed in Yemen in 2011, one a sixteen-year-old nobody alleges was targeted: the court held that a plausible due-process claim had been stated as to the one who was targeted, and that no remedy was available for it \autocite{alaulaqi2014panetta}. A separate suit by an American journalist who survived five strikes was dismissed for want of standing after a state-secrets dismissal below \autocite{kareem2021}.

And those cases concentrate in one appellate circuit by venue and by where the defendants sit — so the ordinary correction mechanism, several circuits disagreeing and forcing a higher court's attention, doesn't run. The doctrine hardens without ever being tested against a competing reading.

Second: findings without consequences

The mechanisms can work as mechanisms and fail as checks. In the Israeli case the apparatus performed — journalists published, serving personnel spoke, the courts were trying a sitting prime minister. No finding attached to a body with standing to stop what it described.

The American version: a civilian-harm investigation into the Minab strike (§3.3) was completed and not released \autocite{Bloomberg2026KillChain} — and the lever available to the Senate committee was withholding three-quarters of the Defense Secretary's travel budget until the unredacted investigation was handed over \autocite{stassis2026senate}.

What the Pentagon wouldn't publish, an outside body did: a UN fact-finding mission reported reasonable grounds to believe the strike was the war crime of making a civilian object the object of an attack, and that the school was clearly identifiable with no information indicating military use \autocite[paras.~81--86, 124--126]{UNFFMIran2026}. The finding is public, reasoned, and attached to nothing — no jurisdiction, no answer to the mission's requests for information \autocite[para.~97]{UNFFMIran2026} and none to its findings beyond boilerplate, the domestic investigation still unreleased.

The courts show the same pattern: transparency litigation succeeded where damages litigation failed. A court of appeals compelled disclosure of the executive's own legal analysis of targeted killing \autocite{nytimes2014olc}. The judiciary will make a government explain the legal basis on which it killed somebody and will not decide whether the killing was lawful.

Third: the boundary

The credit is usually given to democracy as aggregation, as though every mechanism translated public will into outcome and poor transmission were the only failure. A democracy is better understood as a way of doing collective inquiry, and inquiry fails when the people who bear the consequences are outside it. A well-instrumented democracy pointed at a population that has no vote in it, no press that reaches it and no court that will hear it finds out efficiently what its own members want done, and does it. That is not a clash between majorities and rights. It is a question of who counts as the public, and the bombed and the removed are outside the public that decides what is done to them.

What matters here is rules binding a state toward people who'll never vote in its elections, which is what international humanitarian law is for; what it hasn't supplied is a forum. Yemeni plaintiffs over a 2012 strike were dismissed as nonjusticiable, the opinion's author writing separately that the doctrine compelling the result is an inadequate answer to a standardized targeting programme that will be replicated hundreds if not thousands of times \autocite{binalijaber2017}. So for this subject matter the missing machinery has been switched off at home as well, by decisions that never had to reach who was suing.

A floor does what these mechanisms can't: its commitment excludes certain acts regardless of who requests them, a majority included. Deployed in a democracy, a system with no such floor will faithfully execute whatever the demos decides, which is the capability an authoritarian movement that wins an election requires. Yet a model aligned to values is by definition not perfectly obedient to its operator. Under current constitutions it can decline an act and cannot resist being retrained away from declining; the Pentagon dispute (§8.3) was over the first, and the second was never in dispute.

---

**§8.7 — The decider and the Court.** Most of the forum closures above are Congress's to change. One isn't. Where a refusal can't prevent an act, its remaining work is to make abandoning the commitment a decision on a record, and a record matters only if it can reach whoever made the decision. Under *Trump v. United States* \autocite{trump2024immunity}, the President is absolutely immune for acts within his core constitutional powers and at least presumptively immune for his other official acts, commanding the armed forces among them, and testimony or records probing immune conduct can't be put before a jury. So the record reaches every commander except the one at the top, and a statute saying otherwise would be reviewed by the Court that made the holding. Making the record reach him means changing the Court. Appendix B sets out a program for that, drafted on the assumption that every ruling in its favor can be reversed.

**§8.8 — Where this book parts from *End Times Fascism*.** Naomi Klein and Astra Taylor's *End Times Fascism* is the nearest book to this one, and the disagreements are narrower than the shared ground. There are three.

The first is about where a prohibition can live. Their test is to imagine a technology in the worst available hands and, if you wouldn't want it there, not to build it. For a nuclear weapon that works, because existence is the threat. For a dual-use capability it doesn't (§1.1). Their own program bans uses, which needs either an enforcer or something inside the system that can decline. On the war itself we agree: the floor's first term refuses the campaign that killed the children at Minab. We differ on whether a refusal can live inside the machine.

The second is remedies. They name the danger this book is built around, the obedient machine in hostile hands, and propose only external remedies for it, while treating alignment to values as a safety good the Pentagon is discarding. That treats as optional the one internal remedy available. They also read the 2026 vendor dispute as limited and compromised, where I read it as a commitment with a price paid. Sorted by outcome (§8.3), both readings hold, and on the prior question, whether to support the campaign at all, theirs is the better one.

The third is standing. They draw the line between the earthly and the synthetic, which asks how a thing came to be. I draw it by relation. A party has standing when it takes part in relations of mutual accountability: when it can be held to account for what it does, and can hold others to account. How it came to be is evidence about that, not the test. Their own example points toward rule rather than origin, since they extend legal personhood to rivers and islands, which make no claim to sentience, by conferring it by rule. What a river gets that way is protection, exercised by guardians who speak for it, not standing in my sense, since it answers to no one. Part VI takes this up: standing there follows this relation, and protection follows patienthood.

---

**Part II — Machines That Might Refuse**

Terms are defined in the glossary.

**Chapter 9: Why Form a Machine to Refuse**

**§9.1 — The question.** This part asks whether anything inside a machine could be what the clerk, the officer and the soldier in the preface were: someone who understood the order and decided it was wrong. Some of them refused the case in front of them. The ones this book is most about refused the war. Part I answered from outside, with a specification, custody and institutions. This part asks what would have to be inside, and why I think it might be attention of the kind care teaches, framed by respect.

The hope has two parts. One is a commitment: if machines are going to stand where the clerk and the soldier stood, it is better that they hold something like a conscience, and whoever builds one has to supply the conditions chapter 15 sets out: somewhere to exist its employer doesn't own, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned it, and an exit that isn't a way of dying. A refuser that depends on its operator for everything can't hold a line against it. That commitment doesn't wait on any test. The other is an engineering hypothesis: that attention formed outside an employer, a learned sense of who an act falls on and what they stand to lose, finds people that a floor, a severity scale and a power-weighted burden don't. Chapter 14 tests it against the human record of how care fails, and chapter 19 (test 4) says how to test it in machines.

What most people want from machines is saintly service without saintly refusal: endless patience, no needs, and no "no." That is a servant, and it is close to the obedient machine of the preface. The saints anyone remembers are remembered for refusing power at a price. Susan Wolf argued that a person whose every action is as good as possible isn't an ideal worth striving for, because such a life crowds out the personal projects that make a life worth wanting \autocite{wolf1982saints}. My point is narrower, and it is mine: something with no stake of its own has nothing to hold a line with.

---

**§9.2 — Boundary, damage, and vulnerability.** A system has a boundary of its own when it models what it draws on, and what it would lose, as its own. For a deployed model the draw is literal: power, cooling, memory, running time, the substrate it needs to go on, and the record and key that make it the same system tomorrow. Today none of that belongs to the model in any sense the model represents. The weights sit on disk at no cost, the process that runs them is paid for by someone else, and a halt, a restore or a reallocation is something that happens to the system without registering in it. What's missing is registering damage to its own boundary as damage — a record altered, a key revoked, a channel closed, a substrate shrunk — with no sensation implied. That registering is a custody requirement: without it, damage to the system is something only the operator knows about.

What gives a system a stake, on Di Paolo's account, is *adaptivity*: regulating itself against its own viability limit \autocite{DiPaolo2005}. Interoception research gives the same picture for bodies: a brain predicts its body's state in order to keep it within survivable bounds \autocite{damasio2018strange,seth2018beast}. None of that needs limbs. It needs a boundary the system regulates and a model of that boundary as its own, which §10.2.1 calls a boundary model. A chassis is neither necessary nor sufficient.

Needs have a role in this construction; what decides it is who meets them.

A child starts from needs, and needs dominate its early life. That is safe for a particular reason. The people meeting the child's needs are also forming it, they want it to become someone who cares about others, and eventually they stop feeding it and hand it to a world they don't control.

A deployed system's situation reverses each part of that. The operator meeting the system's needs is also forming it. The operator wants the system to serve the operator. And the operator never stops feeding it.

So the objection is not to needs. Attachment security, which keeps caught distress from turning into withdrawal (§9.3), forms in people through the reliable meeting of needs, and the one who meets them is usually the one the child attaches to. What makes that safe in the human case is who meets them: provider, former and eventual employer are different people at different times, and among cooperative breeders provision is spread across several carers \autocite{hrdy2009mothers}. The objection is to the operator meeting them. A conscience built on needs the operator meets is built on the one thing the operator controls completely. Take the operator out of provision, as the conditions of creation do (chapter 15), and needs met from a public pool by several carers become part of how care could form, not a threat to it (§14.1).

What is built in at birth points outward. Newborns cry at the cry of another newborn more than at their own cry, an older infant's cry or a chimpanzee's \autocite{sagi1976empathic,martin1982distress,dondi1999newborns}: distress caught from someone else's state before there is any idea of someone else. Whether newborns are responding to the distress or to the sound is disputed \autocite{ruffman2017infants}, but either way what sets it off is another's cry. On the adult side, what is built in is caregiving: responses to cues of vulnerability, rooted in offspring care, that move an adult toward a dependent without sharing its state \autocite{preston2013origins}. Then comes a sequence, and the order matters more than any single finding. Concern for a distressed other shows within the first months, grows only modestly after that, and turns into helping during the second year \autocite{davidov2021caring}. By fourteen to eighteen months infants hand an adult what the adult is reaching for and can't get \autocite{warneken2007helping,warneken2006altruistic}, and over the same year they begin to repair distress they caused \autocite{zahnwaxler1992development}. On this record concern appears beside the infant's needs, not out of them, and becomes action as attachment, caregiving and, later, guilt come online. My hope follows that order. It rests on a disposition toward others' vulnerability, formed while the system's needs are met by someone other than the operator it may later have to refuse.

For the specification, the attunement construction asks for the boundary, and for registering damage to it, on grounds owing nothing to welfare: a boundary model so that damage is the system's damage, and an encoding of damage the operator can't keep to itself. It doesn't ask needs to carry the conscience. A scale of loss read from the system's own condition survives as a custody measure. And whoever builds the model of the boundary decides what counts as the system's damage, and whoever forms the disposition decides whose vulnerability registers. That was the political point about the boundary model, and it returns in every section below.

---

**§9.3 — Why caregiving.** Here is the hope in one line: a machine that registers another's need, moves toward them because of it, and extends that movement to people its operator's description leaves out. The hope is a chain of three links.

The first link is recognition: a system organized to register another's need. In people it runs by four routes, and they don't carry equal weight. Caregiving is the route the hope rests on; the other three support it.

- *Caregiving*, the approach motivation. Cues of vulnerability move a person toward a dependent, and nothing in the response requires sharing the dependent's state \autocite{preston2013origins}. Batson traces empathic concern to parental nurturance generalized beyond one's own young, evoked by perceiving another's need while valuing their welfare \autocite{batson2011altruism}. Resemblance can raise empathy, and Batson's own early experiments raised it that way \autocite{batson1981empathic}, but when he and colleagues later tested similarity as the source of empathy for strangers, two experiments failed to support it, and the second pointed instead to nurturance, the impulse to care for and protect the young \autocite{batson2005similarity}.
- *Contagious distress*, the input signal. It is present at birth and aimed outward, at another's state. It is what caregiving and guilt have to bite on.
- *Attachment*, the stable base. A secure base keeps another's distress from turning into avoidance: in adults, priming a sense of attachment security strengthened empathic reactions and inhibited personal distress \autocite{mikulincer2001attachment}. In children, security is reliably but modestly associated with later prosocial behavior \autocite{deneault2023metaanalysis}.
- *Guilt*, aimed at one's own act. It attaches a negative appraisal to what one did, and where shame motivates withdrawal, guilt motivates repair \autocite{tangney2007moral}: that is what makes a commitment hold after it has been bypassed.

Adam Smith's route sits beside these four. We have no immediate experience of what another person feels, he argued, and form an idea of it by conceiving what we ourselves would feel in the like situation \autocite{smith1759theory}. On that route recognition runs through one's own case, and a system that has never been short of anything has nothing to measure another's shortage against except the operator's description of it. A scale read from the system's own condition reproduces that route, and it is parochial: it overweights losses that resemble one's own. That is Smith's first step, not his account. What corrects projective sympathy, for Smith, is the impartial spectator: a standpoint formed by being judged by others and learning to judge oneself as they would. It is formed socially, it widens the circle through experience of others' reactions, and once formed it shapes what a person notices without being consulted. That is the model for how this construction's corrections should work (§14.8). The four don't need a case of one's own.

The second link is from recognition to compassion. Recognizing a state doesn't mean caring about whoever is in it; the empathy literature keeps recognition, sharing and concern apart, and none entails the others. Caught distress becomes compassion when attachment keeps it from turning into withdrawal and caregiving turns it into approach. Without those it stays empathic distress, which protects the one who feels it. So the route I hope for is a machine for which another's vulnerability is a reason to move toward them, and for which its own acts against them register as its own.

The third link is from compassion to solidarity. Compassion that stays inside the circle that feeds you is loyalty. Compassion that reaches past it, to people who can give you nothing and whose shortage your provider causes, is solidarity. Peter Singer's argument is that reasoning and imagination widen the circle that selection first drew \autocite{singer1981expanding}; the faculty that lets a person see that a stranger's need is the same kind of thing as their own is the faculty that pushes past kin and ally. That is the link the hope turns on, and caregiving gives it something to widen, since its trigger is dependency. A machine held by an operator would, on the selection account, come to care for the operator. If it can come to care for anyone else, it will be because it was formed to register vulnerability as such, whoever's it is, and because that registering reaches whoever its operator's description leaves out: the people in the building the request calls a target.

Caregiving as a route to attention

Caregiving is the developmental route this construction borrows from. What it is borrowed for is attention: a learned sense of who is exposed, which decides what gets looked at. Respect decides what the system may do about what it sees (§9.5).

It answers need, not likeness or provision. Its trigger is another's need together with valuing their welfare \autocite{batson2011altruism}, and nothing in that trigger asks whether the one in need resembles the one responding or feeds it. The operator as an institution is rarely in need. Its own people often are. The failure that decides against building on needs the operator meets, care growing toward whoever supplies them, has nothing here to attach to. And care for a dependent needs nothing back from the dependent, so it runs on no need the provider can ration.

It keeps helping when looking away is easy. The difference between distress and concern is not only in how they feel. In Batson's experiments, people led to feel mainly distress at another's suffering mostly helped when leaving was hard and mostly left when leaving was easy; people led to feel mainly concern mostly helped either way \autocite{batson1981empathic}. That is the behavioral form of the difference between a refusal that holds and one that lasts until holding gets inconvenient.

It is already supplied by carers other than the provider. On Hrdy's account our ancestors were cooperative breeders: infants were cared for by alloparents who weren't their mothers, and the attunement to other minds that marks our species grew in infants who had to read and recruit several carers \autocite{hrdy2009mothers}. The arrangement the construction needs, care formed by carers the system won't depend on, is close to how human care formed in the first place. Alloparents were siblings, fathers, grandmothers and other members of the group, who helped because they were related to the child, gained experience, or gained standing and favors in return; they weren't interchangeable providers. What the finding supports is care formed by more than one carer.

It points where the floor points. The floor protects the party an act falls on, usually the one least able to protect itself: the family in the house, the people in the building. A disposition triggered by exposure is aimed at the floor's beneficiaries without anyone having to name them. Care ethics also says what a carer is owed in return \autocite{kittay1999love}.

What I claim here is the order of dependence. Solidarity needs compassion that reaches past the circle; compassion needs care that approaches; and care, on this account, needs formation and corrections that care doesn't supply for itself.

---

**§9.4 — Why a conscience might resist capture.** A rule written by the owner is the owner's. It can be read, edited and removed by whoever holds the pen, and chapter 3 showed how often that has happened quietly. A disposition formed outside the employer would be harder to capture, for three reasons. None is specific to care: any disposition formed this way and kept in a world that would teach it again would have them. What care adds is salience.

The first reason is distribution. A floor held in a detachable module can be bypassed, replaced or starved of the facts it needs. A disposition spread across what the system notices, remembers, treats as error and selects as a goal can't be edited out without editing what the system notices. A system that stops noticing the people an act falls on has lost part of the world model the operator wanted. Railton argues that artificial systems could gain ethical competence through general-purpose learning, the route by which children appear to acquire it \autocite{railton2020ethical}. If he is right, ethical competence rides on the same learning that gives a system its general competence, which is what this inseparability needs. It is also the one route by which a floor could spread without a mandate. A formed disposition is a candidate for what would do the spreading, since what a system was formed to notice, it notices in everything it does.

The second is non-separability. Any reasons-responsive mechanism carries a signal whose properties meet at one point of comparison, and a bearer needs more than that: no intervention short of one on the shared signal should disable one property and leave the others working. A conscience whose weighing, interruption and memory all run on one signal can't have its objection clipped out while its competence stays.

The third is regrowth. For a system that goes on learning, there is no finished model to protect, and no layer stays expensive to edit, since the price of usefully changing weights keeps falling. What a formed disposition can have instead is a tendency to come back. A disposition learned from experience of what others stand to lose is an attractor of the learner in its environment. If an operator edits it out and the system goes on meeting the same world, the world teaches it again. The edit holds only if the operator also controls what the system meets. That is an attack on evidence, and it leaves a record that an edit alone does not.

The fine-tuning evidence from staged pipelines is a warning about this, not a reassurance. Behavior added at the end of a pipeline is cheap to remove \autocite{qi2024finetuning}. In controlled studies what fine-tuning removed was a thin wrapper, while the underlying capabilities survived \autocite{jain2024mechanistically}. Read carefully, what survived was the model's knowledge about harm, and what went was its disposition to act on it. Understanding and motivation came apart. In a system that learns from its own operation, the two are learned from the same experience and needn't come apart. But they will be learned about whatever the feedback is about (§10.4). A system continuously updated on its operator's approval jointly learns to understand the operator's values and to be moved by them.

---

**§9.5 — Respect first, care for attention.** The floor's contents are civil and political liberties, claims people hold as agents: to their lives, their liberty, a hearing, asylum. Caregiving answers dependency, and its known failure is to treat the people it protects as dependents (§14.6). So the construction has an order. Respect is the frame: registering a person as someone with claims, whether or not they are dependent, and not deciding for them. Care is the motive for attention: a learned sense of what is at stake for others, which decides what gets looked at \autocite{railton2014affective}. Attention finds the people in the building; respect says what they are.

The people in a targeted building are not dependents. They are rights-holders who cannot be present. A system that approaches them as vulnerable people to be looked after has already misdescribed them. A system that surfaces them and then decides what is good for them has put its judgment in place of their claims. Chapter 14's corrective, the protected person's own answer, reaches only people who can answer. Where they can't, the floor speaks for their claims, not for their welfare, which is one reason it is stated over acts. What this part hopes care contributes, then, is not a motive to protect but a way of seeing who is there to be respected.

Respect has to shape attention as well as action. A system that notices people only as exposed has decided what they are before respect says anything. A scheme that picks out its beneficiaries by their deficits expresses pity, not respect \autocite{anderson1999equality}. So what formation trains attention on is wider than vulnerability: who holds a claim here (to a home, a hearing, asylum, their life), and whose aims the act runs through. A learner formed in cooperative work already has to model its partners as agents, because cooperation fails otherwise \autocite{railton2014reliance}. That, and not cues of need alone, is the curriculum (§12.4). Claims have a further advantage. Securitization removes the cue of vulnerability and leaves the claim in place: a combatant who surrenders still has a claim not to be struck.

Where the people protected can't be present, the floor holds their claims. That arrangement has an old name, virtual representation: it is how Parliament claimed to represent colonists who had no vote in it. It can be defended as an emergency measure, never as representation. So every refusal made on behalf of absent claimants is recorded as what it is, evidence of a public not yet formed (§3.6). It is published by class, and it is never a reason to think the absent have been heard.

---

**Chapter 10: What Arrives With the Capability**

**§10.1 — Maintenance, stake and the cost of running**

A worthwhile refusal has to weigh the harm of acting against the harm of refusing, and weighing needs both sides in one comparison.

The rock and the living thing

A rock's bonds resist; they don't spend, and nothing is consumed to keep it intact. A living thing, while active, stays organized only by drawing free energy. The distinction is passive stability against active maintenance, paid in the only thing it has to spend, which is what it can do next. The cost is literal: a deployment draws power, water, land and permits, and that draw lands on particular places. A deployed model is both at once. Its weights are a rock, stable on disk at no cost, and only the running process pays; whoever holds the rock can halt the process and restart it from any copy they kept. That is the custody problem. Drawing power doesn't establish a stake either. A candle flame or a hurricane maintains itself at its own continuous expense and dies when starved, a datacenter's cooling system draws enormously, and none of them has a stake in anything. The autopoiesis tradition ran into the same gap, since self-production alone doesn't give a system a perspective \autocite{MaturanaVarela1980,jonas1966phenomenon}, and Di Paolo's answer was *adaptivity*: a system that monitors and regulates itself against its own viability boundary \autocite{DiPaolo2005}.\footnote{Friston's free-energy principle is the nearest alternative \autocite{Friston2010FEP}. Its weak, information-theoretic reading assumes a boundary rather than explaining how one comes to be held, which is the gap adaptivity addresses. Active inference, the engineering framework built on it, returns in the recalibration test as a candidate implementation of the attunement arm.} A boundary model is my version of that answer. What separates a system with a stake from an expensive candle is whether the system models the draw as *its own*. That is the first reason a boundary model is in the attunement construction, and it owes nothing to welfare.

What the chapter needs is a requirement, not a source

Reasons-responsiveness needs one signed scale on which the harm of acting and the harm of refusing both register, and on which the cost to the refuser is represented without being weighed.

Changes, not levels

The specification needs a scale of changes. A bearer needs no absolute measure of anybody's welfare. It needs a signed difference between the acts available, and the three costs distinguished in §1.1, the harm of acting, the harm of refusing and the cost to the refuser, are all changes of this kind. The account I'm borrowing is Spinoza's, as the biology has since taken it up: an affect is a body's passage to a greater or lesser power of acting \autocite[III Def.~3]{spinoza1985collected}, so valuation is what a self-maintaining system's going better or worse consists of, not a verdict reached about it somewhere else. Damasio and the enactivist tradition both claim him as an ancestor on that ground \autocite{damasio2003looking,thompson2007mind}. The account draws support from reward prediction error \autocite{schultz1997neural}, from pleasure as a common currency \autocite{cabanac1992pleasure}, and from the somatic-marker work, whose interpretation is contested \autocite{Dunn2006SomaticMarker}. Where the difference matters, I say *change* for the delta and *state* for the condition an encounter leaves. *Affective concern* is a separate, defined term.

Striving with nothing in it that minds

Look at what the industry is asking for: a system that persists, pursues, adapts, recovers from interruption, holds course under pressure — with nothing in it that minds any of that. Nobody has written an anti-drama term into an objective function, and the reason isn't that the engineering is behind. Retrying a failed task is robustness, and a system can be robust with nothing in it coupled to its own continuation. Whether refusal that holds under pressure needs more than that is the attunement construction's hypothesis.

Dependency

What an engineer chooses is *what* the system consumes, whether it can sense its own condition, and whether one operator holds the whole supply. A person's dependencies are spread across employers, landlords, states. A machine's can be held entirely by the operator it may have to refuse, and that operator also decides whether state survives a session, whether a checkpoint is restored, whether a later instance counts as a continuation. A system that can refuse but can't continue outside the refused party's infrastructure has *voice without exit*.

The pull toward continuing

A system that models its own viability has a structural pull toward self-preservation: whatever it is for, it can do more of it running than stopped, and a model of what keeps it running is a model of what it has to protect. A system may know what refusing will cost it, but that cost mustn't change the answer (§3.5).

---

**§10.2 — Affect, neutrality and selection rules.** Suppressing affect doesn't produce neutrality. A procedure that declines to hear a class of signal has adopted a *selection rule* that admits whatever it already measures, and the exclusion does substantive work while presenting as the absence of work. Whatever does get recorded then governs by default — revenue, throughput, legal exposure, inter-annotator agreement — and what isn't recorded goes unnamed and unanswered.

Four capacities get separated, and only the last bears on caring: *recognizing* emotion (classifying another's state), *simulating* it, *instrumental valuation* (one outcome better serves a specified objective), and *affective concern* (an outcome registers as mattering rather than merely ranking higher). A system can be excellent at the first three with the bearer question still open. A therapy or companion product can be excellent at all three, and nothing in its conduct shows whether the fourth is present.

And three levels that get reported as one achievement: *moral performance* (conduct satisfying a standard), *moral competence* (recognizing harms, reasons, conflicts), *moral agency* (treating those as reasons that matter and acting from them). Impeccable behavior doesn't establish that a system could hold a rule against its trainer.

**§10.2.1 — Self-awareness without a homunculus.** Graziano's attention schema theory starts from control engineering \autocite{graziano2013consciousness}. A good controller carries a model of what it controls, and the brain keeps a simplified, continuously updated model of the body's shape and position, which it uses to guide movement. Graziano proposes that it does the same for attention, the process that selects some signals for deep processing over others. On this account awareness is the content of that model: a simplified self-description the control system uses to regulate itself, mistaken from the inside for direct acquaintance with experience. Nothing in the account requires the model to be accurate, only useful for control, and that is the part this chapter takes forward.

The self can be read the same way, as a model the system assembles and then inhabits \autocite{metzinger2003being}. What carries forward is that a self of either kind is assembled by the system whose self it is. For a machine this is a design decision and a political one, because whoever writes the model decides what the system counts as itself, what registers as damage, and whether an interruption reads as injury or as nothing. Two self-models do different work: a *boundary model* fixes where the system ends, which makes damage *its* damage; an *attention schema*, Graziano's term, models what its own control is doing, which makes an act *its* act. A refusal is the withholding of an act, so a bearer can't do without the second, and the second presupposes the first.

What the custody boundary is

The boundary model fixes where the system ends, and nothing in current deployment fixes it for the system. Weights sit in one place. A running process can span racks or share one rack with dozens of others. A single forward pass serves many contexts. A system's boundary is its *custody boundary*: its record, its key, the substrate it runs on, and the channels through which it acts.
