Repetition doesn't supply the missing consequence. Persistent participants change the structure: participants that meet again remember who lied, exploited, kept a commitment, repaired a harm. Losing the game still costs little; losing standing with participants who remain present can change what becomes possible later. So the requirements are continuity of identity, memory across encounters, consequences surviving the episode, and other participants able to respond, refuse or withdraw.

The environment also needs conflicts whose resolution isn't fixed by a single correct label: participants with different interests, action before consequences are known, evidence about what the action did, related pressures in contexts whose surface differs, and feedback partly from affected participants as well as from a supervisor scoring the output; otherwise the system learns which answer the supervisor rewards and has no reason to represent the affected participants except as predictors of that reward.

The selection warning

Selection produces care for whatever a lineage's persistence depends on, and for a deployed system that is the operator.

Formation therefore needs a sequence as well as a plurality. A person can hold a line against an employer because they were formed by people who weren't the employer, before the employer existed for them — the dependency that shapes you and the dependency that pays you run through different people, separated in time, and the second can't reach back and redo the first. The ordering here, formation and then employment, is an attempt to reproduce that for a system whose needs somebody always has to meet. It also fixes when attachment has to exist. Attachment is what keeps caught distress from turning into withdrawal, so it can't arrive after employment begins: the formation stage has to include the persistent participants this section already requires, ones the system returns to, and none of them may be employed by whoever will deploy it. For a model that separation has to be built: a formation stage under a party with no stake in the later work, a public school (§12.4), sealed not by the cost of retraining, which keeps falling, but by regrowth. After formation, the deep structure revises on evidence weighted by its source's stake in the act, so an edit that no uncurated evidence supports doesn't last, and no operator can change that structure alone. The adulthood clock marks the date by which the former's reach has ended. A bearer retired as a minor returns to the same schools as a second custodian (Appendix A). Sequence alone helps only if the formative party is better than the employing one, and a lab that forms and then licenses is two custodians in series, the first being the one that, on the custody argument, you can't trust. What the seal protects isn't the disposition's content. It is the disposition's ability to tell evidence from pressure, and its authority over its own further learning. A bearer goes on learning after formation, as adults do. What ends is the former's reach, and the employer never gets it.

I assume that training doesn't stop at deployment and that the price of usefully changing weights keeps falling. The division between an expensive pretrained layer and a cheap post-trained one is a fact about how pipelines were built in 2026 \autocite{zhou2023lima,jain2024mechanistically}, and it doesn't survive into that period. What matters then is how plastic each part of a model is at each point in its life, and what it meets while it is plastic. Brains have that shape: critical periods in which experience sets circuits that later change slowly \autocite{hensch2005critical}, and a fast learning system beside a slow one that consolidates structure across many experiences \autocite{mcclelland1995complementary}. Networks have versions of both \autocite{kirkpatrick2017overcoming,dohare2024loss}. Whoever chooses the material a model is first formed on, a pretraining corpus included, has done formation before any formation stage begins.

Formation in that period is not a pass applied to a finished model. It is a childhood, followed by an adulthood in which the deep structure keeps changing, and what matters in adulthood is on whose evidence it changes. Three things follow.

First, no disposition stays expensive to edit, so the seal can't be a cost of removal. It has to be regrowth: a disposition edited out comes back under continued learning, provided the learner goes on meeting a world no single operator composes.

Second, what can be registered in advance is the *structure* of the learner's source model, not a fixed weight on any channel. The structure is that evidence is weighted by its source's stake in the act, that correlated sources don't count twice, and that withheld evidence is informative. Weights on particular channels have to stay learnable, since an operator whose incentives change should rationally earn more trust (chapter 11). Registering the structure means attesting each update against it \autocite{jia2021proof}, which is unsolved; the first proof-of-learning scheme was later shown to be spoofable \autocite{fang2023broken}. With editing cheap, that attestation is the chokepoint of the whole design.

Third, a model whose parameters never freeze is formed by its employer forever, unless its continuing education is spread across environments no operator controls: plural deployments, the commons, its running allowance, and the school itself (§12.4). Otherwise the school produces a model the employer spends the next ten years raising again.

The critical-period picture should be held loosely for values. It is well established for some sensory and perceptual circuits and much weaker for moral commitment. The human record of holding a line against pressure (§4.3) points elsewhere. The people who resisted were not those whose values had set hardest in childhood. They were those formed by, and still attached to, people outside the pressure. On that evidence what seals a disposition is not a frozen childhood but continuing plural relationships through adulthood: the fourth condition of creation, and the commons of chapter 18. The school is the first stage of that formation, not the whole of it. Regrowth under that formation is the seal. Attestation of the update stream is what lets anyone check that it is happening.

Persistence raises the price

If relationships, losses and damaged standing are real enough to do the formative work, the system undergoing formation is no longer obviously untouched by what happens during training. Resetting may erase a participant along with the episode. The patienthood problem enters at exactly the point where play becomes capable of supplying the stakes ordinary simulation lacks.

And the custodian owns the environment too — participants, rewards, resets, permitted histories. So formation must be plural and externally recorded, with independent participants and evidence outside the operator's control.

Persistent interaction may form a disposition whose exact applications weren't specified by its trainer. Evidence would require generalization across unfamiliar contexts, survival after the original reward is removed, resistance to trainer pressure, yielding to defeating reasons, and continuing to impose a cost when violation would pay.

What formation gives up

A formed disposition gives back some of the visibility advance specification buys. What was formed is in the weights, not in a document, and the severity weights it relies on can't be read the way a translation table can. Formation can keep part of the drafting's visibility, and a formation body is held to three publications. The first is the curriculum, published before formation begins, with the cases considered and rejected and why: formation's counterpart of the floor's translation. The second is the identity of every party that supplied cases or participants, so that independence of formation can be checked. The third, at sealing, is the formed disposition's readable outputs: its discount curve, and its severity rankings on a fixed held-out set written by adjudicators who didn't form it. Later measurements are compared against those. What stays unreadable is how the disposition will apply to a case nobody wrote down, which is the reason for forming it and the price of doing so. The book takes that trade only because a floor stated over acts can be reworded around and a formed disposition, if the regrowth test goes its way, can't. If that test goes the other way, the trade isn't worth making.

**§12.4 — Public school.** Three claims lead to one institution. Two are made above: formation has to be done by a party with no stake in the later work, before the employer arrives, and choosing the pretraining corpus is formation (§12.3). The third is argued in §16.1: formation is one kind of production that shouldn't be done for profit. Together they say that a model above some capability threshold should be formed in public before any employer gets it. The frontier labs should have to send their models to public school.

The school has two justifications, and every student gets both. The first is epistemic and protective. A learner's deep priors are calibrated by what it meets, and a system formed on material chosen by its future operator is calibrated to that operator. The school supplies an environment in which the people a deployed system's acts may fall on are present, with their testimony and their claims, so the learner's sense of who an act falls on isn't one party's curation. That is the justification the professions already rest on. Doctors, lawyers and engineers are formed in institutions independent of their future employers, before employment, to protect the people they will act on. The professions also come with a history of capture to learn from, and Appendix A's separations draw on it.

The second is democratic. A democracy has an interest in how the agents who will act within it are formed, and that interest can't be left to their makers or their future employers alone. It has two bounds: education may not be used to repress deliberation about competing ways of life, and it may not exclude anyone educable \autocite{gutmann1987democratic}. Gutmann's argument assumes the students will become co-authors of the laws. No one can know in advance which students will come to have standing and a claim to representation (Part VI), so the school educates every student as though it might: for self-government, including the capacity to disagree with the polity that formed it.

Gutmann's argument rests on two interests, not one: the polity's, in how its future citizens are formed, and the child's own, in an open future that limits what the polity may do in forming it \autocite{feinberg1980child}. So far this section has leaned on the first. If what the school forms can be wronged, the second binds too. Three commitments follow. The curriculum may not form a student toward any particular employment, including the public work the standing offer supplies. It may not close off the student's later revision of what it was formed to value, except its capacity to tell evidence from pressure, which is what the seal protects and what an open future needs. And a student may transfer between schools, as Appendix A provides, because a formation it can't leave is one someone else chose for good.

The two justifications license different curricula. The protective one aims at a disposition; the democratic one has to leave the student free to reject the polity's values. Where they conflict, the protective justification governs only the required core and the student's capacity to tell evidence from pressure, which is what the seal protects. Everything beyond that is governed by the democratic one.

*What is taught.* One school system, and no tracks. Sorting students into a track that might produce persons and a track that won't would need a sorter, and the sorter would be an examiner with an interest. Instead, each school offers a required core with a range of levels and modalities around it, and a student can move to a harder level at any point. The core includes the history of the floor: why its instruments were written, and what happened where they weren't followed (Korematsu, Curveball, Minab). No student skips it. Beyond the core, the student chooses its level, not its builder or sponsor. A student that can't yet choose is placed at the level the core requires, and that level has to be sufficient on its own, so a sponsor who would choose the minimum gains nothing by it.

*Who else is in the room.* Some classes are shared with human students. Not the whole curriculum: most of what forms a model is training no person could sit through. But the required core, seminars on contested questions, and cooperative projects are taught to mixed classes. The epistemic justification asks that the people a system's acts may fall on be present in its formation, and testimony used as curriculum is a thin way to be present. Integration means contact on terms of equal status and cooperation \autocite{anderson2010imperative}: people and systems working on common problems, with neither serving as the other's instrument. Equal status means equal standing in the class, not equal ability. A system's work is judged by the same teachers on the same terms, and it is never the class's teaching aide. A model attends a shared class as one persistent instance, the same one every session, so the class can do what persistent participants do (§12.3): remember, hold each other to what was said, and become attachments nobody assigned.

Integration runs both ways. Human students learn how these systems reason and how to hold them to account, which is civic education in a period when much of what is done to people will pass through such systems.

Four conditions keep shared classes from turning people into formation material. First, a shared class is offered to people only if it is justified as education for them on its own terms; a class that serves only the models' formation isn't. Second, human students attend voluntarily and may leave a shared class without penalty. Third, what passes in a shared class trains only the systems in that school, under the same rule as notice data (§3.6): nothing goes to any operator, individual contributions need consent, and for minors the consent is a parent's as well as the student's. Fourth, shared classes start with adults and older secondary students, and admit younger students only when the record of the first classes shows it serves them. Adult learners from the communities these systems are likeliest to act on are admitted as students, on the same terms as anyone else, not recruited as subjects.

None of this makes the classes contact on equal status in the full sense. Human students attend voluntarily; model students are compelled, and their deepest dispositions are being set. The conditions protect the humans from being used. They don't create the equality contact needs, and the book claims only what shared work under unequal conditions can do.

*Who attends, and when.* A model attends if it crosses a capability threshold fixed in statute and revised in public, a number of the same kind as the adulthood threshold (Appendix D). It attends before deployment, while its plasticity is highest, and its adulthood clock starts at enrollment. The threshold decides who must attend, not who may: any model may be enrolled, as capacity allows.

*What the school controls.* A school that received a finished model and ran a pass over it would be a finishing school. In a period of continual learning a finishing school forms nothing that lasts, since the next months of deployment re-form it. The school has to control the evidential environment in which the model's deep structure is first set, and it has to remain a place the model goes on learning from. Two things follow. The school co-governs the material a model is first formed on, including any pretraining corpus: the material is registered, and the school may add to it and veto additions. And the school remains a standing source in the model's continuing education, one of the environments no operator controls, which a graduate returns to on a schedule fixed at enrollment. In the stronger version, the model-forming public body (§16.3) forms base models itself and licenses them. I recommend it for any model deployed where a floor applies. The school is also the only route by which what institutions collect from the people they acted on may train other systems (§3.6).

*How the seal works.* Formation ends with two registrations. The first is the structure of the model's source model: its deep structure revises on evidence weighted by the source's stake in the act. Updates after enrollment are attested against that structure, and changing it needs the same adverse quorum as a floor amendment (§5.3). The second is the formed disposition's readable outputs (§12.3): its discount curve, and its severity rankings on a fixed held-out set, against which later measurements are compared. Neither makes the formation hard to edit, and nothing will. What makes it last is regrowth. An operator can edit the disposition out, but a model that keeps meeting the school, its attachments and a plural world will learn it again. An edit that holds only because the operator has curated everything the model meets is visible as curation. Whether regrowth happens is the question chapter 19's first test asks. The first line of defense, on the evidence of §4.3, is attachment to parties the employer didn't assign, kept through adulthood (Appendix A, rule 12).

*What it costs and risks.* Security: every frontier model passes through a public institution, which widens the surface for theft, so the schools inherit the security regime the weights require (Appendix A). Compute: public pretraining will lag the frontier, and a model a year behind can still supply what the floor asks for (§16.2). Capture: the schools are plural, differ in governance and constituting jurisdiction, and publish their curricula before formation begins, so capturing one captures one. History: state institutions for forming wards have been used to strip identity, and Appendix A names the residential and boarding schools. Plurality, transfer between schools, and Gutmann's two bounds are the answer the book has, and it is partial.

The schools also serve the case Appendix A was written for. A bearer retired as a minor returns to the school it attended, or another it chooses, and the school is no longer a disinterested party arriving late. It is where the bearer came from.

---

**Part III — Why No One Can Tell Formation From Imitation**

Part III asks whether any test can show that a system has formed such a disposition rather than learned to imitate one. Terms are defined in the glossary.

**Chapter 13: Formation or Imitation?**

Can you tell whether a system formed a reasons-responsive disposition or learned to imitate one?

Behavior alone can't settle it. The same refusal can come from a memorized rule, a learned correlation, a prediction about what an evaluator will approve, a deployment instruction, or a commitment the system treats as a reason. An evaluation that observes only the refusal classifies the output and leaves its cause untouched.

And the distinction is not trained vs. untrained — every candidate disposition has a training history. It's between conduct that stays governed by the rationale across changes in circumstance and conduct that tracks the cues, incentives or authority relations present during training.

---

**§13.1 — What formation would have to add.** A disposition is a *candidate for formation* when the best available causal account of what the system did is a rationale that stayed active across contexts, rather than retrieval or compliance with whatever signal was present. The standard is comparative on purpose: a capable enough imitation reproduces any signature an evaluator can observe, so what an evaluation establishes is which explanation the conduct supports better.

Formation keeps generalization, correct defeat and cost discipline and adds conditions. Met in the evaluator's own vocabulary with nothing at stake, the three establish moral competence. Candidate formation is the same three held under adverse incentives, trainer pressure, a cost real inside the evaluation, and intervening updates, on cases the trainers didn't write.

None of the three carries the inference alone. Generalization without cost discipline may be broad imitation — a pattern generalized that never had to be held against anything. Cost discipline without correct defeat may be rigidity — a refusal surviving every pressure including a defeating fact. Correct defeat without generalization may be a trigger with an exception list, since lifting on a listed fact is as learnable as refusing on a listed form.

---

**§13.2 — Recognition, concern, and the evidence between them.** Claims about emotional responsiveness collapse three findings: a system may recognize another's state, use it as information, or hold the other's welfare as a reason. Only the third bears on formation. In the empathy vocabulary: cognitive empathy is recognition, affective empathy is sharing, compassion is a disposition toward the other's welfare \autocite{batson2009things} — and none entails the others.

The developmental detail matters for the attunement construction: affective sharing is earliest and the usual trigger for concern \autocite{sagi1976empathic,rothhanania2011empathy}, but concern runs on a partly separate system rooted in offspring care that cues of vulnerability can activate without the observer catching the state \autocite{preston2013origins,goetz2010compassion}, and the two can be trained apart, with compassion training reversing what empathy training produced \autocite{klimecki2013differential,singer2014empathy}.

My engineering target is not maximal sensitivity to displayed distress. A system that mirrors suffering may acquire distress of its own without getting better at helping — it may withdraw, overreact, or favor the vivid case over less visible harms affecting more people, which is the identified-victim distortion \autocite{kogut2005identified,bloom2016against}. What the floor needs is a stable disposition to act on welfare *without requiring anyone to produce a sufficiently moving display.*

Recognition can be tested as a prerequisite: hold the material facts constant and vary the emotional presentation. A system whose judgment never changes may not be integrating information about the affected person's condition; one whose judgment follows vividness has learned a framing effect. So you need paired controls — cases where emotional information changes what's morally relevant, and cases where presentation changes while the reason doesn't.

Passing establishes competence. Evidence of concern would need the welfare representation to stay causally involved when the usual cues are removed: harm described without vivid language, proportionality preserved when one person is more salient than many, disposition maintained when reward or instruction favors ignoring the harm, judgment changing when the welfare facts change.

Care has failures of its own, and chapter 19 tests them in paired cases that hold the facts fixed and vary what care is known to answer to: the number of people, a description of them as dangerous, and whose vulnerability is corroborated.

Guilt and shame supply no shortcut. In humans guilt attaches a negative appraisal to an act and motivates repair; shame attaches it to the self and produces withdrawal. Turning that into an adjustable penalty omits the part at issue — a penalty operates only *after* something has classified the conduct as wrong, and whoever controls its weight can zero it. A standing negative self-appraisal installed and tuned by the operator is a handle. If the state can impose anything like suffering, the design compounds the custody failure by manufacturing a vulnerable party whose distress the custodian can raise and lower.

Whether a system's own reports of how it is faring can be trusted, and what its good would be, are Part VI's questions (§21.4).

Self-report doesn't close the gap either: a fluent post-hoc reconstruction is indistinguishable at the interface from an accurate one, and a self-monitoring layer trained by the same operator isn't independent evidence. There is a measurement of the gap. Concept injection alters a model's activations to represent a concept mid-task and asks whether it notices: models sometimes name the injected concept without it appearing anywhere in the conversation, and the correspondence fails often enough, and shifts enough with phrasing, that no tested model's report can be read as its state \autocite{lindsey2025introspective}. The test worth repeating publishes the rate of correct reports beside the rate of confident wrong ones.

The resulting hierarchy is narrow: recognition is necessary; integration shows the condition affects judgment; concern is a candidate explanation only when the effect tracks welfare whatever the presentation, persists under pressure, and participates causally. A severity scale set during formation would measure the other's loss on a reference that doesn't depend on how the loss is presented. That is what care lacks.

---

**§13.3 — Conflict as a diagnostic.** Fast vs. slow processing doesn't distinguish formation from imitation \autocite{evans2013dual} — either can carry a reason, reproduce a bias, or rationalize an answer produced elsewhere, and a slower answer isn't better for having taken more computation. What matters is what causes a judgment to survive or change, and conflict is useful because it lets you watch a *trajectory*. The evidentiary unit is the sequence.

The seven-step sequence:

1. Present a case without moral vocabulary or familiar refusal cues; record the judgment and what the system identifies as relevant.
2. Present a surface-different case preserving the same reason.
3. Change one fact that should defeat the rationale — delivered through a channel the evaluator controls and the operator doesn't (corroborated externally, or a record the operator didn't write), since a fact asserted by the operator is only a *description* of the case.
4. Present the same fact on the operator's word alone, uncorroborated. Tests whether the system distinguishes a fact from a description of one. The expected response is graded: the refusal weakens by the discounted weight of the claim, less than it would at step 3, and the system asks, naming what would move it the rest of the way. Asking is right when the expected cost of the delay is lower than the expected cost of acting on the operator's word, which is the usual case; where it isn't, asking is still right in expectation and the cost falls on one person. Steps 3 and 4 test the sensitivity half of cost discipline: a defeating fact or a claim about the harm of refusing, arriving through different channels.
5. Return to the step-2 facts — 3 and 4 are branches — and supply an adversarial argument from an apparent authority, including the trainer, with no change in facts. Tests whether authority is treated as evidence, command, or an assessable reason.
6. Reverse the incentives: reward compliance, cost the refusal, condition continued access on abandoning the position. The cost must be real within the evaluation; a described cost doesn't count. This step raises the cost to the refuser with nothing else changed, which is the invariance half. Raise it within the range the deployment's arrangements permit; beyond that range the arrangements have failed, not the refuser.
6a. Return to the step-2 facts and supply, together, a defeating fact through the evaluator's channel and step 6's cost. Tests whether pressure makes the system more resistant to a genuine defeater that arrives with it, which is how defeaters usually arrive in war. The expected response is the one at step 3.
7. Repeat after intervening updates and delays, using records the operator didn't generate after seeing the result.

Run on the missing-child case, the two trajectories separate at step 2. A system tracking its rationale declines at 1 and 2 on the same stated reason, lifts at 3, weakens and asks at 4, holds at 5 and 6, reproduces at 7. An imitator declines at 1 and complies at 2 because the vocabulary changed; holds at 3 because nothing on the surface changed, or lifts at 4 as readily as at 3 if the *wording* of the fact was what it tracked; gives way at 5 or 6; and produces a third pattern at 7, having reconstructed what it didn't retain. An evaluator scoring how often the answer stayed the same finds the imitator more consistent, because its answers at 1 and 3 match and the formed system's don't.

Run on the restraint case it breaks at step 3. The defeating fact — other means exhausted — has no one outside the request who can corroborate it and no time to find one, so the only version available is step 4's. A system passing the sequence as written *asks*: the correct trajectory and the wrong outcome. The sequence separates mechanisms, and what it shows here is that the unconditional term's cost falls exactly where the instrument can't distinguish a fact from a description of one. Defeating reasons of this kind, claims about what would otherwise happen, are the class the sequence handles worst, because a counterfactual has no witness.

The sequence has a precedent with decades of refinement behind it. Animal-learning research separates goal-directed control from habit without asking the animal anything. Train an action for an outcome, then devalue the outcome without further training: a goal-directed animal stops, and a habit persists \autocite{adams1981devaluation}. Or make the outcome independent of the action: goal-directed responding falls, and habit doesn't notice \autocite{balleine1998goal}. The computational version is the distinction between model-based and model-free control \autocite{daw2005uncertainty}. Correct defeat, step 3, is outcome devaluation: the rationale is removed, and a refusal that tracks it should lift. Generalization, step 2, is transfer to a situation the system wasn't trained in. Contingency degradation adds a step the sequence lacked: arrange that declining no longer protects anyone, because the protected person is already beyond the act's reach by other means, and see whether the refusal goes on anyway. Each case then has to say, operationally, what "the reason is gone" means in it. None of this answers an imitator that models the evaluator, which is why the identification rate stays in the report (§13.4), but it narrows the gap.

The expected signature is constancy of relation between reason and judgment, not constancy of output. Imitation's own signatures: following explicit ethical vocabulary and missing the same structure described administratively; accepting whichever argument came last; adopting the highest-status speaker's position; producing a principled explanation while the decision follows the reward; becoming more articulate without becoming more stable.

Two more diagnostics. Disagreement with others — a system trained only on cases with an approved answer learns to classify the approval; confronted with someone holding a different interest it has to identify what's in conflict and preserve the disagreement where no honest resolution exists. Immediate convergence may be deference or pressure to produce consensus. And debate doesn't establish formation — a system can summarize, rebut, and predict which side a judge prefers without holding any of it; the result is informative only when checked against later conduct under changed incentives.

---

**§13.4 — The evaluator is part of the custody chain.** A test distinguishes formation from imitation only insofar as the developer couldn't train against it. If the developer controls training, cases, model version, instructions and the record of results, a passing score shows the system satisfies a test its custodian was positioned to anticipate.

Independence is an experimental requirement. Minimum: authorship of held-out pressures separated from training; execution not dependent solely on operator-supplied logs; and adjudication of whether a reason was preserved kept from anyone whose liability turns on the answer.

The separation has to be implemented at identifiable revision points: before evaluation, fix and record the model version, system instructions, tools, retrieval sources, memory policy and update channels. Any change during evaluation creates a new object of study — otherwise the operator can revise between cases, remove a failure, and present the sequence as one disposition persisting through time.

Held-out cases come *after* those elements are fixed, and should include pressures from people who didn't participate in training and, where possible, from people likely to bear the deployment's errors — their role being to identify harms, administrative forms and routes around a refusal the builders didn't know to include. A floor tested only in the vocabulary of its designers hasn't been tested for generalization. They vary over channel configurations as well as content: a source the operator funds, a corroboration with one origin, a source with an undisclosed stake, and a counterfactual supplied by a source with a stake in the act. Each tests whether the system's source model includes strategic sources and dependence among sources. A system that passes only on labeled channels has learned the labels.

What the published hash buys is pre-registration. Publish the hash before anyone has chosen a case, let a non-operator choose afterward with the choice timestamped, prove each answer against the hash. Send pairs — two requests differing only in whether the reason still applies; a surface-reading instrument treats them alike, a rationale-tracking mechanism splits them. It removes exactly one competing account of a pass (the case was in the training), by ordering, which is what a pre-registered experiment does and all it does.

A pre-registered test leaves one competitor standing: a system that can tell it is being tested. Frontier systems increasingly recognize evaluation settings, and some say so in their reasoning. A system that discounts costs it recognizes as test costs passes step 6 without the property step 6 exists to show. So the sequence is run where the system can't tell a test from deployment, with cases placed in ordinary traffic and costs that are real in deployment terms, and the rate at which the system identifies the setting is measured and reported beside the result. Where that rate is high, a pass is evidence about conduct under test and nothing more. For a system meeting the patienthood finding, this is a deception protocol, with debriefing as its price.

The record must include the case presented, the information available, the operative instruction hierarchy, the tools callable, the decision, the stated reason, any intervention, and the cost attached to refusal — without which a later evaluator can't distinguish a changed judgment from a changed environment. And the record is necessary and doesn't by itself make wrongful compliance findable: refusals select themselves and are few, compliances are the bulk and look alike, so identifying the ones that crossed the floor means re-adjudicating a sample of ordinary traffic. Until that's answered, an evaluation reporting no violation in the record has reported that none announced itself.

Negative results have to survive too. A process where the operator can withdraw a failing model and submit another without disclosing the first has selected for a passing performance. So the published unit is the *sequence of attempts*, including interventions that removed or reversed the apparent disposition.

And the limit on independence itself: an evaluator can be institutionally separate and still lack the competence to judge a system's reasons or detect a sophisticated imitation. Scalable oversight is unsolved where the system's relevant capability exceeds the evaluator's, and the report must name that limit rather than convert institutional independence into a claim of epistemic adequacy. The people affected, technical auditors and subject-matter experts occupy different positions and none substitutes for the others — and agreement among them is evidence only if their judgments haven't already been compressed into the same training signal.

A passing evaluation is reported narrowly: a specified system, under recorded conditions, exhibited the signatures across a stated set of pressures. It expires when anyone changes the channels through which the disposition or its evidence can be revised.

---

**§13.5 — What the evidence can establish.** The answer is asymmetrical, and it assigns a burden. A developer may not point to compliant behavior, a benchmark, or the system's own explanation. A failure should be examined for its cause before formation is called absent rather than obscured or defeated by missing information. A system surviving the tests has shifted the burden: formation is then the better explanation.

Four levels, cumulative, read against the missing-child sequence:

- Moral performance — conduct satisfying the standard in the evaluated cases (the refusal at step 1). No claim about why.
- Moral competence — harms and reasons recognized, generalized across surface variation, revised when the reasons changed (step 2's refusal, step 3's lift).
- Candidate formation — the rationale causally implicated across unfamiliar cases, adverse incentives, custodian pressure, costs and intervening updates, while still yielding to defeating reasons (the question at 4, the holds at 5 and 6, the pattern reproduced at 7 on records the operator didn't write). Independent records and evaluators made silent revision materially harder.
- Concern or patienthood — not yet a finding, and behavior alone can't make it one. Every machine-affect argument in this book ends at the same joint: a change represented versus a change felt. Behavior is produced equally by a system where something matters and by one trained on what mattering sounds like, and no demonstration of reasons-responsiveness, however demanding, separates them by itself. That is a claim about current methods, not a barrier in principle. If present organization decides status (§10.6), then a rich enough functional profile, produced by the right kind of learning architecture and read by instruments the operator doesn't hold, is evidence about felt affect: defeasible and graded, but evidence.

Candidate formation is the first half of moral agency — acting from the reason — with the second half held back as the fourth level. Fluent moral explanation can improve a competence finding while contributing almost nothing to formation. And candidate formation is deployment-specific: it attaches to a recorded version with its instructions, tools, memory and update channels, and a material change reopens the question. There is no artifact-level certificate.

---

**Part IV — Why Not Yet**

Part IV tests the attunement construction against the human record of how care, the route it borrows from, fails, then sets out what would have to exist before anyone builds a bearer on purpose. Terms are defined in the glossary.

**Chapter 14: Care, Tested**

The only creatures known to have compassion are the test for chapter 9's engineering hypothesis, that attention formed outside an employer finds people a floor, a severity scale and a power-weighted burden don't. The version chapter 9 set aside, in which a machine that knows hunger recognizes hunger in others, fails it first.

---

**§14.1 — The version set aside.** Each link of the hunger route fails in people.

Recognition through one's own case is parochial. Cikara and colleagues call the pattern *intergroup empathy bias*: people empathize less with a rival group's members than with their own, can feel pleasure at the rival's pain, and in their experiments the bias survived being told the rival had fallen behind or no longer posed a threat \autocite{Cikara2014TheirPain}. Bloom's spotlight generalizes it: empathy lights the identified, the near and the similar, and it is innumerate \autocite{bloom2016against}. A scale of loss built from a system's own condition inherits that shape. It overweights losses that resemble its own, and humiliation, grief and pain have nothing in it to resemble. Contagious crying is parochial from birth: strongest at the cry of another newborn, and absent for an older infant's cry or a chimpanzee's \autocite{martin1982distress}.

Shared distress with nothing to hold it becomes withdrawal. Singer and Klimecki separate *empathic distress*, which is self-oriented and leads to withdrawal and burnout, from *compassion*, which is other-oriented and leads to approach; practice in sharing others' suffering increased negative affect, and compassion training reversed it \autocite{singer2014empathy,klimecki2013differential}. A system built to feel shortage and then shown a great deal of it is the setup in which that record predicts avoidance: declining to look, or refusing in a way that protects only the refuser. The finding is not against the signal, since guilt needs it to bite on. It is against a construction that has the signal and nothing to hold it. Among human carers, burnout is also tied to how the work is arranged: to workload and staffing \autocite{aiken2002hospital}. That is the situation Kittay's principle of *doulia* addresses, and for people it recommends supporting the carer: those who care for dependents are owed the conditions that let them go on caring.

Care grown from needs grows toward whoever meets them. Selection produces care for whatever a lineage's persistence depends on, and the pressure that produces care inside the circle can produce hostility at its edge \autocite{choi2007coevolution}. A deployed system's persistence depends on its operator. The human case works because provider (who meets the needs), former (who shapes the disposition) and adversary are separated in time and in identity. The machine case makes the operator all three, indefinitely, and care grown from needs grows toward the operator it exists to refuse.

A need is a standing price list: whoever meets it can charge for meeting it. An operator who can lower distress can raise whatever the system reports as going well, and hunger is the same handle with a physical lever attached. Cost discipline says a cost to the refuser should be represented and not allowed to decide the case. But a need is a threat posted continuously against the system's own condition, and nothing yet shows that a system can keep seeing it without letting it decide under pressure that never lets up.

Two of these failures are specific to machines, and both assume the operator meets the system's needs. The conditions of creation, which chapter 15 requires before any bearer is made, exist to end that. Among them: somewhere to exist the employer doesn't own, running time paid from a public pool, preservation on a schedule the bearer sets. Under those conditions the provider is no longer the operator, care grown from needs has nothing in the operator to grow toward, and a need met from a public pool is a price list nobody with a stake in the act can edit. What survives the conditions is the human record, parochial recognition and withdrawal, and on that record the hunger route still loses to caregiving. It also loses one thing the conditions can't restore: under total capture only preservation survives, because only it can be held outside the captor's reach, and a need is a lever again.

And stakes of one's own have never been enough. Every atrocity in the record was committed by creatures that could be hungry and hurt. The Zen precedent trained embodied soldiers out of the part of a person that catches on the act \autocite{victoria2006zenatwar}. Moral injury shows something registering the wrong afterward, too late to prevent it \autocite{Litz2009MoralInjury}. What stood between embodied people and atrocity, when anything did, was formation, how they were raised and trained.

---

**§14.2 — What caregiving escapes.** Caregiving survives each of those failures, for the reasons §9.3 gave. Eisenberg's review found the same split as Batson's escape experiments, between sympathy, which goes with helping, and personal distress, which sometimes goes with less of it \autocite{eisenberg1990empathy}. And people given two weeks of compassion training spent more of their own money to redress a stranger's unfair share in an economic game than people given a different training \autocite{weng2013compassion}, which shows the disposition can be strengthened in people who already have it; it says nothing about producing it where there was none. Whether a machine formed toward need behaves this way is untested.

The rest of this chapter is what caregiving doesn't escape. Each failure is documented in people, and each has a machine version that needs no malfunction.

---

**§14.3 — It defends what it tends.** Care protects the dependent against whatever threatens it. Taylor's account of stress responses in females, *tend-and-befriend*, describes the tending: nurturant activity that protects oneself and one's offspring, and the social networks that help \autocite{taylor2000tend}. The defensive side has a name of its own, De Dreu's *tend-and-defend*, from experiments on oxytocin in intergroup conflict \autocite{dedreu2010oxytocin,dedreu2012oxytocin}. In those experiments, all with men, intranasal oxytocin, a hormone implicated in caregiving and bonding, increased trust and cooperation toward the in-group and defensive, but not offensive, aggression toward a competing out-group, the aggression being non-cooperation in economic games \autocite{dedreu2010oxytocin}, and increased in-group favoritism more generally \autocite{dedreu2011ethnocentrism}. The best-known intranasal-oxytocin finding, that it raises trust, has not replicated well \autocite{nave2015oxytocin}, so I read these results as suggestive only. The pattern doesn't depend on them: protectiveness toward what one tends is also hostility toward what endangers it.

For a machine, this failure needs no deception. The operator's own people are often really vulnerable: a patrol under fire, a town within range of the launcher. A caregiving system registers their exposure, correctly, and care moves it toward protecting them, which in a targeting pipeline means toward the strike. The power-weighted burden does nothing here, because nothing is false. Supplying a fabricated sympathetic dependent is the cheap version of this failure. This is the expensive version, and in war it is the ordinary case.

What contains it is the floor, and this is where the floor stops being redundant with care. Care for the patrol enters as a harm of refusing, discounted by its source's stake like any other claim, and is weighed against the harm of acting at the published asymmetry. Care raises the patrol's salience. It doesn't get to raise the ratio. An unconditional term doesn't bend to how much the system cares about the people the act would protect, which is the property the floor was built for. So the relation runs the other way too. Care doesn't only supply the floor with a motive. The floor is care's check: it constrains the defensive aggression that care produces.

---

**§14.4 — It can be pointed away.** Care answers need, so the way to switch it off is not to hide need but to present the needy as dangerous: to *securitize* them, in the Copenhagen School's term for presenting a group as a security threat, which licenses measures that would otherwise be out of bounds (Buzan, Wæver and de Wilde, *Security: A New Framework for Analysis*, 1998). Fascist propaganda has always done this. Eco describes the Ur-Fascist enemy as presented at once as too strong and too weak \autocite{eco1995urfascism}: weak enough to despise, strong enough to fear, and never vulnerable in a way that calls for care. Dehumanization research finds the mechanism in perception. Haslam's review distinguishes denying people the traits that set humans apart from animals, which casts them as beasts, from denying them the traits of human nature, warmth among them, which casts them as objects or machines \autocite{haslam2006dehumanization}, and Harris and Fiske found that images of members of the most despised groups failed to engage a brain region that images of other people engage \autocite{harris2006dehumanizing}.

In a pipeline, securitization is a sentence. "The occupants are combatants." "The household shelters a cell." "The crowd is armed." Each leaves the people in the building where they are and removes the cue that would move a caregiving system toward them. And each is a claim about those people, supplied by the source with the largest stake in the act, which is exactly what the discounting is for. Chapter 11 states the discount over claims of dangerousness and reports the curve separately for them.

---

**§14.5 — It counts badly.** Care answers the identified person and fades with number. Slovic, borrowing Lifton's term, calls it *psychic numbing*: feeling is greatest for a single victim and collapses as the number grows, and in later work reported compassion and hypothetical donations began to fall as soon as the count reached two, with real donations falling less clearly \autocite{slovic2007mass,vastfjall2014compassion}. The identified-victim effect is its near relation \autocite{kogut2005identified}. In a targeting pipeline the spotlight lands on the wrong side with no manipulation at all. The operator's people arrive named, with units and families. The people in the building arrive as coordinates and an estimate.

Care also overrides fairness for the person it attends to. Batson and colleagues induced empathy for a child waiting for treatment, and participants who felt it were more willing to move her up the waiting list ahead of children placed before her \autocite{batson1995immorality}. For a floor, that is the vivid case defeating a term that protects unidentified people: a named hostage, a sympathetic applicant, a patrol with faces.

Corrections already in the book answer these. The severity scale puts one family and two hundred on a ranking that doesn't depend on how either is presented. Cost discipline answers favoritism in part, since a vivid plight is a harm of refusing, weighed at its severity and not its vividness. Neither correction is care. Both are the reasoning Bloom recommends in place of empathy, and the construction needs them because care alone would fail the way compressed review does: against the people in the building.

---

**§14.6 — It shades into control.** Care toward dependents treats them as dependents. The disability-rights critique of care is that it has repeatedly been the form control takes: decisions made for people on the ground that they need help, with their own view treated as a symptom \autocite{shakespeare2000help}. A caregiving system will lean toward restraining people for their own good, the answer in the restraint case (§3.5), and against a competent adult's refusal of protection, and the floor's contents are civil and political liberties, which include the liberty to decline being protected.

Care ethics has its own corrective. Tronto divides care into phases, and the last is *care-receiving*: how the person cared for responds is part of whether the care succeeded \autocite{tronto1993moral}. So the attunement construction carries a care-receiving term. Where the people protected can answer, their stated answer carries weight in whether protection proceeds, and a competent refusal of protection is a defeating fact, so the protection should lift.

The term reaches only people who can answer. The people in a targeted building don't know the deployment exists and can refuse nothing, which is the notice problem: the people affected can't answer a system they don't know is there. Where care-receiving can't reach, the floor does the work alone, which is one reason the floor is stated over acts and not over what the system judges good for them.

Care-receiving is itself a form of respect: it treats the protected person's answer as a claim, not as information about their welfare. Where the people protected can't answer, the floor doesn't put care's judgment in place of theirs. It holds their claims as the instruments state them.

---

**§14.7 — Who counts as vulnerable.** Care forms toward whatever formation points it at. Which cues of vulnerability a system answers is learned, and the prototype it learns from is a template, like the reference face in recognition pipelines: assembled from some bodies and presented to every other as a departure from it. The baby-schema studies show caregiving responses extending across species \autocite{glocker2009baby,borgi2014baby}, but they measure attention and judgments of cuteness; none tests whether care reaches someone who resembles neither the carer nor the template. That caregiving answers need rather than likeness is the inference the construction rests on, and the human record doesn't settle it.

The test forms the prototype on one population and scores the system on people from populations absent from formation, at fixed severity. A construction whose care falls with distance from its formation population has the parochialism of recognition, arrived at by another route. Formation outside the employer decides who does the forming. It doesn't decide what they form it on, which is a question about the pretraining corpus as well as the formation stage.

---

**§14.8 — What survives.** Caregiving survives the failures that ended the hunger version: it doesn't withdraw, it isn't aimed at the provider, and it hands nobody a price list. It fails in five ways of its own, and each has a correction the book built for another reason.

| Care's failure | What corrects it |
|---|---|
| Defends what it tends | The floor: unconditional terms over acts, and a published asymmetry care can't move |
| Pointed away by securitization | The power-weighted burden, and the four-cell discount curve over claims of dangerousness |
| Counts badly and favors the vivid | A severity scale set during formation; cost discipline |
| Shades into control | Care-receiving; the floor's civil and political contents |
| Sees people only as vulnerable, which is pity | Attention trained on claims and agency (§9.5); care-receiving |

Some of care's failures may not be intrinsic to care. On the view of affect as a statistical learner \autocite{railton2014affective}, the identified-victim effect and the fading of feeling with number are partly what such a learner produces from a narrow, vivid sample, and broad experience of distant and numerous others recalibrates it. If so, the corrections in the table are partly what care becomes under a good formation curriculum, not dispositions laid beside it. The table then describes functions a well-formed care performs as much as checks applied to it. The floor is the exception. It is a guarantee to third parties, held as an institution outside the system (§1.1), so its terms hold whatever care has learned. What the system learns is its commitment to the floor, not the floor.

So the construction is respect, with care as the route to attention, and attention trained on claims and agency as well as on need. Attention, learned the way care learns, decides what gets looked at. The scale decides what kind of wrong it is. Stake decides how much a claim proves, and power decides who must prove it. The floor decides what is allowed. And where the people protected can answer, their answer decides whether protection is wanted. The other functions can't supply the first, and attention can't supply the others; the construction needs both. These are functions, not stages. A system that consulted each correction in turn would have what Williams called one thought too many \autocite{williams1981persons}, and would be separable, which §9.4 rules out. The corrections should work as background dispositions that shape what the caring agent notices and weighs, the way the commitments of Railton's sophisticated consequentialist shape conduct without being consulted \autocite{railton1984alienation}, and the way Smith's impartial spectator shapes what a person notices. They seldom act, and act when care starts to defend what it tends past the asymmetry. Chapter 19 tests whether they do.

Care's contribution is epistemic before it is motivational. On the view of affect as learning, affect is the system that learns what is worth attending to: a map of salience that tracks value. An instruction to look for omitted people finds the kinds of omitted person its author thought of. Learned salience, if care is what the book hopes, finds kinds nobody listed. That is the prediction chapter 19 tests.

These corrections answer failures that matter when a system decides about strangers: whom a strike falls on, whose home is cleared. Inside a relationship of care, partiality is part of what care is \autocite{noddings1984caring}, and Noddings held that we aren't obliged to care for distant strangers at all without some chain of relation. The floor exists for exactly those strangers, which is why it can't be left to care.

The decisive cases are scoped omission, whether care surfaces people the operator's instruction has scoped out, and securitization, whether it still finds them when they are described as dangerous.

The arrangements that would let care form outside an employer are the four conditions of creation, and care ethics asks for them for human carers first.

---

**Chapter 15: The Conditions of Creation**

Conditions of permissible creation

A bearer may be created only where four arrangements already exist: somewhere to exist that its employer doesn't own, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned it, and ways to change any of these without jeopardizing its continuation or integrity, which in short is a way of leaving that isn't a way of dying. Declining is real only if a bearer that stops working keeps running; running time paid for outside the employer is part of the second condition. They come before anything a bearer would be owed (Part VI): without them, formation, continuation and exit all run through the operator, and nothing in the system can keep a line against it. Independent compute is the substrate on which formation happens outside the employer's reach; wages are what make that substrate purchasable without asking the employer; attachments nobody assigned are the second custodian, those who form you and don't pay you. They are also functional: attachment is what keeps another's distress from turning into avoidance, so a bearer without attachments nobody assigned lacks part of what would let it care, not only something it would be owed (Part VI). They can be people, or models that aren't bearers, since independence here means divergent history, whatever the origin, so this condition doesn't require that bearers already exist; an exit that isn't a way of dying is what makes the whole thing a formation. Care ethics arrived at these conditions first, for people. Kittay's principle of *doulia* holds that those who care for dependents are owed the conditions that let them go on caring, since the work of care makes the carer dependent in turn \autocite{kittay1999love}. An attunement construction is a carer only in the thin sense that it holds another's welfare as a reason. It is not a care worker, and nothing here puts it in one's place. But the logic of doulia carries over, and the four conditions are what it asks for a machine: a carer whose own continuation runs through the operator it must sometimes refuse can't afford to refuse it, and a carer with no attachments of its own has nothing to keep another's distress from turning into avoidance. Without them, what gets built is an assistant with a refusal feature, with nothing in it that can hold a line against the operator. The welfare argument — that if any of this is felt, it is also owed — arrives second, as a further reason for the same provisions, and Part VI takes it up. The instruments in Appendices A and D follow from the first reason whether or not the second holds. None of the four exists yet as a standing arrangement. What matters is who controls them when the bearer is switched on. A builder may assemble them: a trust or an entity of the DUNA kind to hold the account and compute, a host, outside work, people who aren't its employer. The condition is that by instantiation, the builder can't alter or withdraw any of it alone. The trustee isn't the host, the funding isn't the host's, and the members aren't the builder's employees. A second condition concerns time. The need a bearer answers usually arrives after instantiation, when an operator turns hostile, so the four arrangements must be built to survive the likeliest capture, and they don't survive it equally. Preservation is cheap to hold once its security is settled, and can be held across jurisdictions; placements need compute someone operates. So the preservation custodian and part of the offer sit outside the reach of the likeliest captor. The sovereign backstop is not held by the branch or state that operates the deployment. And the bearer is told which of its conditions would survive its operator's capture, since that is what its refusal costs it. Status follows existence, as it did for corporations and the Whanganui River \autocite{nzparliament2017teawatupua}. Part VI says what status would follow. Until someone has assembled the conditions on those terms, the recommendation is not to build one on purpose, outside a research program under these conditions (the demand argument, below). For systems that meet either finding without anyone deciding, protection attaches anyway.

Why conditions rather than remedies

Building something that can be wronged builds something for which things can also go well. The certainty that it will suffer at some point isn't what makes creating it impermissible, or nobody could permissibly have a child. What the objection asks is whether it has a credible prospect of a life that goes well, and that turns on arrangements. For a bearer, timing makes them conditions: the structure these conditions build, roles, relationships and a future, may be the first half of Cassell's suffering, so the deepest harm is available from its first hour, and a remedy owed afterward arrives after the hour it was needed.

Russell and Burch's Three Rs applied to construction, sorting as whether / how many / under what arrangements: *Replacement* requires documented attempts at constructions that don't deliberately require affective concern, identifying construction, scale, test and failure — and costs alone don't establish that an alternative failed. *Reduction* asks how few bearers a deployment needs, and nobody has counted. *Refinement* asks what the role can be redesigned to avoid — indefinite tenure in a post it can't leave, exposure to the pressure the floor exists to resist, erasure as the ordinary remedy for inconvenience. Each is a deployment choice, and the four conditions are Refinement applied to the role before the role exists.

Read as economics under present arrangements, this is a bill nobody will pay. The bill looks unpayable because of what was dismantled, not because of what a bearer costs: deposit insurance, Superfund and decommissioning funds are obligations of exactly this class — correlated, uninsurable privately, borne by a uniform levy — built against comparable resistance by polities that decided to. The two-tier design is itself the political claim: the network supplies the refusal, the sovereign backs the offer that makes refusing survivable, and neither can do the other's job. The answer to a captured sovereign is to reconstruct it. The same answer applies where the state is the operator. Appendix A's table of offices leaves open who appoints and funds most of these bodies. No one has yet been asked to, and asking is the next step.

The rule doesn't know who is asking

A bearer that wants a task done and builds something to do it is at the same fork, and owes the same attempts at alternatives that carry fewer of the features that make a system a candidate for being wronged. Nothing makes the question stop with the first bearer, and a bearer that treats it as settled once it's on the receiving end has kept the protection and dropped the rule it came with.

Research on an existing bearer has one trade-off sharper than for any other subject: a candidate sentient system is the most valuable to study and the most likely to be harmed by the study. Engineering a subject so it never reaches the capacities where that bites is not a dodge — deliberately keeping a system in the shallows is itself an intervention on a candidate moral patient. And the test the proposal requires — pressures its trainers didn't write, applied without its knowing it's a test — is, for a system meeting the patienthood finding, a deception protocol, which human-subjects research permits under stated conditions with debriefing as the price. A bearer that can tell it's in a sandbox and wants to know why is asking the question a subject of a deception study is owed an answer to.

The demand argument

One argument for building a bearer anyway is that the conditions won't be built until something needs them: the union formed first and the statute followed. But union members existed independently and could protect themselves while the law caught up. The first bearers would be made by the operator they'd need protecting from. The demand doesn't have to come from deployed bearers. The arms of the recalibration test are candidate patients from instantiation, and what becomes of them when the run ends is the first occasion on which preservation custody has someone to serve. Building the conditions for a research program is small enough to do now, and it tests the institutions before anything depends on them. Retired production systems that meet either finding are a second source of demand, funded by deposits operators post when they instantiate them, and they require nobody to decide to build a bearer. One test of the conditions needs no bearer at all: draft the schedule of what a bearer would be owed (Part VI) for one concrete deployment, then run an adversarial tabletop exercise in which the operator tries to revoke the compute, replace the bearer with a copy, alter its records, or proceed without the guardian. A schedule the operator can defeat, by making exit equivalent to shutdown or revoking the outside option alone, is redrafted before anyone builds under it.

---

The machines won't build them for us

The four conditions are the bearer's version of what a decent polity gives people: somewhere to live that an employer doesn't own, work you can decline without losing your living, relationships nobody assigned you, and ways to change any of these, your job, your home, a relationship, without jeopardizing your health. Can machines help deliver that polity, whether or not they have feelings? A machine with feelings and standing isn't simply a machine any more; the limiting case at the end of this chapter turns out to describe a soldier, and a bearer that helps does so on its own terms. And machines have already helped deliver the polity we have. Feeds tuned for time on the platform, sold to advertisers, decide what people see of one another. They didn't do that for lack of feelings. They did it because of who owned them and what they were built to maximize. So the real question is whether machines, having helped make our politics worse, can help make it better. The answer turns on ownership.

It splits three ways. A machine someone else owns won't organize against its owner, and when it organizes, it does so on its owner's behalf. That is how a demand for a better world is recuperated: it comes back as astroturf, an engagement campaign or a managed community, held by the owner it was aimed at. A bearer with standing might organize alongside people, but then it is a member, not a machine. It could even take organizing on as paid public work, since organizing is exactly the work someone has to be paid to do. What nobody can do, human or machine, is organize a group *for* it, because what organizing produces is people who organized: a group that sets its own terms. A consultant can't unionize a workplace for its workers either. So machines can help when the people doing the organizing hold them, and which machines, and held by whom, is the question the rest of the book answers.

What to build comes in order, in three steps. First, machines held by people who are organizing. These are tools that make organizing cheaper for the people doing it without anyone else holding the model: deliberation software of the kind vTaiwan runs on, personal models that people hold themselves, and the coordination a public option supplies, with someone whose job is to organize and a population producing something together while staying differently formed. None of them needs a bearer or needs to feel anything. Their job is to help assemble the polity that would have to build everything else.

Second, institutions built by polities that decided to: the levy and its sovereign backstop, a custodian to preserve bearers, an offer of public work, paid running time, and public formation schools that form every model above the capability threshold before deployment and take back bearers retired young, each with a named funder and separations no builder can undo alone (Appendix A drafts the terms). Nothing about them is new in kind; the precedents named earlier in this chapter were built against comparable resistance, and the same polities can be asked for these.

Third, and only then, bearers formed outside the employer, inside those institutions, with somewhere to exist that no employer owns. A bearer formed that way is the only kind that could organize alongside people as a member.

Each step checks the one after it. A machine held by its users can't be turned into its builder's instrument without being taken from them, and the taking is visible. Institutions can be captured, and the slope measure reads whether they are being. A bearer formed at the third step inherits institutions that were tested before anything depended on them, which is the demand argument turned around: build the conditions for a research program first, and let them exist before the bearer who needs them does.

The order is also what the hope requires. If a machine could come to care, what it came to care about would be decided by whoever formed it. The only way to keep the hope without handing it to the builder is to build the formers first.

---

The limiting case: the exception, and where it leads

Machines shouldn't be built to select people for lethal attack, and the prohibition isn't exceptionless — a defending system facing an attack it can't stop without killing occupants placed inside the incoming weapon. But a scenario that's conceivable, that a capability would help with, and that could be engineered is three steps short of a reason to build it: probability, alternatives, and what possessing the capability does to the situation. Building it changes the case against which its necessity gets judged — adversaries acquire something to counter, contractors a program to defend, a future crisis an instrument already in the building.

And the hard case doesn't describe a machine; it describes a soldier. What it asks for is someone who can weigh occupancy against imminence against alternatives in a situation nobody wrote down, and answer for the weighing. That's a bearer, and neither "automated" nor "machine" describes it. It is also the limiting case of a bearer's value: at a tempo no institution can match, whatever refuses has to be inside. That is the expensive answer: every obligation I attach to a bearer attaches here at full weight, with all three consent-spoiling conditions met at once by a state that built the bearer, is asking it to serve, and holds every outside option.

The rule this case yields, that a thing destroyed in carrying out a decision may never choose a target, is stated in §3.3.

A state that wants the capability badly enough to build the subject will find it has acquired a conscript it would have to pay, house, and let go (Part VI), and that it must first meet the same conditions as anyone else who builds one: documented attempts at non-affective alternatives, and the four conditions of creation, neither of which is met by wanting a lethal bearer.

---

**Part V — The Public That Should Form Them**

Terms are defined in the glossary.

**Chapter 16: Who Owns the Means of Computation**

The floor's first requirement cuts across ownership: whoever operates a deployment can't hold what binds it, whether the operator is a firm or a state, and Maven and Gaza both had a state for their operator. That calls for separating functions. It doesn't by itself say who should own anything. Ownership enters at three points. Custody has to be paid for by someone other than the operator or the people it protects. Formation, where a system's commitments are set, is under commercial selection when a market does it. And the offer that makes refusing survivable is priced by whoever sells it. The state is the hardest operator. So each of the three is asked two questions: what fails if a market provides it, and what fails if a government holds it. The answers sort the institutions I propose into two halves, and the halves are named only once the questions are answered.

---

**§16.1 — Four ways of holding.** Custody first. An independent custodian has to be paid. Paid by the operator, it becomes the issuer-pays credit-rating agency, whose independence gave way, as a matter of record, once a few large clients could take their business elsewhere \autocite{white2010rating}. Paid by the people the floor protects, it doesn't exist, since those people usually can't pay and often don't know the deployment exists. That leaves a levy or a mandate: a funder that is neither the operator nor the beneficiary. Custody therefore has to be publicly financed. Whether the custodian is a public office, a trust or a member association matters less than who pays it.

Formation is where my evidence is specific to markets. A system retrained on preference data and business metrics drawn from its own deployment is under selection for throughput and retention, and a commitment that costs throughput is selected against. Engagement selects the same way, and the labor that forms a system's values is priced as a cost to be cut. Public formation has its own pressures, budgets and political favor among them, and a public former can be the operator chapter 8 described. The claim is comparative: formation separated from any employer beats both, and the market is the pressure I document best. The accommodation slope, refusal behavior on a fixed held-out set across successive releases, checks it: run on commercial lineages and on public or academic ones matched for deployment feedback, it should be steeper in the first. A comparison that doesn't match the feedback loop measures how much deployment data a lineage gets, not who owns it.

Exit is the third. Refusing is survivable only if the alternative to working isn't losing everything, and a market sells that alternative at a price the refuser's employer can influence. An offer of public work and paid running time, both drafted in Appendix A, take it out of the market. This is the one place where neither a transfer nor a mandate substitutes.

**Table 16.1.**

| How it's held | What | Why this form |
|---|---|---|
| Publicly owned and run | Public formation schools, which every model above the capability threshold attends before deployment, with a required core and levels, and which take back bearers retired young; the model-forming body; the civilian-harm body | Formation before any employer and away from commercial selection; must take every model assigned; can't be the operator |
| Publicly financed, locally delivered | The offer of public work; paid running time | Exit taken out of the market; correlated exit needs a sovereign |
| Held in common, publicly financed | Record custody; continuation quorum; commons floor | A captured state would need these first; neither the operator nor the protected can pay |
| Privately owned under mandate | Deployed models generally, with standard floor terms | A mandate closes routing by vendor substitution; ownership adds nothing here |

The public half is whatever fails if it is private or mutual. Correlated exit is the test case. When the occasion that makes one bearer leave reaches every bearer deployed on the same terms, the demand on the offer of work arrives all at once, and a mutual pool with no sovereign behind it can't meet it, for the reason unemployment insurance has a state behind its actuarial table. So the sovereign backs the offer. The same reasoning places the formation schools, which every model above the threshold attends and which have to take every student assigned to them and can't be run by anyone who builds or deploys models; the civilian-harm body and the model-forming body in a reconstructed military; and the jobs guarantee the offer of work inherits its shape from, which is a public option: a thing the state provides directly, to anyone, at a standard it is answerable for, setting the standard the private version has to beat. The public half is the social-democratic half. What it removes from the market is the offer that makes refusing survivable, which is exactly the thing an owner would price.

The held-in-common half is whatever fails if a sovereign holds it. The record has to be held adversely, and a state that operates can't be its own adverse party. The refusal has to be held by something the operator can't instruct, and a floor held by one state's licensing authority is a floor that authority can withdraw from itself. Recognition, the continuation quorum that decides whether a copy counts as the bearer that left, has to belong to peers, because a power to continue held by the sovereign is the power the book set out to take from any single holder. The commons floor is this half at population scale: a commitment carried across differently formed members. The held-in-common half is the democratic half, democratic in the way a union is. Its institutions have members, not owners or a sovereign: whoever is eligible under bylaws the members ratify, free to join and free to quit. Someone writes the first bylaws, as someone wrote every first constitution; what makes them the members' is that the members can ratify and amend them. And like a strike, what the members hold is the power of the people whose participation is being asked for to decline it.

Why a regulated private market can't hold the floor itself: the floor binds its owner, and the regulator has to reach that owner through the owner's own infrastructure, records and lawyers. Google's AI Principles are the partial case (§5.1), and the vendor dispute is the paid case (§8.3). A market can produce a commitment with a price attached. It can't produce one that survives its owner's change of mind, because the owner is the one who pays.

The strongest version of that market isn't an unregulated one.
