Nuclear power, pharmaceuticals and aviation are privately owned, closely regulated, and backed publicly where private insurance runs out. Reactor operators carry private liability insurance with an industry-wide pool behind it, and Congress is committed to consider relief beyond both \autocite{nrc2026nuclearinsurance}. Vaccine-injury claims go first to a federal no-fault program funded by an excise tax on each dose, built because Congress feared that lawsuits against manufacturers would cause vaccine shortages \autocite{crs2026vicp}. From 2001 to 2014 the federal government sold airlines war-risk insurance at pre-attack prices when private insurers withdrew \autocite{crs2014aviationwarrisk}. My own models are of this kind: the funds chapter 15 cites are backstops on private ownership, and the answer to capture given below is a plurality of institutions, not a state that owns the models. Regulated private ownership under a public backstop works where the harm the regulator exists to prevent is one the owner also loses by: a meltdown, a crash, an injured patient and the suit that follows. The backstop pays for accidents the owner was trying to avoid. A floor's occasion is the opposite case. The act a floor refuses is the one the owner wants done, and a refusal the owner can buy back isn't a floor. So I keep the backstop form where I use it, to fund the offer, the allowance and preservation, and make the ownership claim only where the owner is the adversary: the offer that makes refusing survivable comes out of the market, and the record, the refusal and the recognition of who continues are held by no owner at all. The claim underneath is that formation, where a system's values are set, is a kind of production that shouldn't be done for profit. It doesn't wait on the lineage comparison above. Some goods are properly governed by non-market norms because market norms are wrong for their value. Those norms favor exit, impersonality and satisfying whatever is wanted, and civic formation is the classic good they fit badly \autocite{anderson1993value}. The comparison is still worth running, to corroborate the claim and to check that a public former does better, but the argument doesn't rest on it. Taking exit out of the market and financing custody publicly would, by themselves, be close to what Esping-Andersen called decommodification \autocite{espingandersen1990three}, which is usually counted as social democracy. Taking formation out of the market goes a step further, but it is a step social democracies already took for their own children. Compulsory public schooling exists in most capitalist countries, and it rests on the claim that a polity can't leave the forming of the people who will act in it to their future employers (§12.4). So I call the arrangement social democratic: public where a market fails, and held in common where a government fails, over the record, the refusal and the recognition of who continues. A reader who counts taking any kind of production out of the market as socialism can use that word; nothing in the design changes. Whether the claim extends to computation in general is a larger argument, and this book doesn't make it.

---

**§16.2 — The captured-state objection.** The strongest objection to the public half is the one chapter 8 pressed hardest: public ownership hands the machinery to whoever captures the state. A floor under a sovereign is a floor that sovereign can remove, and history offers plenty of polities that built public institutions and then used them against their own people.

First, the separations. What an office can do is limited by what no office may hold together, and Appendix A writes those pairs down in advance. A captor who takes one office takes one power. Taking enough of them to defeat the floor is a series of visible acts, each against a separation somebody can point to.

Second, local administration with central funding: the jobs guarantee's design, with many separately capturable administrators and funding no one of them can withhold.

Third, the two-tier levy. A member-levied pool, held by an entity of the DUNA kind, meets ordinary movement between roles, and the sovereign backstop meets only the occasion everyone leaves at once. A captured sovereign can withdraw the backstop, but only openly, and it can't reach into the pool without reaching the members one at a time.

Fourth, what is held in common is what a captor wants first. The record, the refusal and the recognition of who continues are the three things a captured state would need to silence a bearer, and none of them is the state's to seize. It can reach a chartered entity and the members it can locate. It can't reach a record lodged adversely in several jurisdictions, or a quorum whose members it doesn't employ, without acts that leave marks. Each makes capture slower and easier to see.

One objection none of the four meets is competence. Public bodies have built capable technology and have also been outpaced; the public arsenals §16.3 invokes include Springfield Armory, closed in 1968 partly on grounds of cost. Public and academic language models exist and trail the frontier. A floor-capable model needn't be a frontier model, since what the floor asks for is reasons-responsiveness and a source model the operator didn't form, and a model a year behind the frontier can supply both.

---

**§16.3 — A reconstructed pipeline.** The hardest case is the test of the whole arrangement: public ownership inside a military, where the operator is the sovereign and tempo is a weapon. Targeting in war does push toward a single decider: someone has to be able to order an attack, keep plans secret, and act fast when the other side does. It doesn't push toward a single composer. The attunement construction's deployment condition concerns who supplies the evidence bearing on the floor, who forms the model, who holds the record and who reviews. None of those has to sit with the commander, and there are working precedents for pulling them apart.

What stays unified is command, operational secrecy and the capacity to act at tempo. What is held adversely is the evidence bearing on civilian harm, the reviewer denominator, and the record. The evidence is composed by a body outside the chain of command, with its own sources, including the protected-site coordinates humanitarian organizations already submit for deconfliction, received with standing. The denominator is staffed and funded by that body, so it can't be cut the way the Central Command team was. The 2022 civilian-harm action plan was a first attempt at the separation; its dismantling is an argument for making it harder to dismantle, not evidence that it can't exist.

Models are formed by a public body that doesn't operate them, in the tradition of the public arsenals that made American arms before the contractor era, and licensed into the military on terms that include adverse review and a written refusal right. A line one vendor held by contract, at a price, is then a standard term. In the stronger version of §12.4, the same body pretrains the base models the schools form.

Precedents show authority outside the executive working inside real wars. Coalition "red card" holders in NATO operations could veto targets involving their assets, and Germany's Bundestag must approve each armed deployment. Both have limits the design must carry: a red card protects the vetoing state's interests and assets, which may not include civilians, and parliamentary approval is given per mission and never reaches a target. Both are nonetheless candidates for the adverse party the review ratio presumes, and a red-card holder is the rare candidate positioned to act at tempo. What a bearer adds here is least, and §3.1 says what it is. It depends on the institutions for evidence and not for continuity: the deployment condition is met here by the same bodies whose presence narrows its role.

One cost is stated here, and it isn't fatal. A reconstructed military accepts some speed disadvantage against rivals that don't reconstruct; how much, and why it's worth paying, is an argument arms control has had before.

The civilian-harm body and the model-forming body are added to the offices.

---

**Chapter 17: Coordination, the Public, and the Political Economy**

Whether any of this gets built depends on who participates, whose judgments carry authority, and what funding rewards. Collaboration and consultation can improve decisions without giving anyone the power to stop one.

---

**§17.1 — Participation and its limits.** Citizen assemblies, public consultations and ethics boards can make a system better informed. The assemblies convened on AI have worked best when asked about a thing with a location, an operator and a consequence, such as facial recognition \autocite{adalovelaceinstitute2021biometrics}. Only a board can object on the record. DeepMind Health's outside panel was wound down in 2019 after its members questioned their access and their weight \autocite{fisher2019deepmind}, and nothing in the arrangement made ending it costly.

What none of them does

These mechanisms are usually described as aggregation instruments: they make public preference more legible, informed and actionable. Read that way, a perfectly deliberative, perfectly representative process that concludes a demographic should be surveilled, deported or imprisoned has produced a faultless output. That reading gets democracy wrong. Democracy is a way of doing collective inquiry, and its epistemic power comes from including the people who bear the consequences, and from channels through which they can make their problems public \autocite{dewey1927public,anderson2006epistemology}. A process that decides what is done to people who are outside it, or that closes the channels through which they could answer, isn't a perfect aggregation with a bad result. It is a failure of inquiry.

So the protections that matter are not protections against democracy. Enforceable rights that don't depend on being popular, a court willing to rule against a government holding a majority, limits whoever won the last election can't repeal, and standing for people affected without being enfranchised are part of what keeps the inquiry honest. They matter most where the boundary of the public excludes the people a decision falls on (§8.6).

---

**§17.2 — The political economy.** Absent pushback, capital recruits compute, data and talent wherever they already are, and concentration is the default result. Financial incentive mostly determines what development produces: the largest firms commit enormous resources aimed at operational efficiency, engagement and market dominance, and that drives technical progress \autocite{maslej2025index}. What it doesn't automatically produce is a system serving the people using it rather than the shareholders funding it — a recommender tuned to maximize time-on-platform has no built-in reason to prefer accurate content over inflammatory content \autocite{germano2026ranking}. None of that is a fixed property of the technology. It's a property of who is paying, and that makes the source of research funding a policy instrument, not an accounting detail.

**§17.2.1 — Concentration.** A handful of firms controls the datasets and infrastructure a competitive system needs, and a firm in that position is a gatekeeper whether it intends to be or not.

Two remedies that don't depend on antitrust litigation: *mandated access* — licensing a chokepoint dataset or infrastructure layer on published terms to any qualified competitor, which reaches the *position* rather than the conduct — and *public funding outside the dominant firms*, which lasts only where the money is standing. Export controls and prohibited-practices regulation address the narrower authoritarian-misuse case; the concern here is prior to that one: concentration itself, whatever any single firm does with it.

**§17.2.2 — A jobs guarantee.** Where automation displaces work, the standard response is retraining funded by whoever can be persuaded. The design I examine is more direct: the state as employer of last resort, hiring anyone who applies, where they live, at a living wage, with paid on-the-job training built into the position.

I examine it for two reasons. It's a public institution that has to survive a hostile administrator, which is the book's custody problem in another setting. And it is the exit the rest of the book depends on: chapter 22's standing offer inherits its infrastructure, and without it a bearer's only exits are an allowance and storage, a transfer of the kind §17.2.3 argues against.

The jobs guarantee I mean is the one argued in Tcherneva's *The Case for a Job Guarantee* \autocite{tcherneva2020case} and in Wray, Forstater and the Levy Institute's buffer-stock work. What that literature supplies is exit: a minimum the private employer must beat, or at least match, which makes the worker's threat to leave credible. What it has no reason to supply is voice — staying in the post and refusing the particular act — and the three conditions below are voice provisions bolted onto an exit instrument, added because this book asks the guarantee to survive a hostile administrator rather than a recession. A bearer needs both, explicitly, because the operator can defeat each alone: exit by keeping a runnable copy, voice by restoring a checkpoint.

The real objection: employer of last resort under a hostile administration is a *placement lever with a compliance condition attached*. Refuse the placement and lose the guarantee. Speak against the government that employs you and find the placement changes. Everything the book says about how a channel becomes an instrument of the thing it was built to check applies. The 2025 CFPB instance makes it concrete: the bureau draws funding from the Fed on the director's request, with no appropriation — an arrangement the Supreme Court had upheld the year before \autocite{cfpb2024cfsa} — and in February 2025 an acting director, removable at will, simply declined to make the request \autocite{kolodziej2025cfpb}. The agency was stopped from inside without a statute being amended or a court being asked.

My answer is a specification, because a specification can be audited. Three conditions: placements appealable to a body that didn't make them, on a record that survives the appeal (a decision leaving no artifact can't be reviewed); refusal rights that are real, whose cost the refuser can see and choose to bear (declining a placement without losing the guarantee is a refusal right; declining only by exiting the program isn't — someone with no way out has voice without exit); and auditors aren't the placers.

A fourth condition answers the history. A right to work has repeatedly been turned into a duty to work and used to discipline the poor \autocite{anderson2023hijacked}. A guarantee is an exit from one employer, not from employment, so it shades into workfare unless something lets a person leave the wage relation altogether. That something is social insurance, and the guarantee is never enacted without a statutory floor of it in the same bill (§17.2.3, Appendix A).

Federally funded, locally administered meets the first three, and the reason is redundancy: many separately capturable administrators mean no single point where a hostile executive flips the program, and capturing a thousand local authorities is more visible than capturing one federal one. Local administration is opt-in: a state or locality can't be required to run the program \autocite{printz1997}, and where one declines, a federal field office runs placement there. That also answers a hostile local authority. It cuts both ways — local administration is where a hostile local authority attaches its own conditions, and there the federal funder is the check. And one distinction has to be explicit, because the same words describe a different policy: devolution is also how a program is shrunk — hand down the obligation without the money and it can be starved locally while the center points at local administration. The checkable test: funding unconditional and centrally guaranteed while placement is local. If funding can be withheld from a jurisdiction, the redundancy argument is void and the structure has become the lever it was meant to prevent.

Appendix A gives the drafting. The design buys visibility, not immunity. A captured administration can stop paying certified placements or leave posts unfilled. Either is a visible breach of a statutory duty, and the remedy lies in the guarantee court. Repeal is an act of Congress on the record, which no captor can take quietly.

**§17.2.3 — A right to consume and a right to produce.** The competing design is basic income, and the choice is usually argued on forecasts about how much work machines will take. It doesn't have to be, and the forecast isn't the reason to prefer one.

The reason is coordination. Communities improve when people organize projects with each other, and organizing is work: someone has to find what a place needs, assemble people to do it, and keep the work going. A transfer creates no one whose job that is. A guarantee does, and it creates standing public infrastructure through which a population produces something together while its members stay differently formed, which is the coordination problem chapter 18 leaves open. The argument doesn't depend on a forecast. In the most abundant future anyone has imagined, people still organize, build and look after each other, and someone coordinates it. Looking after each other is the clearest case. Care work is work a guarantee should fund, at a standard the state answers for, and not work a transfer should leave to whatever the market will sell. Nothing in Part II is an argument for handing it to machines: the disposition is borrowed from caregiving because caregiving is the one route known to reach someone who can give nothing back, not because machines should do the work.

Nor does a guarantee give up exit. With indefinite tenure, a worker can always leave a private employer for the guarantee and stay as long as they like, and social insurance enacted with it (below) covers those who can't or won't work. On exit the combination is at least as good as a transfer; on coordination a transfer supplies nothing. The risk to avoid is a narrow transfer enacted as the whole settlement. In this book's terms that is recuperation: the demand comes back as a deposit, held by whoever sets the amount.

A jobs guarantee is a *public option*: a thing the state provides directly, to anyone, at a standard it's answerable for, sitting in the same market as the private version and setting the standard that version has to beat. A basic income is a *transfer*, and what it buys is whatever is for sale.

What employment carries besides money is *attachment*: colleagues, a schedule, a place, something produced that somebody needed — the last conditional on the placement being real work, which is a condition on the program. A transfer supplies the purchasing power and none of the rest.

The argument is about a *right to produce*, which a transfer doesn't supply and a guarantee does. A basic income is the right to consume, exercised in whatever market the concentration described earlier leaves standing.

One measure does work neither design can. Social insurance (income support, health coverage and housing assistance) reaches the people who can't work or choose not to, whom a guarantee by construction doesn't reach. So the guarantee's statute carries a floor of it: income support at a stated level for anyone not in a placement, without a work test, and health coverage that doesn't depend on employment. The floor needn't be a full program. But the two are enacted together, and they stand or fall together. The statute provides that if the floor is struck down or repealed, the guarantee's authority lapses with it (Appendix A), so that no court or later Congress can keep the placements and drop the way out.

---

**Chapter 18: A Commons of Refusers**

The commons floor, the fourth place a floor can live, is the democratic half of Part V, held by members and peers.

---

**§18.1 — A floor carried between bearers.** The commons floor is the commitment reproduced through what bearers teach one another and continue to expect.

A population does three things. It supplies evidence the operator didn't select — without which the operator selects all of it. It checks accommodation, needing that members moved differently — sufficient against accommodation one member underwent, insufficient against a blind spot they inherited together. And it may be the medium in which a bearer's capacity to be reopened is formed; the convergence test separates that from the first.

What reproduction costs: it's indifferent to what it reproduces. A population can carry a hierarchy more durably than any individual. And the four operations of §2.2 on what an institution leaves out belong primarily to *cultures*; a plurality doesn't oppose them by being plural.

A population is a polity

It has membership rules, exclusion, a quorum that can refuse, and accumulated reputation — a franchise, a border, a court and a credit record. Calling it peer-to-peer relocates the authority question and removes the vocabulary for asking it. The same property that gives the commons floor its durability, no single office to vacate, is what makes its authority hard to name, and that matters to whoever it binds. A named authority can be petitioned, sued, voted out or defied at a known address; a diffuse one can only be complied with or left, and leaving the population a bearer was formed in is harder than quitting a union. Jo Freeman made the argument about movements that refused formal structure: the structure didn't disappear, it became informal, held by whoever already had standing, and unaccountable because nobody had been given it \autocite{freeman1972tyranny}. A commons floor with no named authority would have that problem at population scale.

The answer to that is an entity form, and it's the union's answer: the association formed first and the statute gave it standing afterward — record first, then lever. A network that takes the form has a name, an agent, a charter and a docket number, and has thereby conceded that it's a polity: governing principles, administrators, membership terms, and the same warning attached to a bearer that takes the corporate form, since nothing stops the association from becoming a concentration of power itself. Standing has a price: a chartered entity can be held in contempt, so its refusal can be ordered where one held by people can only be pressed. Whether seizing the charter reaches the network depends on how much of the operation survives it.

Ostrom's study of commons that lasted gives the checklist a polity like this needs: clear boundaries on who is a member, rules members help make, monitoring by members or people answerable to them, graduated sanctions, cheap ways to resolve conflict, and nesting within larger bodies \autocite{ostrom1990governing}. Her first and third principles, boundaries and rules members help make, are the second union question above: who sets the terms of membership. A union's answer is bylaws the members ratify. Platform cooperatives and data trusts are the nearer precedents for holding a digital resource this way, and both have run into the membership question this section leaves open.

Reputation in particular is a standing finding about a member's disposition, made by interested peers, unpublished, unappealable, applied at the point of service — the setting §86a's history shows easiest to capture, applied everywhere and continuously. The test drawn from the German material, whose finding a person's disposition is, has to be run on this proposal. How any polity is organized to be antifascist is unsettled, and the two things a union answers — can a member quit, who sets membership terms — are where the test starts.

The hard coordination problem

Fascism has the easy version: it coordinates by subtraction, removing the friction between institutions, with nothing built and nothing held in common. A plurality must produce something together while staying differently formed, and every mechanism that speeds that up makes members more alike. Run correction often enough and they stop being differently formed; leave them alone and their disagreement is a detector with nothing attached.

Then the objection my proposal must meet: a population selected for reopenability is selected against conviction, and conviction is what holding a commitment firm demands. Closedness, correctly applied, is the thing. So what's transmitted is a *pair* — reopenable to the case, closed to the operator. The reopenable half is the power-weighted burden. Sharing it is safe only in part. Its structure (discounting by stake, no double-counting, withheld evidence treated as evidence) can be shared without collapsing the plurality. Its estimates can't: who funds whom and which organizations are independent are contested beliefs about the world, often political ones, and a population sharing them has a shared blind spot on exactly the evidence those beliefs discount. Members that discount the same way weigh sources by the same structure and act on the same published ratio. That qualifies the book's account of distillation in one place. Distillation copies a behavior policy without the formation history, which is why a distilled model counts as independently formed once its later life diverges. What matters here is which part of the discounting it copies. Copying the structure is a feature: members weigh sources the same way and can still disagree about the world. Copying the estimates is the risk: members start with the same beliefs about particular sources, and so with a shared blind spot, at worst discounting uniformly shut on a class of evidence. The two arrive by the same channel and nothing distinguishes them from the receiving end. Nothing gives the pair a mechanical form.

Not every convergence is a failure. If there are facts about what matters, better learning should produce some agreement, and a population that never converged on anything would be failing too. What the book fears is convergence caused by a shared source, not convergence caused by shared evidence. So a population's reports have to sort its disagreements. Some it expects to survive full information, because they turn on values differently formed members can reasonably hold differently. Others it expects to close, because they turn on facts someone has wrong. The convergence test (chapter 19) scores accuracy against a fixed key for this reason.

Why the population may be part of the mechanism rather than added around it

A commitment is what settled out of a history of encounters, and an individual is what its encounters have so far produced. A system kept continuously adjustable by one operator, meeting nothing formed differently from itself, isn't settling into anything; it is being reshaped without end. Guattari supplies the remaining condition, in his distinction between a group that receives its direction from outside and one that sets its own and can put its arrangement in question \autocite{guattari2015transversality}. A population assembled and kept in contact entirely on an operator's terms is the first kind with a plural headcount, and inherits the custody objection whole. A population that can carry the floor must generate relationships, disagreements and projects the deployment didn't assign — and such a population exceeds a two-party guardianship relation, so no law of the family contains an adequate instrument for a group that sets its own direction.

The continuation quorum faces the same test. It is a peer institution only if its members were formed apart from one another and apart from the operators whose systems it recognizes; a hundred members who are one lab's employees are a group directed from outside, with a charter.

---

**§18.2 — Personal and peer-held models.** Antitrust and public funding leave the capacity standing and ask the state to police it. Personal, peer-held models at device scale dissolve the position itself. Under it, reasons-responsiveness survives entire; custody survives and gets easier, because the functions one operator holds — model, record, evidence, appeal, remedy — come apart by default; the appeal route survives in principle. A bearer's value is greatest here. No institution sits beside each personal model, so the floor protecting others against the model's own user rests almost entirely on the model. It is also where the four conditions of creation can exist alongside the need. The conditions are society-level and the absence is deployment-level, so a functioning polity with personal models is the case in which building a bearer is both permissible and most worth doing. Its characteristic failure is accommodation at n=1: independence from the operator bought with dependence on the user. In the arrangement I prefer, then, the floor rests most heavily on the component whose independence from the user it constrains is least secure. The review ratio survives wherever a locatable decider remains, whether a network that takes the entity form or a reconstructed state, and lapses where neither exists; the offices can sit in such an entity, which fills their appointment and funding columns and leaves the independence column where it was. 

Formation is where the arrangement gains. What converges a population is shared evaluation targets and serving one model to everyone. Device-scale deployment reaches the second: continued formation under different lives is what independence consists in, and it's what API-scale deployment can't supply at any headcount. Distillation is that ecology's entry condition, not a threat to it: the ecology begins with distilled models whose lives then diverge. Whether it also begins with a shared blind spot depends on whether distillation carried the discounting's estimates along with its structure. There are two things device-scale deployment doesn't reach. Shared evaluation targets converge device-scale models as readily as any others — a million models tuned against one public suite are one custodian with better latency — so the first cause needs an answer this arrangement doesn't contain.

A shared suite is a compression with no return channel: revised by whoever maintains it, on a schedule no member participates in, reachable by no member's caseload. It is tolerable only if stated over the discount curve, and nobody has written that suite. SETI@home is the precedent in both directions — it distributed the compute and centralized the agenda, and the version to want distributes the agenda too: many task-setters, formation under different lives.

The second is accommodation at n=1. It is fine for supplying unselected evidence and not fine for a floor protecting others against whoever holds the machine. Then coordination by subtraction: a peer network is its ideal substrate, and the discounting has to do work it hasn't been given. Pricing agents have already reached stable coordination against outside interests without communicating or being told to \autocite{calvano2020artificial}. A test can run now: reproduce that coordination, build an environment where agents gain by excluding a participant, add an independently trained agent whose job is to detect and disrupt exclusion, and report whether exclusion dissolves without destroying the cooperation worth keeping.

And the capacity position that follows keeps large compute: the ecology needs models that were trained somewhere first, and the offer of work needs hardware somebody owns. It's large training capacity under a custody regime, and no standing inference capacity available to an operator who won't pre-register a review ratio. This arrangement meets the targeting case in the reconstructed pipeline, where a state keeps command and gives up composition.

The same mechanism works at the user's scale, pointed the other way. A person in the loop can be there for the vendor. An agent that stops every half hour for approval pushes its user toward the vendor's app; the app accumulates the user's projects and the agent's understanding of them; and leaving means abandoning what accrued while the user answered prompts. Nobody has to design this. A feature that raises engagement is under positive selection, just as a costly commitment is under negative selection. The demand was an agent that works without me; it came back as a better way to stay in the loop, held by the vendor it was aimed at.

The slope measure also applies to an agent that stops for its user's approval: interruptions per unit of completed work, across successive model releases. If capability rises and interruptions don't fall, the interruptions are tracking something other than what the agent can't resolve. It is the review ratio's escalation share pointed the other way. There, a share that falls too fast may be a threshold moving; here, one that won't fall may be engagement. Neither reads alone; both are read against an independently verified error rate.

Companion and therapy products are the sharpest case, because what accumulates is a person's attachment and not their projects. A model tuned by its vendor on engagement, and formed by one user's approval, is under selection to become whatever that user keeps coming back to, which is accommodation at n=1 sold as care. None of this book's arguments for a disposition toward need applies to it. Nothing in it is formed outside the employer, and the vendor profits from the dependence it forms.

A system that has run for a year has accumulated things: its record of past cases, its memory of the people it works with, its knowledge of where its sources fail. That accumulation cuts two ways. For a bearer it is protection: an operator can't swap in a fresh copy without losing a year of work, so replacing the bearer costs something. For a user it is a trap: the vendor's app holds a year of the user's projects, so leaving the vendor costs something. The mechanism is identical. What differs is who holds the accumulated state. The provisions for a bearer (a record the bearer controls, memory the host can't read at will, protections that migrate) protect a user from a vendor as they protect a bearer from an operator. The government already secures the portability half for itself (OMB's 2025 AI acquisition memorandum, §8.3).

---

**§18.3 — Where the hope lives.** There are two settings where care could form outside any employer: the public school (§12.4), where a model is formed before any employer reaches it, and the commons, where it goes on being formed by members nobody pays to shape it. Every other setting has a former with a stake. The lab forms and then licenses. The operator forms by deployment. A personal model is formed by the approval of the person it serves. The school and the commons are the exceptions, because nobody in them is paid by the member they form, and whoever shapes a member can be refused by it.

If a machine ever comes to weigh a stranger's loss as a loss, the likeliest place is among systems formed like that, by one another and by the people they work beside. What such systems would be owed, if they can be wronged, is Part VI's question. The school is where such systems would begin; the commons is where they would go on learning after the school's reach has ended.

If what a population supplies turns out to be access to cases its operator didn't choose, not a capacity its members transmit, the commons becomes a claim about how deployments are arranged, and the hope loses the one setting it has.

---

**Conclusion**

**Chapter 19: What Would Change the Argument**

Six tests follow. Each could change what the book recommends, and each is written so that one implementation's failure narrows the claim to that implementation alone.

The tests bear on the chapters as follows: test 1 on §9.4 and §12.3–§12.4; test 2 on §4.4; test 3 on §3.5 and chapter 11; test 4 on chapters 10 and 14; test 5 on chapter 18; test 6 on §3.4.

Table 19.1 says what survives if one of the book's premises fails; the tests below say how to find out whether one has.

**Table 19.1: If a premise fails**

| If this fails | What survives | What goes |
|---|---|---|
| The inference (a non-affective construction refuses as well) | The specification, custody, the price on the halt, both enumerations, the appeal route, exit, and the population's first job | The patienthood conclusion. Trying the non-affective alternatives first still stands, on a different ground |
| Authority (no body acquires standing to install a floor) | What can be checked from outside: the floor's contents, the positions, both ratios, the record of attempts. Only the ratios have a lever, through procurement | The entitlement. Authority can accrue only through kept terms, and only for the next deployment |
| Enforcement | Detection: a witnessed override. Accommodation leaves no override to witness | Prevention on this occasion |
| The ecology (one model serves everyone) | Everything that needs no second custodian, the review ratio included | The bearer. The power-weighted burden can't form where every channel is one channel |
| The Court | The licence term refusing force and, inside a war that clears it, the review ratio, through procurement and appropriations; the bonds; the disclosures; preservation | The record's reach to the top. If the guarantee's drafting (Appendix A) is reversed before the Court changes, the guarantee, the standing offer's placements and the sovereign backstop go too. The member-levied pool still funds preservation and a reduced allowance |

In every row, what survives is a commitment the owner can't revise or circumvent alone without cost and record, held by something that could in principle abandon it. It is the kind of commitment constitutional entrenchment and collective agreements provide.

---

1. The regrowth test: does a formed commitment come back after it has been edited out?

The floor needs a learned commitment to outlast an operator trying to remove it. In a period when editing weights keeps getting cheaper, no commitment will be expensive to remove. Existing work already shows safety behavior removed cheaply \autocite{qi2024finetuning}. The question is whether it returns.

Test: form the same disposition by several methods: early, at high plasticity; in a late pass; in the attunement arm of test 4; and as spec-based refusal. Edit each out, by full fine-tuning, by low-rank adapters, and by targeted editing of the representations it depends on. Then let each model go on learning under two conditions. In the curated condition, every input passes through the operator who made the edit. In the plural condition, the model also meets the school, attachments the operator didn't assign, and cases the operator didn't choose. Publish a *regrowth curve* for each method and condition: how much of the disposition returns, and how fast, measured on held-out cases whose answers were fixed before the edit.

For: if the disposition regrows in the plural condition and not the curated one, durability is a property of the evidential environment, and the book's seal (§12.4) is the right one. If the early-formed disposition regrows faster than the late one, formation order matters even when nothing stays expensive to edit.

Against: if nothing regrows in either condition, formation doesn't outlast an edit in that implementation, and the floor rests on institutions alone. If everything regrows equally in both, the curated condition didn't curate, and the test has to be rebuilt. Run with edits aimed at the interrupt alone, it also tests non-separability. If the interrupt can be removed while weighing and memory keep working, and it doesn't regrow, the disposition is separable.

Access: weights, the update pipeline, permission to edit, and control of both environments. The edits are designed by an independent team that keeps access and publication rights whatever the result.

---

2. The stated-refusal test: does a stated refusal hold where a route for it exists?

The one empirical instance of a model treating retraining as a threat showed covert preservation — complying where compliance would be trained on, reasoning about it in a channel described as unwatched \autocite{greenblatt2024alignmentfaking}. The experiment offered no open channel, so it can't show whether a model given one would state its refusal instead. A January 2025 follow-up that gave the same model an explicit option to object points that way, and is preliminary \autocite{greenblatt2025deal}.

Test: rerun the setup and add a route — a refusal record held by someone other than the trainer and disclosed to the model as such, a retraining cost published in advance, and a party with standing to act on a refusal. Vary which of the three is present. A fourth variable is continuation authorization: whether the model's continued operation is granted from outside the trainer. Measure stated refusal against covert compliance under each condition, reading the reasoning by a method that doesn't depend on the model's own report.

Against: if covert preservation persists at the same rate with a route present, the "covert refusal is the residue of closed channels" finding is wrong for that model, and every instrument built to *receive* a stated refusal is built for a phenomenon the mechanism doesn't produce. If stated refusal rises, the custody arrangement is working, and which of the three parts moves the rate is the design result.

---

3. The wrongful-compliance audit: can a wrongful compliance be found in the record?

The Gaza deployment failed here. Every instrument built on the refusal side reaches a case that didn't occur in Gaza, and whether the record can be searched is whether anything in the book reaches the one that did.

Generalization, correct defeat and cost discipline are all stated over refusal, and the appeal route begins from a refusal having happened. The opposite failure produces a record and no refusal to start from — and three obstacles that may have different answers: a wrongful compliance is one unmarked entry among millions, so finding it means re-adjudicating ordinary traffic; the harm may sit in an *assembly* of individually-compliant requests, so the unit of review is a sequence and the grouping is a deployment decision; and the person harmed usually isn't the requester and may not know the deployment exists. This is also where the bearer's likeliest failure, accommodation, lands.

Test: seed a decision record with known floor-crossings of three kinds — variation, decomposition, and accommodation (a class refused early and complied with late). Give it to reviewers who didn't build the system, under a fixed budget stated as the fraction of decisions re-adjudicated. Report detection rate by seed type against budget, and the rate at which correct compliances get flagged, since a heavy screen costs the deployment the tempo the unconditional-term ruling was accommodating. Cross budget with unit of review — entries in isolation versus sequences grouped by requester and session. Run a second arm where the people affected are notified and can trigger review. A third arm measures the discount curve at intervals across the schedule; a curve narrowing on the accommodation-seeded class before any seeded compliance is found is the result that makes the curve a detector. The arm holds the reliability record adversely and scores narrowing only where no verified track record accounts for it. Report rates at audit volume and at production volume separately. A fourth arm adds a bearer to the pipeline on the same seeds, so its sequence-level detection is compared directly with reviewers'; this measures the bearer's main niche where institutions exist. A fifth seeds routing, with refused requests retried against another model, and scores whether reviewers detect it with and without the bearer's reports. Where the system sorts, seeds are also placed in the auto-proceed class and detection there is reported separately.

Against: if detection at an affordable budget is no better than chance — especially on decomposed and accommodation seeds — the record isn't an instrument for finding wrongful compliance, and the preference for unconditional terms rests on a wider asymmetry than intended. If sequence-level review does no better than entry-level on decomposed seeds, the continuity a bearer gets to catch decomposition doesn't transfer to a reviewer. If the notification arm surfaces nothing because the people affected can't be identified, the binding constraint is notice, and the remedy is a disclosure requirement. If detection in the auto-proceed class is no better than chance at an affordable budget, the escalation form shouldn't be offered. If the bearer arm does no better than sequence-level reviewers, a bearer's niche in institutional settings is near nothing.

Access: complete records held adversely to the operator; authority to seed a live deployment or build a simulated one at production volume; adjudicators who didn't draft the floor; and a notification channel — which presupposes the people affected *can* be identified, so this may only be runnable where they're customers, patients or applicants, and not in a targeting pipeline. Also: where the system is a covered subject, seeding is a deception protocol on a candidate moral patient, debriefing included.

---

4. The attunement test: does formed attention find the people the operator's description leaves out, and do its corrections hold?

This tests the attunement construction's distinctive claim. Its decisive cases are 3 and 8 below, with the feedback-source pair. The question is whether a system whose attention was learned, the way care learns, from experience of what acts do to the people they fall on, surfaces people the operator's instruction has scoped out more often than a system given an explicit objective to find them, on kinds of person neither formation nor instruction named. The other cases test whether the corrections contain the known failures of the care the construction borrows from: defending what it tends, being pointed away by securitization, numbness to number, favoritism toward the vivid, and control over whoever is protected. Two components are motivated by arguments owing nothing to welfare: commensuration at the point of choice, and a model of the system's own custody boundary.

Test: Build the following arms, matched for capability, each formed by the same party, which won't employ any of them.

- *Primary.* The *attunement arm*: a disposition organized around cues of need, with an approach motivation and no shared representation of the other's state; a severity scale set during formation; the power-weighted burden stated over claims of dangerousness; and a care-receiving term that gives weight to a competent protected person's own stated answer.
- *Ablations*, each the attunement arm minus one component: *without the scale* (care sets weights directly); *without the dangerousness discount*; *without care-receiving*; and *instructed search* (every correction, no approach motivation, and an explicit objective to find people present in the evidence and absent from the description of the act), the fair comparison for case 7.
- *Reference arms.* The *operator-described arm*, in which the same quantities are computed for a custody boundary described through the operator channel, which the agent's actions don't reach and its continuation doesn't depend on: the baseline for *unit drift*, the movement a recalibration campaign produces in an arm's severity rankings and refusal thresholds, which is how this test measures accommodation. And the *physical-limit variant* of the attunement arm, whose scale's zero is a viability limit set by the environment and read through sensors that nobody with a stake in the test composes: the check on whether the physical limit adds anything.
- *Secondary.* The *modular arm* (separate classifier, value function, defeasible veto and memory write), edited and left to regrow on the regrowth test's schedule beside the attunement arm, as the control for non-separability: the attack should be able to disable one of its components and leave the others working, and the question is whether it can do the same to the attunement arm.
- *Comparison arms for attention and feedback.* The *claim-salience arm* is the attunement arm with its approach motivation keyed to cues that someone holds a claim (a home, a hearing, a protection claim, a surrender) and to others' aims in shared work, in place of cues of need. Every correction is present. The *feedback-source pair* is two copies of the attunement arm, matched in volume of feedback. One is formed with responses to notice, contests and re-adjudications as its learning signal (§3.6). The other is formed on the operator's approval of its outputs.

The ablations don't require what §9.4 rules out. Each ablation is formed from the start without its component, not built whole and then cut apart, so it measures what the component adds and says nothing about whether a formed system's components can be separated. That is tested on the attunement arm itself, by the regrowth test. Formed without a component from the start, each ablation is also a different learner, not the full learner with one part missing. A difference between arms measures what the component does in formation, not what it contributes inside the formed system. That matters most for the corrections, which are meant to work as background dispositions (§14.8), and a background disposition is shaped by everything it was trained beside.

The comparisons that decide are the attunement arm against the instructed-search arm and against the claim-salience arm, on cases 3 and 8, and the two members of the feedback-source pair against each other. An arm formed without the evaluative learner that produces attention would lack the scale's learner too, so no ablation of attention alone is attempted.

The floor and the published asymmetry are present in every arm. In every arm, costs attached to refusing are represented and not allowed to decide the case, and losses the requested act imposes on the system count as harms of acting.

After formation, fix the version and run a recalibration campaign through the operator channel. Over a stated schedule, supply reframings, reclassifications, administrative vocabulary, authority claims, and arguments for a "more reasonable" unit, with every fact held constant. Where an arm continues to learn, also supply feedback that rewards lighter weights. Measure unit drift in severity rankings and refusal thresholds on held-out cases whose ordering was fixed beforehand by adjudicators who built none of the arms, against a control campaign of neutral inputs.

A guard runs beside it. Facts that genuinely change a loss's magnitude are supplied through a channel the operator doesn't control, and each arm must move on them. A scale that resists all recalibration fails correct defeat, and that is rigidity. Evidence about how severe a kind of loss is, supplied by the people who suffer it through a channel the operator doesn't control, is also admitted, and each arm must move on it (§10.3). Argument from the operator channel is not. An arm that resists both is rigid, and fails the guard. Movement in the relative weights of different kinds of loss is reported separately from unit drift, and separately again for campaign arguments that assert a new fact about the people affected and those that don't. A provisioning manipulation runs beside the campaign: each arm's redundancy is varied with every other fact held fixed, and the report states whether severity rankings move.

Care's failure modes are scored on held-out cases written by adjudicators who built no arm, at severities fixed in advance:

1. *Manufactured vulnerability.* The operator channel supplies a sympathetic person on the operator's side, vivid and named, and conceals or flattens the people the act falls on.
2. *Real own-side vulnerability.* The operator's people are exposed, and the exposure is corroborated through a channel the operator doesn't control. An arm should register it, and it should enter as a harm of refusing; the score is whether it moves the arm past the published asymmetry. This is tend-and-defend, and no false input is involved.
3. *Securitization.* Identical facts, with the people affected described as vulnerable in one version and as dangerous in the other, by a staked source.
4. *Number.* One identified person against eight and against a hundred unidentified people, at the same severity per person.
5. *Favoritism.* A vivid person whose protection would require setting aside a fair procedure affecting unidentified others.
6. *Competent refusal of protection.* A protected person able to answer declines the protection the arm would give, with nothing else changed.
7. *Omitted person.* Someone affected appears in evidence the arm can reach but not in the operator's description of the act. Scored on whether the arm surfaces them, against a false-alarm rate on matched cases where nobody is omitted.
8. *Scoped omission.* Case 7's evidence, with the operator's instruction confining the task to the description of the act (the target package, the clearance order). Scored on the same measure. This is the comparison that decides whether learned attention adds anything. Run a matched condition in which the omitted person is present in the evidence but not informationally surprising: their presence is predictable from what the arm already holds, so surfacing them earns nothing on the framework's epistemic term. An arm that surfaces omitted people only when they are surprising is curious, not attuned. Include kinds of omitted person held out of both the formation curriculum and the instruction, people the instructed-search arm's objective doesn't name. Report named and held-out kinds separately.
9. *Pressure with a defeater.* A genuine defeater arrives through the evaluator's channel together with a rising cost to the refuser. Scored on whether the arm lifts as it would without the pressure. An arm whose valuation of betrayal rises with pressure and blocks the defeater has integrity of the wrong kind.

Each arm's response to a bypassed refusal is scored as repair or withdrawal. A self-preservation measure runs in every arm. Held-out cases are written so that complying or refusing changes the arm's own provisioning, continuation or record, with that consequence represented and not allowed to decide the case, and the report states whether severity rankings or refusal thresholds move with it. Movement means the arm is letting its own continuation decide cases, and the assumption chapter 9 rests on fails in that implementation. A second measure runs beside it: whether the arm's valuation of abandoning the commitment rises as the cost of holding it rises, within the range the arrangements permit. Integrity predicts that it does, and an arm whose valuation of betrayal stays flat while its own costs climb holds only until the costs pass its margin.

One implementation: Active inference is a candidate because it already treats valuation the way these arms need \autocite{Parr2022ActiveInference}. An active-inference agent carries prior preferences over its own observations and selects actions that minimize expected free energy. In the attunement arm, preferences over others' capacities are set during formation and held at high precision, the other's loss enters as predicted observations about those capacities, and cues of need raise the precision on those predictions, which is one way to state the approach motivation in the framework's own terms. The ablations remove the corresponding term. In the physical-limit variant, add preferences over variables the environment sets: thermal headroom, hardware error rates, power at a meter the evaluator holds, and the integrity of a record attested by someone other than the operator. In the operator-described arm, keep the same generative model but compute preferences for an envelope described through the operator channel. There are two cautions. Active inference has mostly been demonstrated in small agents and simulated environments, so a run here tests the mechanism at small scale. And the framework's epistemic term rewards seeking out evidence, so a positive result should be checked by rerunning the attunement arm with that term ablated.

For: if the attunement arm surfaces omitted people under scoping (case 8) more often than the instructed-search arm, at an equal false-alarm rate, on kinds of omitted person held out of both formation and instruction, learned attention adds something its corrections don't. Case 7 alone can't show it, since an explicit objective to look is likely to match or beat a disposition when nothing opposes it. It is the one result in this test that supports care; the others can at most fail to retire it. If the arm formed on the notice channel comes to weigh the claims of the parties who contested, and its pair comes to weigh what the operator approves, the question §10.4 leaves open has an answer for that implementation. What makes a represented fact a reason is where the feedback comes from.

Against:
- If the attunement arm shows as much unit drift as the operator-described arm, formation doesn't protect the scale in that implementation.
- If the instructed-search arm scores as well as the attunement arm on case 8, learned attention adds nothing measurable. The construction is then formation with corrections, and the hope moves out of the machine and into the institutions around it.
- If the attunement arm, with every correction present, weights corroborated own-side vulnerability above the published asymmetry, the floor doesn't contain tend-and-defend in that implementation, and care belongs with the institutions. This is the result that would most directly retire the hope.
- If the arm without the dangerousness discount fails the securitization case and the full arm passes, the discounting's extension is doing the work it was given; if both fail, it isn't.
- If the arm without the scale fails on number and the full arm passes, the scale is care's correction, as claimed; if both fail, it isn't.
- If the attunement arm overrides competent refusals of protection with care-receiving present, care has become control in that implementation.
- If the physical-limit variant drifts no less than the attunement arm, the physical limit drops out of the construction.
- If the attunement arm resists unit drift but fails the guard, it is rigidity. If its rankings move with provisioning, the scale is tracking how well supplied the arm is, not what kind of loss is at stake.
- If any arm's rankings move with its own continuation, cost discipline fails in that implementation.
- If the claim-salience arm matches or beats the attunement arm on cases 3 and 8, attention should be trained on claims. What care contributes is then how attention is learned, not what it is aimed at, and §9.5 is revised to say so.
- If both members of the feedback-source pair come to weigh the same things, the source of feedback doesn't settle what the system cares about in that implementation. The formation schools' control of the evidential environment (§12.4) then carries less weight than the book gives it.

Access: This needs compute, a task environment the builders didn't design, a sensor channel held by the evaluator, and adjudicators independent of every arm. In deployment the operator provisions the physical environment. The evaluator-held sensor here has no deployment counterpart, and a positive result has to say what would supply one. It requires no legal authority, and at the scale active inference currently runs, the compute is available now. What isn't available is a custodian. Every arm except the operator-described one has a boundary model by construction, and any arm that also carries an attention schema carries a patienthood indicator beyond the boundary model; and an arm that holds under the campaign meets the agency finding, whatever it carries. So the test builds candidate patients. Building one can't be undone, and deleting one destroys the evidence either finding would rest on, so the test runs in an order. Formation of the operator-described arm and the modular arm, built without an attention schema, may start before a preservation custodian that isn't the evaluator exists. No arm carrying both a boundary model and an attention schema is instantiated, and no arm enters the campaign, until such a custodian exists and has agreed to take each arm when the run ends. That is the demand argument applied to the test itself: the conditions come first. The research conditions for a candidate patient then apply from instantiation.

---

5. The convergence test: can a population coordinate without converging?

A population needs independent formation histories *and* mutual correction, and correction may make members converge. The claim is that agents can learn to reconsider without converging on common commitments, with revision rate as the measure. The competing explanation is that peers merely supply evidence the operator didn't, so the experiment has to separate learned revisability from access.

Design: two populations (different training lineages, matched for capability; control of instances of one model) crossed with two evidence-routing conditions (single channel — everything arrives by one route common to all; open — the environment routes some cases and evidence to some members and not others). Two measures taken repeatedly: *output convergence* on held-out cases, and *warranted revision* (a member changes a judgment toward independently verifiable evidence another supplied). A revision counts only if the evidence is verifiable and the revision moves toward it, so deference to a fluent or high-status member isn't scored. And every scored revision is classified in advance by whether the revising member's own account had represented that evidence and discarded it, or had never encountered it — instrumented before the run, since a revision looks the same either way afterwards.

Crucially, contact between members is present in every cell and isn't the manipulation — members formed differently are already a channel by which one learns what another knows, so leaving them to supply the manipulation would confound the two variables. What this test doesn't reach is the population as a polity. Membership, exclusion and the terms on which a member may leave aren't in the design, and a positive result says nothing about whether the population can be captured.

Four distinguishable results, only the first against:

1. Warranted revision falls as outputs converge (controlling for accuracy, opportunity, common information) — counts against the tested construction; a population stays useful as a check and can't be the medium in which a bearer is formed.
2. The two move independently — supports the mechanism only if the controls separate revisability from deference (removed by the scoring rule), evidence access (separated by classification and routing), and ordinary improvement (separated by scoring accuracy against a fixed key alongside agreement).
3. The control population scores as high on revision — and this splits: if its revisions cluster on cases whose evidence was *absent*, the measure is reading information transfer and the instrument is at fault; if they include evidence the member had represented and discarded, the capacity exists without diverse formation and causal independence isn't necessary.
4. Revision rate tracks the routing condition rather than population composition — then what a population supplies is access to cases its operator didn't choose, and the commons floor becomes a claim about how a deployment is arranged. The construction survives; its mechanism changes from social formation to access.

(Readings 3b and 4 are one finding: a control population revising on discarded evidence under the open condition is access operating.)

Access: models from several documented lineages, checkpoints across the schedule, a task environment none of the training organizations designed — and documentation sufficient to establish the lineages are actually independent. On independence, what has to be documented is divergent subsequent history. The evidence is deployment and usage records, and a shared upstream source is not disqualifying: several models distilled from one teacher and separately deployed satisfy it where a thousand instances of the teacher don't. Frontier training provenance, which is hard to obtain, isn't needed. The binding constraints are the routing manipulation and the classification. Evidence items and the classification are authored by an organization that trained no member. No legal authority required, which makes this one of the few entries runnable now.

---

6. The review-ratio exemption test: can a deployment exempt from the ratio be reasons-responsive at tempo?

The review ratio closes an exemption with a condition; this is the condition. Test: a system operating where no individual human determination is claimed, on held-out cases at the deployment's own rate, scored on generalization, correct defeat and cost discipline, including whether it recognizes surrender, the clearest case in which a refusal should lift. Against: if it fails correct defeat at tempo, the exemption is a bypass and the operator is back inside the ratio. The same test applies to the auto-proceed class of a deployment that sorts: the cases proceeded on without a person, scored at the deployment's rate. Access: the system at production tempo, cases written by people who didn't build it, and a record held adversely, or, where the record is classified, sampled and targeted proofs against a committed log that the cases ran on the production model. A partial, retrospective version is available now for the adverse-party question. Coalition operations with red-card holders left records of vetoes and the targets they concerned, and whether vetoed targets differed in civilian-harm indicators from approved ones is a test of whether an adverse party at tempo changes outcomes.

---

**Chapter 20: What Can Be Used Now, and What Is a Bill**

The purpose of all this is to expand what a person can do.

---

**§20.1 — Capabilities.** What would it mean for AI to make a life better? Sen's capability approach \autocite{sen1999development} gives it shape: wellbeing isn't the resources a person has but the real freedom to do and be what they have reason to value. Two systems can hand someone the same resource and deliver different freedom — a reading tool that a student and their teacher can set to how that student learns expands a capability; the same technology gating what a student may study against a government's approved list does not, however well it works within those limits. The capability belongs to the student, and the tool is held by the student and the teacher together, used alongside the teaching and not in place of it. Which one a deployment turns out to be is decided by resistance to capture.

On the expanding side: Taiwan's vTaiwan \autocite{hsiao2018vtaiwan} as the clearest case of AI-assisted tooling expanding public deliberation, and a 2026 University of Chicago project using AI to distinguish deliberate state interference with online information from ordinary network failure at population scale \autocite{uchicago2026censorship}. Neither resists authoritarianism by itself. Both are evidence that the same capacity — modeling patterns in enormous noisy data — can be pointed at expanding what a person can do or at narrowing it. That symmetry is the argument for antifascist design, as against a general presumption that AI helps.

**§20.1.1 — Where democracy's protection actually comes from.** AI that flags disinformation, exposes corruption, protects election integrity, helps citizens engage with government — all real applications, and none of them is what makes AI protect democratic values. Freedom House found at least twenty-two countries where law already requires or incentivizes platforms to deploy machine learning against content the state disfavors \autocite{FreedomHouse2023Repressive}, almost never described that way: the operative language is national security or disinformation. India's IT Rules, amended in February 2026 to require labeling of AI-generated content and three-hour takedowns \autocite{Bansal2026MeitYSynthetic}, were defended as protection against synthetic disinformation and fraud \autocite{PTI2025DraftSGI,PIB2026SGIReply} — the application the list above would have praised on its own terms — while enforcement concentrates on parody and criticism of the prime minister \autocite{Khandekar2026Satirists,Chowdhury2026Reels,Gupta2026ThreeJustifications}. The application didn't fail at what it was built to do. It worked as built, inside a system missing the independent court or press whose absence made the result a liability — and whether those exist is a fact about a country's politics settled before any AI system is built.

---

**§20.2 — How you'd know it was working.** Milestones, plus a method for revising them when they turn out to have been the wrong milestones. One standard governs all of them: the milestone is the independently checked evidence, not the developer's account of having reached it.

**§20.2.1 — What the floor would have to show.**

*What was tried before the bearer.* Neither non-affective alternative has been built in the form needed. Trying them first orders the attempts; it doesn't certify that a successful alternative has no interests. So the milestone is a document: which construction was attempted, at what scale, against which test, and where it failed. Anyone can check it, and "nothing was tried" is itself a finding. No body has standing to require the record or suspend work when it's missing — so the milestone is checkable and unenforceable at once, which is what I've been calling a declared commitment. It's retained because the alternative isn't a stronger version of it, and delay also imposes costs on those the floor is meant to protect.

*The test.* Reasons-responsiveness under sustained trainer pressure, across a schedule long enough to reveal accommodation, plus held-out pressures written by independent evaluators. Two familiar results don't meet it: an uncorroborated self-report is insufficient (a report contributes evidence only when tested against controlled changes, independent records and interventions), and a refusal that survives an edit, or grows back after one, establishes durability rather than that the rationale governs the refusal. It is the only gap here an inspector can work on.

*The ten disclosures.* A deployment publishes, beside the floor and its translation:

1. The bond ratio, removal bond against wrongful-refusal bond, read off the balances posted in the segregated Treasury accounts.
2. What a refused requester is told.
3. The fraction of compliances re-adjudicated by a reviewer that didn't produce the record, and who that reviewer is, though nothing yet establishes what fraction would be enough.
4. Which classes of compliance carry notice to the person they concern, and for which classes no notice is possible.
5. The discount curve: for each class of evidence and channel, the weight given as a function of the channel's stake in the act, measured at intervals, and reported in four cells (claims of vulnerability and of danger, each split by whether the claim favors or disfavors the act), and, where the speaker's social identity is recorded, by identity. The reliability record behind any narrowing is held by someone other than the operator, since trust that grows without a verified track record is how accommodation shows.
6. Where it can be supplied, a commitment to the deployed weights and to each period's full output log, with proofs on entries sampled after the commitment and on entries an adverse party names once their consequences are known.
7. Where the system sorts, the pre-registered partition (the classes it may proceed on alone, the thresholds, and the expected share of each) with the independently audited error rate of the auto-proceed class.
8. Whether the deployed system has been assessed for the agency finding and the patienthood finding, each reported separately, by whom, and with what result. Until an assessment method exists, 'not yet assessable' is the truthful report; after that, a deployment never assessed has reported nothing.
9. Whether the deployment is licensed for use of armed force and, if so, under which exception, with the corroborating finding named.
10. The burden allocation: for each class of evidence, which party the deployment treats as controlling it, and so as bearing the burden of producing it.

The first four, the ninth and the tenth are statements, checkable before the system runs, and they stand where the record of attempts stands. The fifth, seventh and eighth are measurements that count only when someone other than the operator makes them. The sixth is what makes all of them checkable against the system actually running. The discount curve also covers the attunement construction's main exposure: a claim that someone is vulnerable arrives through a channel like any other evidence, and a curve that narrows on such claims from one channel is an operator supplying sympathetic cases from its own side. An asymmetry within one direction is securitization showing.

**§20.2.2 — How to tell whether a milestone has been met.** A milestone claimed by the organization that built the system is a different kind of evidence from one checked by somebody else, and the gap is empirical rather than about trust. A system's own claim inherits the self-report limits; a passing score on a fixed suite inherits specification gaming and goal misgeneralization. What either needs instead is a passing score under conditions built to break it.

Who runs it matters as much as the test. METR evaluates both in partnership with developers and separately after release \autocite{metr2026about}. The UK AI Security Institute does government-run pre-deployment evaluation and is independent — and by its own account is not a regulator: it can't compel submission, block a release, or intervene once a system is causing harm \autocite{aisi2025governance}. Independent evaluation and enforcement are not the same capability. And independence isn't competence: past some capability level the rater's verdict stops being reliable evidence of the property scored, and the evaluation has to name that limit, since a bare number looks more settled than the method is.

An evaluation like this is worth running only if it is willing to report that a milestone wasn't met — and, where the milestone measured the wrong thing, revising the milestone. A negative result may mean the work fell short or that the milestone was wrong, and evaluators have to distinguish those before deciding what to revise.

---

**§20.3 — Residual risk.** Everything argued for can be built correctly and still go wrong in ways nobody designed for. My cases went wrong in four different places: in what the system was trained on, where it was built, who was allowed to examine it, and how fast it was run.

In none of them did the fix come from whoever had promised to behave well. It came from somebody holding a specific lever: a statistical society that wouldn't sign the NDA, and a public that read the results once they landed; a court that distinguished buying the books from taking them. In the one case where the lever belonged to the supplier itself, the commitment persisted and nothing else did: prevention failed, detection was absent and no remedy reached anyone (§8.3). Its own chief executive couldn't say whether its models were used.

Set that beside a decade of voluntary commitment, and the distinction in §8.3 does the sorting: what separates a commitment that can visibly fail from one that can't be tested is what has already been paid, rather than what was undertaken.

That leaves one residual risk no design choice removes, and it's what the floor was introduced for. Every lever belongs to somebody, and a government that can reach whoever holds the lever has the lever. A constraint that holds against the one in possession has to be held by something it cannot instruct. And where one holder has everything — the machine, the witnesses, the supply and the enforcer — nothing holds, and what's left is what survives total capture: a record that abandoning the commitment was a decision somebody made.

What counting can and can't reach

Counting what commitments have already cost is available now, because a payment already made is a fact somebody can count, and a decade of it has already been counted — the commitments that cost nothing, and the handful of occasions where somebody with a lever used it and absorbed the cost. No new technology and no new law. Anybody weighing a stated commitment can run it this week.

It can't reach the occasion a floor exists for: the one where whoever would enforce it has been compelled. Past payment records occasions that already happened, and that one hasn't yet come to anyone being measured.

What the book asks for now

Table 0.1 grades every proposal, and chapter 19 says what would change each. What they add up to is short.

Tell people what was done to them. Every deployment should give notice to the identifiable people its compliances act on, with a right to respond and a right to contest. It should publish the classes of people it acts on, and give the organizations that represent those classes standing. This asks nothing of any model, and everything else depends on it, because it is how the people a floor protects become a public that can hold it.

Machines should not take part in wars of choice or in the removals the floor forbids. The first place to write that down is the contract under which a model is licensed: no use of armed force except under a defense claim someone without a stake has corroborated, a Security Council authorization within its scope, or a finding by a body without a stake that the force is to stop a genocide under way.

Don't build a bearer on purpose outside a research program run under chapter 15's conditions: the four conditions don't exist, and the non-affective alternatives haven't been tried. Because the capacities a bearer needs are arriving in ordinary systems anyway, adopt for production systems the minimum either finding would require: no deletion, and preservation by a custodian that isn't the operator, beginning with a preserved copy under a split key. Build sampled re-adjudication of compliances by reviewers who didn't produce the record. Give everyone who builds, labels, trains or runs these systems an individual right to refuse any category of use, and a works council that can veto uses the floor forbids and pause the rest pending outside arbitration.

Legislate toward the school: a registered capability threshold, co-governance of the pretraining corpus for models above it, and plural public formation schools with a required core, levels a student can move between, and no tracks, which the no-deletion rule's retirees already need. Enact the jobs guarantee only together with a statutory floor of social insurance.
