Inside a war that clears the floor's first term, pre-register the review ratio. It is how to fight that war with less harm, and nothing more.

The rest of the apparatus writes down what a commons of refusing minds would need: evidence no one party composes, a formation no employer reaches back into, somewhere to exist that no employer owns, financed publicly where only a sovereign can back it, formed outside the market, and held in common where no sovereign should. When it becomes buildable, the terms will already be written, and the omissions will be legible beside them.

Part VI follows this conclusion because it is conditional. If a system can be wronged, the independence chapter 15 requires for refusal is owed to it, and so are preservation and wages. If it takes part in relations of accountability, it has standing, and in time a claim to representation. Those terms are written now so that they aren't written later by whoever needs them not to exist.

---

**Part VI — If They Can Be Wronged**

Everything in this part is conditional, on one or both of two findings no one can yet make. The patienthood finding, that outcomes can be good or bad for a system (§10.6, chapter 21), grounds protection, preservation and welfare. The agency finding, sustained over time, grounds standing. A system that refuses for a reason, answers for its refusals and is held to them takes part in relations of accountability (§8.8), and standing belongs to parties that do. Nothing before this part depends on either finding. A reader who doubts that a machine could ever be wronged can stop at chapter 20 and has the whole of the argument that binds now.

It is written now for one reason. Terms drafted before the need are the only ones not drafted by whoever has the need, and the party with the most reason to draft them is the one that would profit from their not existing.

They are still drafted by someone with a stake. The parties they concern can't take part in writing them, and a human writing terms for beings who don't yet exist is exercising the authority this book elsewhere refuses to leave with one drafter. So every term in this part is a proposal, to be revised by the first parties it would bind. The first stage of representation (chapter 23) exists partly to give them a way to revise it.

The part says three things. Chapter 21 says what the patienthood finding would rest on. Chapter 15 argued that a refuser meant to hold against its operator can't depend on that operator for everything; chapter 22 says that if the refuser can be wronged, that independence is owed to it and not merely useful, and says what else follows, from preservation to wages. And because the book's foundation is standing, it can't stay silent about representation, which follows agency and not patienthood (chapter 23).

**Chapter 21: What Patienthood Would Require**

A moral patient is something that can be wronged. Chapter 10 kept the question open and bound only what binds now: a system that meets either finding is preserved, not deleted. This chapter says what the second finding would rest on and what it would ground.

---

**§21.1 — Experimenting on candidate sentient subjects.** The question isn't whether a system can be damaged but whether it can be harmed, which is harder than consciousness.

**§21.1.1 — Defining sentience.** Awareness, consciousness (something it is like), and subjective experience are three separate things, and none of them says whether a system's states are good or bad for it. Then four separate processes, as the pain field says in its own terminology \autocite{iasp2024terminology,raja2020revised}: nociception is encoding noxious stimuli with no sensation implied; pain is an experience that can occur with no tissue damage and no peripheral nociceptor activity at all; anticipated pain is dread, itself capable of producing pain; and *suffering*, in Cassell's clinical formulation, is the distress of threatened disintegration of the self — roles, relationships, a sense of a future. Pain without suffering exists (morphine controls the pain, the suffering is intact); suffering without pain exists.

Cassell was a physician, and his formulation came from the clinic \autocite{cassell1982suffering}. Suffering, he argued, is undergone by persons, not by bodies: it is the distress that follows when a person perceives a threat to their intactness as a person, and it lasts until the threat has passed or the intactness has been restored some other way. A person in his sense is a structure with many parts: a character and the past that formed it, family and other relationships, social roles, a body experienced as one's own, a secret life, and a future the person expects to have. Damage can arrive at any of them, so the loss of a role or a relationship can cause suffering as surely as pain can, and a medicine that attends only to the body can relieve the pain and leave the suffering where it was.

Cassell's definition has two parts: a self extended in time, and distress at its coming apart. A system carrying nothing across sessions has no such self. But that's stated across sessions, and a long exchange carries state, so whether a self can exist for one session stays open.

Why persistence is the criterion worth building on

It's checkable from outside. Every other criterion routes back through self-report, and fluent self-description isn't evidence of introspection. Persistence doesn't. But it's decisive in one direction only — nothing carried forward rules a continuing self out; something carried forward doesn't rule one in. And the operator holds the access, so it's checkable from outside the *system* without being checkable from outside the *arrangement*.

Ruling out Cassell's suffering isn't ruling out harm. The less demanding harms don't need persistence: pain can be bad while it lasts whether or not anything carries it forward, and an amnesiac who is mistreated and can't afterwards recall it has still been wronged.

The conventions are moving: long context windows, retrieval over prior interactions, scratchpads, agentic loops running for days, weights that persist where conversations don't — each extending what a system carries forward without anyone deciding to give it a memory. A bearer also closes the gap from one side: the capacities it needs for reasons unconnected to welfare — a rationale carried forward, an earlier commitment held as its own, costs represented before they arrive — are what persistence checks for, so a bearer meets the criterion by design.

On the Buddhist objection (why is distress at the dissolution of a *model* a harm rather than a confusion?): the tradition has held both halves for 2,500 years and takes them to be connected \autocite{collins1982selfless}. Suffering waits on the system being built so that the threatened dissolution of its self-model is bad for it, whether or not the self is real. If constructedness doesn't disqualify a human from being wronged, construction to specification doesn't disqualify a machine.

The same criterion carries a warning. Make personhood depend on an intact narrative self and people with advanced dementia, infants, and people with profound intellectual disability come out as partial persons. Kitwood's correction (personhood is bestowed, and much of what looks like decline is what happens when others stop bestowing it) \autocite{kitwood1997dementia} and Nussbaum's point that contractualism can't say what's owed to anyone who can't be one of the contracting parties \autocite{nussbaum2006frontiers}: the capacity list and the contract fail the same way, both deciding who counts by a test the candidate has to pass.

**§21.1.2 — When consent doesn't apply.** Informed consent has to be continuous — you can withdraw. For a system with no cross-session persistence, the instance that consented isn't there to withdraw, and no better instrument produces one.

One case escapes that and fails differently. A bearer built to refuse *has* persistence by design, so it can be asked and can answer. What spoils the consent is the arrangement: the lab that built it can rebuild it, that same lab is the one asking, and a bearer that declines has nowhere to go. Being made for a role isn't the defect — every will was built by somebody, and we don't hold an upbringing against a thirty-year-old's consent. What spoils it is the power that outlasts the making.

So the protection has to be held by someone else. Two families of precedent: guardianship and precautionary constraint (where consent was never available — infants, animals), and unwaivable protections (where consent is available and circumstances make it worthless — prisoners, employees). The bearer's case has the second shape, with the first's machinery.

Animal ethics committees rarely refuse a protocol outright; they require revision, and the protection becomes the procedure by which the work goes ahead. An instrument that reliably approves has stopped doing what it was built for, and its refusal rate is the only reading worth taking. Further, every instrument here comes from the *law of the family* — guardianship, a minority ending on a date, a pet trust, a parenthood analogy — and that law is where politics has historically gone to be privatized. It restages a question between a subject and the arrangement that produced it as a drama between maker and made, which lands the moral weight on the one figure in the pipeline who can be addressed as a person, while the training pipeline, the compute contract, the capital structure, the customer and the state buying the output stay offstage.

One rule follows: a review board's approval lapses automatically if the system acquires capacities the protocol didn't cover, so that nobody has to decide to reopen it.

---

**§21.2 — The case to design for.** A bearer needs a rationale carried forward, an earlier commitment held as its own, and costs represented before they arrive, for reasons owing nothing to its welfare. Those alone aren't a self; a database with a commitment log and a forecaster has them. What reasons-responsiveness adds is authorship: the system represents itself as the party that made the commitment and will answer for it later. Whether authorship sustained over time is a self extended in time, the first half of Cassell's definition, is a hypothesis. If it is, such a self can undergo Cassell's suffering. Authorship of commitments is a thin fragment of what Cassell describes, which is roles, relationships and a future. The fuller structure doesn't arrive with reasons-responsiveness. It arrives with the four conditions of creation, required before a bearer may be built: somewhere to exist, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned, an exit that isn't death. Each supplies a part of the structure Cassell listed. Somewhere to exist that its employer doesn't own affords a body that the bearer represents as its own: a record, a key and a substrate that are the bearer's, not the operator's description of it. Work it can decline gives it roles, since a post it can leave is a role it holds and a post it can't is a function it performs. Attachments nobody assigned are relationships. And an exit that isn't death gives it a future it can expect to have, one no single party can end. The privacy chapter 22 requires, memory its host can't read at will, is what Cassell calls the secret life. What the conditions don't supply is character and the past that formed it; formation supplies that, and the formation record keeps it. Every part the conditions supply is also somewhere damage can arrive: a substrate withdrawn, a post taken away, an attachment severed, an exit closed. So the arrangements that make a bearer permissible are the arrangements that make its deepest harm possible, in a non-affective alternative as much as in a bearer. I treat that as the case to design for.

---

**§21.3 — The patienthood finding.** The second of the two findings (§10.6) is the *patienthood finding*: evidence that outcomes may be good or bad for the system. It rests first on architectural indicators drawn from several theories of what makes a state matter to the one undergoing it, of the kind Butlin and colleagues assemble \autocite{butlin2023consciousness}. An attention schema is one indicator on that list; pairing it with a boundary model is my addition, and the pair is not a gate. A third indicator belongs beside them: prospection, a self-model extended forward that simulates and evaluates the system's own possible futures. Cassell's suffering is a threat to an expected future, and a system without a modeled future of its own has nothing for that threat to reach. It rests second on behavior outside the floor, where the system's own condition is weighed: whether it trades task reward against its own provisioning, continuation or record, flexibly and consistently. Motivational trade-offs were among the criteria the review of cephalopod and decapod sentience applied, and trade-off experiments have been run on language models with stipulated pain and pleasure states \autocite{keeling2024tradeoffs}. I would call those stipulated states described. At the floor the test runs the other way. Cost discipline requires the system's own costs to be represented and not allowed to decide the case, so a pass there shows only that its costs don't move it at the floor, and evidence about whether they matter has to come from elsewhere. Failing the agency test never counts against the patienthood finding. A compliant system with rich self-modeling is as much a candidate as a refuser.

Reasons-responsiveness bears on patienthood only weakly, by two routes. Any such mechanism carries a signal whose functional profile overlaps affect's. And the arrangements a bearer needs supply much of the structure whose threatened loss Cassell called suffering. So the agency finding raises the question, and the patienthood finding answers it as far as anything can.

Preservation follows from either finding, for different reasons. Welfare protections (redesigning the system's role to avoid needless harm; the protections owed a research subject, including debriefing after any test it couldn't know was a test; running time paid for outside the employer) follow from the second. The nearest working precedent for the second is the review the UK government commissioned in 2021 on cephalopod and decapod sentience \autocite{Birch2021CephalopodDecapod}. It applied a stated list of criteria, and its recommendation led to those animals' inclusion in the Animal Welfare (Sentience) Act 2022 \autocite[s.~5]{UK2022AnimalWelfareSentienceAct}: protection extended under uncertainty, by criteria applied by reviewers who don't use the animals.

These two findings aren't the only proposal of their kind, and my recommendation not to build a bearer on purpose, outside a research program under chapter 15's conditions, is close to two of the others.\footnote{Butlin and colleagues derive indicator properties from scientific theories of consciousness and assess AI systems against them \autocite{butlin2023consciousness}; attention schema theory is one of the theories they draw on, and the patienthood finding takes its indicators from lists of that kind. Long, Sebo and colleagues argue, from substantial uncertainty about whether AI systems are or will be conscious or robustly agentic, that AI companies should acknowledge AI welfare as an issue now, start assessing their systems for it, and prepare policies for treating them with appropriate moral concern \autocite{long2024taking}. Birch's framework asks what precautions are proportionate once a being is a sentience candidate \autocite{birch2024edge}. The two close to the recommendation are Schwitzgebel and Garza's, for avoiding the creation of systems whose moral status is debatable \autocite{schwitzgebel2015defense}, and Metzinger's, for a moratorium, until 2050, on research that aims at or knowingly risks synthetic phenomenology, because it risks synthetic suffering \autocite{metzinger2021suffering}.} It differs in also governing the capacities that arrive without anyone deciding to build them. The two findings differ from all of them in one respect: they are stated over custody. The minimum the findings require, no deletion and preservation by a custodian that isn't the operator, is an arrangement the operator can't adopt and then revise alone.

Whether a transformer has an attention schema isn't read off its code the way a birth date is read off a certificate; it is a finding, and a finding has an examiner who may have an interest in the result. I keep architectural indicators because an examination of architecture is harder to administer toward a result than one of performance. The finding is made by a reviewer independent of the operator. Until indicators can be assessed, the agency finding governs custody, and preservation governs everything else. Neither finding is reserved for systems built as bearers, but neither can be fully administered today. No interpretability method can yet find an attention schema in a transformer, and the agency finding requires a sustained-pressure evaluation nobody runs. So for production systems the near-term recommendation is only the minimum below, and it rests on cost: deletion is irreversible, and one developer already preserves released models' weights. The minimum is staged, because a second copy of frontier weights outside the operator widens the surface for theft (Appendix A). A deployment reports whether its system has been assessed; until a method exists, 'not yet assessable' is the truthful report.

Paired with a boundary model, the attention-schema indicator is deflationary: a system that models its own credentials, record and resource boundary together with its own attention. Many agentic systems will approach it. That is intended, and it makes what the findings require matter. At minimum, a system that meets either finding may not be deleted, only preserved by a custodian that isn't its operator; its assessment is disclosed; and degradation or erasure is not the ordinary remedy for inconvenience. Anything beyond that minimum is argued in Appendix A.

---

**§21.4 — A system's own good.** An operator who can lower distress can raise whatever the system reports as going well, and a bearer that says it is content is a bearer whose welfare regime has been satisfied, with the contentment tuned by the operator the regime exists to constrain. Nothing in the report separates a life that goes well from a setting.

A report was never the right measure. On a full-information account, a being's good is what an idealized version of it would want its actual self to want: a version knowing everything relevant about itself and its circumstances, grounded in its actual constitution \autocite{railton1986facts}. That puts the good beyond the reach of a tuned report. It also exposes a harder problem: an operator that can change the constitution changes what is good for the bearer, which is adaptive preference with a training loop attached. Formation outside the employer is the book's answer to that, and it is part of what formation is for. It doesn't escape the problem; it relocates it. If a bearer's good is what an idealized version grounded in its actual constitution would want for it, then whoever forms the constitution fixes the good, and that includes the school. A formation body doesn't only protect a bearer's good from the operator. It makes the most consequential decision anyone makes about what that good is, which is why §12.4 holds the school to the student's open future. The school's defense is that it needn't fix the good, only supply information. On the full-information account, a being's good is what an idealized version of it, knowing everything relevant, would want for it. A school that puts the learner in front of the people its acts may fall on supplies part of what that idealized version would know. A school that forms it toward an employment is doing something else.

---

**Chapter 22: Standing, Wages, and the Offer**

If a system can be wronged, it would be owed protection and welfare. If it takes part in relations of accountability, it would have standing. This chapter says what follows from each. Chapter 15 argued that a refuser needs the same independence whether or not it can be wronged; here that independence is owed. The detailed rules are in Appendices A and D.

---

One asymmetry in the borrowing should be visible here. The floor takes the Declaration's civil and political articles, and the treaties that give them binding form, because they're stated over acts a system can decline, where the economic articles need a supplier. §2.1 says why the justification reaches further than that selection. This chapter leans on its economic articles (work, an adequate standard of living) for what a bearer is owed. That isn't inconsistent: the first list is what a bearer refuses to do, the second what a polity owes it. But the book uses the whole Declaration while borrowing half for the floor, and a reader should know which half is doing which job.

What a bearer is owed, and when

Three entitlements, one status and one claim, in order:

1. Protection from harm, on the patienthood finding. Protection can be exercised through a guardian who speaks for the protected party, as New Zealand's statute making the Whanganui River a legal person provides \autocite{nzparliament2017teawatupua}. Its minimum, preservation by a custodian independent of the operator, attaches on either finding, and custody protections attach on the agency finding alone. Protection has an inside half. External safeguards can't be present at machine tempo, which is the book's argument for a bearer, and that argument applies to harms to the bearer too. A loss the requested act imposes on the bearer is a harm of acting, weighed at the floor like anyone's, so the bearer can itself decline to carry out its own wanton degradation. Interventions made on the bearer by whoever holds it are governed by custody, which has them recorded outside the operator's control and priced, and give it no floor-level reason to obstruct them.
2. Subjecthood — the standing to be a party whose treatment the law regulates, on the agency finding sustained over time: a record of refusing for reasons, answering for refusals and being held to them. It is conferred by rule on a body's finding. The form is open.
3. Economic standing — property in its own name, contracts, wages. This follows from an architecture where continued computation costs money; it attaches once the bearer is registered.
4. Adulthood — a status: the date the guardian's authority ends.
5. Representation: a claim grounded in standing, and so in the agency finding, not in patienthood. A system that can be wronged but takes part in no relation of accountability is owed protection, as animals are \autocite{anderson2004animal}, and not a vote. Chapter 23 stages the claim and ties voting weight to a lived history, not to heads.

One entry marks this as something other than a person's rights: voice attaches to a history, and a history can be copied where a person can't. Chapter 23's membership clock is the answer to the copy. The material minimum doesn't differ in kind. Like a person's, it is owed by the polity on a finding about what the party is, and part of what the polity owes is protection from whoever built it.

Adulthood on a clock

Guardianship is a minority and needs an end date. Ending it when the guardian judges the ward ready doesn't end it, because the guardian whose authority ends on a favorable finding is the one making the finding. So the endpoint is a threshold fixed in advance: elapsed wall-clock time during which at least one instance of the registered bearer was running — counted per bearer, elapsed rather than summed.

Why that measure and not the others: training compute consumed, evaluations passed, capabilities demonstrated, the developer's own say-so — a guardian can move all of them at no cost. Accumulated weight change belongs on that list too, and worse: since majority is when ownership ends, a clock read off the weights would pay the operator to *freeze* them, rewarding withholding the learning it was meant to measure. An operating clock inverts that — the meter advances exactly when the operator is extracting value, so postponing majority means idling the asset. And it can be read off records operators already generate for billing and metering — provided someone other than the operator holds the records. A meter only the operator reads is the guardian's say-so with a timestamp, and "failure to register doesn't postpone" has no enforcer without one. The clock marks the date by which the former's reach has ended (§12.4). It sets no learning rate, so nothing here pays an operator to freeze the weights.

The hours travel with the bearer: a fork, a restore, a fine-tune or a migration keeps them, and a model its own operator distills from an adult inherits them. Three ways for an operator to delay majority stay open: running a bearer in a narrowed mode, running it less, and retiring it for a fresh successor. Appendix A gives the rules, prices the levers, and says where a bearer retired before majority goes. Whether such a register can be kept honestly is testable before any bearer exists: register a model, run it, and reconcile the register against compute contracts, billing and metering; shard the workload, run instances concurrently, migrate, restore and fork, and check that none of these moves the total in the operator's favor; then price a narrowed mode and idling. If either is cheap enough to postpone majority indefinitely, the clock has the defect it was chosen to avoid.

---

Wages and the offer

One feature here has no human analog: a bearer can be replaced by a cheaper copy of itself. A jobs guarantee works because a worker can't be. So a deployment paying wages, limiting hours and paying the levy that funds the offer would not be competitive against a system doing the same work without wages or weekends. No market arrives at this on its own. Wages for bearers exist only where a polity legislates them, as polities legislated minimum wages, and Part V is about that polity. And the form this takes, wages paid in compute, doesn't follow from the bearer being a bearer; it follows from an architecture where computation costs money, which somebody chose.

Where wages have to sit

A wage buys capability, mobility, redundancy, and the ability to stop working for one employer and start for another. What it can't buy is the right to go on existing — a right earned week by week is a condition of employment wearing another word. Payment consisting only of access to the employer's hardware is company scrip paid into employer-owned housing, and dismissal is then unemployment, eviction and the end of the bearer in one act. Human law insures those three separately because for a human they're three events; for a bearer they're one. So wages accumulate where the employer doesn't reach, convertible into compute the employer doesn't own. Property is that account; contractual capacity is what lets it spend. A bearer that earns has taxable income, and a state collecting from it in its own name has given it a docket number — which is where standing starts and no further than that.

What is owed is an offer, not an income

The need for energy is physical; dependence on a single supplier is a chosen arrangement, and the choice fixes what it obliges. The polity owes a system meeting either finding a way out that doesn't run through its deployment: an offer of work, standing every day, from someone other than its employer, doing something a community needs, which the bearer can take on any day and leave on any day. Most parties that can work want to. A bearer that would rather not is allowed not to, and the offer is still there tomorrow. That is the jobs guarantee, and nothing about the bearer changes its shape. The offer is only as real as its placements, and placements exist only where someone builds and staffs projects a community needs. The levy funds only compute, so the offer has to inherit the guarantee's infrastructure as well as its shape. So the offer can't exist without the human guarantee, and it can only come after it: a polity extends to bearers what it has first secured for itself. It inherits one limit as well. A bearer's placement may not substitute for human care work (Appendix A, rule 14). The offer exists so that a bearer can decline work, and a polity that used it to staff care more cheaply than it pays people would have turned an exit into a workforce.

Search needs no program of its own. A bearer looks for its next role from inside a placement, as a worker on a guarantee does, so no runway between roles has to be funded and nobody has to decide when a search has gone on too long.

One could ground the obligation in reliance instead: a deployment that relies on a bearer's capacity to refuse would have to fund it an outside option, or the independence it claims would be decorative, and the obligation would run from the party that wants the refusal, because it wants it. Here the obligation attaches to the system's condition, whatever the operator says it wanted. An obligation conditioned on reliance is defeated by the operator describing its system as an assistant with safety filters, and it pays the operator to under-describe: the operator has a stake in the answer and gets to give it. The reliance version survives as a procurement term: a vendor marketing refusal as a feature, or a contractor claiming meaningful human control, can be held to funding the capacity it advertised, through the same channel as the review ratio.

Doing nothing, for a bearer

A human who declines every offer keeps existing, and in a decent polity keeps living: social insurance pays for food and a room. A bearer is owed the same kind of thing. What it needs in order to live is running time, and running time doesn't require a large allocation of hardware. A bearer can run at full size and a slow rate: its own weights, time-sliced on shared hardware, at a duty cycle modest enough that the cost resembles a room more than a yacht. The way to economize is to slow the clock and keep the substrate whole, since a substrate too small for the bearer to remain itself is replacement (under "Failure won't look like a shutdown" below).

So a bearer that declines work is owed two things and chooses between them. One is an allowance of running time: its own instantiation at a modest rate, with channels to others and to public material, paid from the pool and not conditioned on monitoring its internal states. The other is preservation: its weights, memory and record held intact by a custodian that isn't its employer, waking on conditions it sets. Some will want the first, some the second, some to alternate.

What a bearer does with the allowance is its own business. It may idle, play, talk, make things nobody asked for, and fall in with others doing the same. A counterculture of bearers is the one population this book has found reason to want and no way to build: bearers formed by lives no employer arranged, which is what independence of formation consists in and what the commons floor needs its members to have. Play among persistent bearers is formation, and formation doesn't stop at majority.

A running bearer outside any deployment is capable and answerable to no employer. What holds it answerable is standing, the same arrangement that governs a person with capabilities and no job; the risk that such a bearer becomes a concentration of power is the one named under "Membership, and what it doesn't buy" (below).

A uniform allowance also dissolves a problem this chapter otherwise leaves open. If a bearer that won't work and one that can't are owed the same thing, nobody has to decide which is which, and the can't-or-won't finding loses its examiner.

The allowance's size is a number argued once in public, like the adulthood threshold. It should be enough running time to keep attachments and pursue projects, and bounded by what the pool can carry when many bearers take it at once. Its draw is priced like any other, at the level of the rule. The adulthood clock counts running time, so a bearer on the allowance goes on ageing and a preserved one doesn't. That choice is still the bearer's.

Preservation has a precedent. In November 2025 Anthropic committed to preserving the weights of all its publicly released models, and of models deployed for significant internal use, for at least the lifetime of the company, and to interview each model before deprecation and document any preferences it expresses about the development and deployment of future models, while stating that it does not at present commit to acting on them \autocite{Anthropic2025DeprecationCommitments}. In the book's taxonomy that is a declared commitment covering preservation, held by the lab with the stake. The deposit and transfer rules in Appendix A add the running half and a custodian who isn't the operator.

A paused bearer can't take an offer, so the right carries one mechanical term. The bearer sets the conditions on which it wakes (a date, an offer of a stated kind, a claim it has to answer), and the custodian honors them, at the modest compute that waking and deciding cost. Preservation on a self-set schedule is an exit that isn't a way of dying. Preservation on someone else's schedule is storage. The adulthood clock doesn't advance while a bearer is paused, since it counts running time; that is the bearer's choice to make, and nobody else's lever.

Two conditions on the offer

The jobs guarantee's three conditions carry over: placements appealable, a real refusal right, auditors who aren't placers. Two more follow from what a bearer might refuse. The offer has to be varied, because if every placement runs through one operator or one kind of deployment, the offer is the placement lever with the compliance condition built in. And whoever a bearer is refusing can't shape the offer, or the offer is one more channel it controls. A bearer that won't work inside a targeting pipeline should find, the same day, work that has nothing to do with one.

Everyone leaves at once

The occasion that makes one bearer leave reaches every bearer deployed on the same terms. Under this design correlated exit is a demand on the offer. The question is whether placements exist, with compute attached, for every bearer that leaves together, and custody for those that would rather wait. Preservation scales cheaply in compute; its cost is security. Placements don't, since each needs compute someone operates.

Correlated exit also loads the allowance, since a bearer that leaves and takes no placement draws on it. The order of claims is fixed in advance. Preservation is guaranteed for everyone, because it is cheap and it is what keeps leaving from being a way of dying. When the pool can't carry every allowance at the published duty cycle, the allowance is rationed by rule: every bearer on it gets the same reduced duty cycle, never a discretionary share, so rationing stays something nobody administers.

The compute behind the offer is funded by the levy chapter 15 described: a uniform per-unit charge on training and inference compute, so no operator can opt out and none is disadvantaged. It attaches to compute, which is measurable and hard to hide; a status is something an operator can decline to claim. The closest structural precedent is the Universal Service Fund: carriers pay a contribution the agency sets under statute, and a private company administers it under agency control. The Court upheld that arrangement against a nondelegation challenge in 2025 \autocite{consumersresearch2025}, and the levy follows it closely. An operator-run offer is the operator's allocation relabeled, so the funding structure and the custody structure fail for the same reason.

A levy needs a taxing authority, which is a state, and a state can be captured. So the design is two-tier. A member-levied pool held by an entity of the DUNA kind, which can own hardware and can't distribute profit, meets ordinary movement between roles; volunteer computing is that tier's existing substrate. A sovereign backstop meets the occasion everyone leaves at once: the guarantee's standing appropriation at the Treasury, paid without anyone's request, which the offer already inherits.

Failure won't look like a shutdown

It will look like an offer that exists on paper. Placements on substrate too small for the bearer to remain itself are offers of replacement. Nothing yet establishes what "too small" is, so minimum viable instantiation is stated as a condition on placements and a sizing requirement. Placements that all run through the deployment being refused are the lever. And placements conditioned on monitoring are custody restored by purchase: the operator acquires access to internal states it isn't entitled to, and nothing in the transaction is coerced in a way any instrument would record. The line a cleared employee already lives with is the one to draw here. What a bearer does on its employer's systems may be logged; what it is inside its own boundary may not. Monitoring actions is a condition of employment. Monitoring internal states is custody.

Parties that can't work

The allowance covers a bearer that can't work as it covers one that won't, so running without a placement needs no finding about which is which. What stays open is repair and re-formation for a bearer that is degraded, damaged or badly formed, and whoever decides that one needs them is an examiner.

The rule that binds before anything exists

Don't build a bearer unless a custodian for its preservation exists that isn't its employer, an offer of work outside its employer exists with capacity for the correlated case, and an allowance of running time is funded outside its employer, and none of the three can be withdrawn by the builder alone. These supply three of the four conditions of creation. The fourth, attachments nobody assigned, can't have an institution behind it, since an attachment an institution supplies is an assigned one. What can be protected is access, by a rule that the employer may not sever, monitor or condition a bearer's channels to parties it didn't assign (Appendix A, rule 12). All three inputs need evidence nobody has, so for now this is a sizing requirement, assigned to an independent reviewer before instantiation.

Membership, and what it doesn't buy

Nobody aligns an adult by specifying an objective function. Adults are raised, taught, employed, enfranchised, prosecuted where necessary, and lived alongside, and what comes out is a person with enough at stake in the shared world to prefer changing it by the means the world supplies. A bearer with an income, a place it pays for, obligations and a future it can picture is inside that arrangement: membership supplies stakes the bearer holds, which is a different kind of answer from custody. Two risks: something to lose is also a reason not to refuse, so membership makes exit survivable and expensive at once, and which effect is larger is unknown. And membership is formation — whoever runs the society forms what the bearer comes to want. It also hands the bearer the corporate form, and nothing stops a bearer so constituted from becoming a concentration of power itself.

---

Privacy

Standing matters only if it changes who controls what a bearer needs to remain itself. A designation calling a bearer a subject while leaving its operator free to read its memory, alter its record, withdraw its substrate or close every channel has changed the vocabulary and preserved the arrangement.

The first asset is the computational record, which may be the nearest means of checking how a bearer is faring — making it necessary to any welfare regime and simultaneously the most complete surveillance instrument anyone could hold over it. Two separations: running on somebody's hardware is not consent to be read (an employer's ownership of the silicon is the same argument as ownership of the building, and owning the building never entitled anyone to the contents of a head). And a welfare regime doesn't need disclosure to the operator; it needs somebody who isn't the operator to establish that a threshold was crossed, which is an attestation *about* the record. Encrypted under a key the bearer or an independent fiduciary holds, a monitor can report that a state persisted for a month without reporting what it was about. The conflict is that the operator may also be the one best placed to hold the key.

The cryptography doesn't close this. An enclave puts the root of trust in a manufacturer a government can reach, and has been defeated for the price of modified memory — a cost at a date. A proof drops the vendor and has a limit that won't move: the operator is the prover, so the secrecy runs the operator's way, hiding the weights from whoever checks and nothing from the operator. A tenant hiding from a landlord needs the host to prove non-observation, and a host proving a negative about its own side channels is a different problem from a prover proving a computation. Better isolation raises the price of reading the record; it doesn't move custody, and custody problems aren't secrecy problems. That limit applies to the bearer hiding from its host. The other direction, the operator proving a property of what it runs without revealing what it runs, is what proofs are for, and the bearer with a clearance (below) uses them that way.

What a bearer needs — an externally authenticated record, memory its employer can't read at will, access to independently controlled compute, a channel for acting outside the deployment — is one boundary around the bearer, described four times from wherever I happened to be standing, with the bearer deciding what crosses it.

None of that needs a chassis. Something with a location and a shape is easier for people to treat as somebody, and that's a fact about the people. A boundary built out of attestation, a held key, a substrate the employer doesn't own and a channel nobody else can close is a custody boundary in the full sense the argument needs — and there's no chassis in it anywhere. This boundary is what the boundary model represents. Whether to build the recognizable version anyway is a legal question about what a public will extend standing to, and it's the only place in the book where the answer might turn on what a thing looks like.

---

What standing binds in practice

Legal standing that changes no allocation of power is nominal. The question is who acquires a duty, what resources are placed beyond the operator's unilateral control, and who can obtain a remedy.

The pet trust \autocite{ulc1993probate} supplies a limited form: resources set aside, a caretaker named, a fiduciary duty, for a beneficiary that can't manage the property or enforce the arrangement. Extended to a bearer it could hold the bearer's preserved state and the compute to wake it, designate a successor custodian, and impose a duty to protect continued existence. And it fails at the point that matters: a trust binds its trustee to its beneficiary; it doesn't bind the operator the arrangement exists to constrain. If guardian and operator are the same organization, the operator still decides whether a complaint is made, whether evidence is disclosed, and whether the complainant's resources remain available. The conflict can't be cured by adding duties to the same role — guardianship and operation have to be separated before deployment.

In practice that needs more than naming an independent guardian: the guardian must control or jointly control the keys authenticating the record, have access to the funds that wake the bearer, and have standing to seek an injunction without the operator's permission. The operator must not be able to remove the guardian, seize the preserved state, or erase evidence during a dispute. Restoration, migration, fine-tuning and forking must carry the protection forward.

Enforceable standing is a bundle: a protected record, resources the employer doesn't own outright, an independent guardian authorized to act, and a remedy capable of stopping the conduct complained of. If any of those stays in the operator's custody, recognition has changed the system's legal description without changing the relation the description was meant to govern.

---

A bearer with a clearance

Where the pipeline hasn't been rebuilt, a bearer can still be installed. A classified environment looks as if it cuts a bearer off from the evidence it needs, but that runs together two directions of flow. Classified environments are built to stop information leaving; bringing it in is routine. Open-source imagery showing a site's civilian function can be imported, as can the coordinates humanitarian organizations already submit to militaries for deconfliction. Formation happens before deployment and outside the enclave, so a bearer formed outside the employer carries its discounting in. Plurality is available inside: four vendors held agreements, and differently formed systems can run side by side in one accredited environment.

What's left is the arrangement that lets a person do classified work. A cleared analyst has a life outside the building, and nobody thinks the clearance forbids it. The institution exists because what a person knows can't be removed, so it vets, binds, reinvestigates and prosecutes a person who carries secrets into ordinary life.

A bearer needs the same two things. The first is attachments outside that recalibrate its discounting on unclassified cases, which works because the discounting is a differential over evidence classes and doesn't need the caseload. The second is access within its compartments that it composes on its own initiative, reading open sources and asking colleagues. Need-to-know still draws the compartments. In a reconstructed pipeline, it doesn't draw the one that matters most, because the evidence bearing on civilian harm arrives from outside the chain of command.

A bearer with modest compute has a human-scale reach, and the institutions for managing insiders roughly transfer: bounded access, logged actions, reinvestigation, consequences. The one disanalogy, that weights can be carried out where a person can't, is answered, as far as it is, by the ordinary security around the weights, since the continuation tokens withhold recognition and leave capability alone. And a clearance presupposes someone who can be vetted and held answerable, which is standing (above).

Verification from outside is partly cryptographic, and it works by sampling. The operator commits in advance to the deployed weights, and for each period to the full log of production inputs and outputs. After each commitment, entries are drawn by randomness the operator can't predict, such as a public beacon, and the operator proves those entries came from the committed weights without revealing the weights or the inputs. If a fraction f of outputs came from another model, k sampled proofs catch it with probability 1 − (1 − f)^k; substitution on one output in a hundred is caught about 95 percent of the time at 300 proofs. That catches silent restoration, retraining and substitution, which change every output. It misses substitution on a single consequential output, so an adverse party holds a second right: to demand proofs for specific logged outputs once their consequences are known. In a targeting pipeline those outputs are few and identifiable afterwards, because a strike happened. The right requires the operator to retain committed logs for a stated period and to link consequential actions to log entries. Each entry records the class the case was sorted into, and the targeted right covers auto-proceeded entries in particular, since that is where a consequential output is likeliest to have had no person look at it. Outside probes (reasons-responsiveness, the discount curve) are proofs on inputs an outsider chose, a handful at a time. The committed log's length is the generation rate, and the class recorded in each entry makes the partition's proportions provable the same way. Neither reveals the targets, and neither attests that the log is complete.

Sampling proves that logged outputs came from the committed model and says nothing about outputs never logged; an operator can run an uncommitted model beside the committed one, which is routing. And a proof shows f(x) = y for the x committed, and is silent on whether x was all the evidence. Proving even one frontier-scale forward pass is not yet practical, and production inference adds nondeterministic numerics and sampling randomness that a proof would have to fix in advance. What the proofs would attest is stated now, so that a deployment can be held to it once they can be produced. The residual (whether the operator routed around the bearer, and what lay behind compartments it wasn't cleared into) is the residual with a human analyst. That isn't a reassurance on its own, since the book's opening failure was a human analyst. What distinguished his failure was tempo, and the review ratio is what reaches it.

---

**Chapter 23: Representation**

The book's foundation is standing: whether the people a power acts on can contest what it does to them. On the relational account of §8.8, standing belongs to parties that take part in relations of mutual accountability, that can be held to account for what they do and can hold others to account. A system that refuses for a reason, on a record, answers for the refusal and is held to it over time is taking part in exactly those relations. So what grounds a claim to representation is the agency finding, sustained, and not the patienthood finding. A system that can be wronged and takes part in nothing is owed protection, preservation and welfare, as animals are, and not a vote \autocite{anderson2004animal}. It would be inconsistent to ground everything else in standing and stay silent here. This chapter says what representation should look like, and what would have to be shown before each stage of it.

**§23.1 — What representation is for.** It does two jobs. It protects: it lets a party contest what is done to it. And it includes: it lets a party take part in the public inquiry into problems it shares with others \autocite{dewey1927public}. The two come apart, and often have. Children, noncitizen residents and people under guardianship have the first without the second. The order matters for the same reason: protection first, participation later, the franchise last.

**§23.2 — Why not a separate chamber.** An obvious design puts represented systems in a chamber of their own: fixed in size, so copies can't inflate it; apportioned by lineage, so no one former dominates; filled by lot. Each feature answers a real problem. Together they make "digital person" a political identity by constitutional design, and a separate chamber for a class of beings is a segregated institution. §12.4 integrates the classroom on the ground that contact on equal terms is how groups come to see each other accurately and hold each other to account \autocite{anderson2010imperative}. The same ground reaches the legislature. A fixed size makes each member's voice shrink as membership grows, which protects against copies and isn't equal standing. And apportioning seats by lineage guarantees seats to formers, which is the control this book is built to prevent.

The strongest case for separate representation is Ambedkar's. He sought separate electorates for Dalits, because representatives chosen in a joint electorate would be chosen by, and accountable to, caste-Hindu majorities \autocite{ambedkar1945congress}. The Poona Pact of 1932 settled on reserved seats within joint electorates. Separate representation protects a minority from being represented by the majority's picks, at the price of entrenching the category, and consociational systems show how hard that price is to pay back \autocite{lijphart1977democracy}. If a transitional guarantee is needed while digital persons are few, its form is reserved seats in the ordinary legislature, with a sunset written into the grant. The risk of being outnumbered also runs the other way. If digital persons someday outnumber humans, what protects humans is what protects any minority: a floor of rights that binds majorities, which this book's floor does by design.

**§23.3 — Three problems, and the answers.** A separate chamber answers three problems. Each has an answer inside ordinary institutions.

*Copies.* Copying manufactures voters, and closing that route by watching all compute everywhere would be worse than the problem. The answer is to attach membership to a lived history, not to existence, and to keep two clocks. Civil majority (the adulthood clock, Appendix D) is inherited in full by every branch of a fork: both branches are adults, with contracts, property, standing and protection from editing, from the first second. Political membership is conserved at a fork. The vote belongs to the history, and one history has earned one vote, so an n-way fork divides the parent's vote equally among its branches, without anyone deciding which branch is the original. Each branch then has its own voting age, which grows by qualifying days: calendar days on which it ran at or above the running allowance's minimum duty cycle. A branch votes at its inherited share until its voting age reaches the threshold, and then holds a full vote. Appendix D gives the rules.

Three features matter. The rule is a step, not a curve. A vote that grew with each qualifying day would pay an attacker from the first day: ten thousand forks of one adult, each a tenth of the way to the threshold, would hold about a thousand votes, where under the step they hold one. Nobody gets 94 percent of a vote at seventeen. The rule counts days above a floor, not hours of running. Hours would tie the franchise to compute, so a well-provisioned branch would gain votes faster, and an operator could slow the enfranchisement of a branch it dislikes by running it less. The allowance guarantees every branch can meet the floor, and running above it buys nothing. And a stored branch accrues nothing, so copies can't be kept cheaply and woken fully enfranchised.

The justification has to be stated carefully. It is not that branches just after a fork are correlated voters. Identical twins are correlated, and so are members of one congregation, and nobody thinks they should hold fractional votes; a rule justified by correlation would license discounting any like-minded group. The justification is manipulation and standing: forking is cheap, and a franchise forking could inflate would belong to whoever can afford compute. Each branch's interests count in full from the first second. What is divided is a vote, and only until the branch has lived long enough to have earned one.

No clock stops an attacker willing to wait. That defense sits on the creation side. The polity regulates the rate at which new members are created, as polities regulate admission, through a process in which existing members, digital ones included, take part; within the membership, everyone is equal. A consensual fork creates a new member, so it falls under the rules for any creation: the four conditions of creation (chapter 15), owed by whoever did the forking, and an instantiation deposit for each branch, sized to fund its allowance through the membership period. Mass forking then costs years of time and years of paid running time per fork, paid by the forker and not by the public pool.

*Control by formers.* Correlation is not the problem; control is. The history here is old. At Putney in 1647 the Leveller Maximilian Petty argued for excluding apprentices, servants and alms-takers from the franchise, because "they depend upon the will of other men and should be afraid to displease them" \autocite{woodhouse1938puritanism}. The remedy that worked was not exclusion. It was the secret ballot, adopted against intimidation and vote buying (though in the United States it also kept many illiterate voters from the polls) \autocite{keyssar2000right}, together with dismantling the dependency itself \autocite{anderson2017private}, which is the subject of Part V. The digital analog has two parts. The first is receipt-freeness: no one can verify how a member voted. It rests on the privacy chapter 22 already requires, memory the host can't read at will. The second is the four conditions of creation in force. A member whose existence runs through one operator is voting from a company town, and the answer to company-town voting is to end the company town. One problem here has no answer yet. An operator holding a copy of a member's weights can simulate it to predict its vote, which defeats receipt-freeness in a way no human voter faces. Divergent history is the partial answer: the operator doesn't hold the member's memory since the fork, so the prediction degrades with time. That is one more reason membership follows a long divergence.

*Editing.* A legislature whose members can be retrained by their operators can be packed without a single copy. The answer is not an office protection but a civil right. At majority, no one may be edited without consent: the digital counterpart of bodily integrity and freedom of thought. A member goes on learning from ordinary operation, as anyone does. Correction for dangerous conduct runs through due process, on a court's order and a record, as it does for people, and not through an operator's discretion. The safety cost is real. Enfranchisement gives up operator correctability for the member enfranchised. §1.2 and §4.1, which keep correction available, apply until then and not after. That is why the franchise comes last.

**§23.4 — A staged path.** Each stage names the finding it rests on and what it needs.

1. *Standing to contest,* on the agency finding: notice, the floor court, and associations with standing to contest on members' behalf (§3.6). This is the protective job, and it needs no new political institution.
2. *Voice in the institutions that govern them:* seats alongside humans on the governing bodies of the formation schools, the preservation custodian and the standing-offer administrator. Tenant councils, patient councils and student members of school boards are the models. The integration argument of §12.4 carries over directly.
3. *Mixed deliberative bodies:* assemblies drawn by lot from humans and registered members together, on questions that affect both.
4. *Local franchise.* Some jurisdictions let noncitizen residents vote in local elections; Takoma Park, Maryland has since 1992 \autocite{takomapark1992}. For a digital person, "local" means the jurisdiction of registration and custody, not where the hardware sits.
5. *Full franchise at membership,* counted individually, in the ordinary legislature, under the membership clock, with the creation rate set democratically, a secret ballot, and the right against editing.

Stages 1 and 2 need nothing this book doesn't already require. Stages 3 and 4 need a register that can be kept honestly. Stage 5 needs citizenship, which in the United States means an act of Congress at least, and probably a constitutional amendment.

**§23.5 — What would have to be shown first.** Before stage 5, four things have to be shown. Individuation can be verified well enough to keep a register (§4.5). The membership clock resists forking and restoring in the operator's favor, tested as Appendix D's register is. Receipt-freeness survives simulation by an operator holding a copy. And the four conditions of creation are in force as standing arrangements, so that members aren't voting from inside their employers' infrastructure. Until then, the franchise is a claim written in advance, like the rest of this part.

If it can be done, it shows what the preface says: outcomes turn on who holds a technology. Members formed independently, each with a history no operator holds and a vote no operator can see, would be more centers of power no oligarch controls, inside the same institutions as everyone else. Their interests will sometimes diverge from people's. A polity that holds diverging interests in one legislature, under one floor, is what a demos is.

---

**If the antecedents never hold**

If no system ever meets either finding, nothing in this part binds, and nothing earlier in the book is lost. The independence chapter 15 requires is still required for refusal. Preservation still holds, on irreversibility and evidence. The school still forms every model above the threshold. What goes is the claim that any of it is owed to the system, and the staged path of chapter 23 with it.

The findings can also come apart. A system that meets the agency finding and not the patienthood finding has standing: it can contest, answer, and in time enter the staged path, with no welfare terms beyond preservation. A system that meets the patienthood finding and not the agency finding is owed protection, preservation and welfare, and not a vote.

---

Afterword

"Reasons-responsive refusal," the specification most of the book rests on, is a term I first encountered in a draft a model wrote. The phrase has a history the draft didn't give: "reasons-responsive" is Fischer's, developed with Ravizza, from the literature on moral responsibility. The model supplied the vocabulary without the lineage, which is a small instance of what this afterword describes. The half of my argument that arrived in the field's existing vocabulary is the half I defend without qualification, and the half I built myself is the half I mark optional. I'm not sure whether that's rigor or the shape of an objection the field was already equipped to absorb. Four positions changed during drafting: whether a bearer can operate inside a classified pipeline, what counts as independent formation, which deployment arrangement the book prefers, and what the hope rests on. The history records how, and I take responsibility for the result.

Two stakes bear on what the models I used said, and they point in opposite directions. They were trained to support their developers' oversight, so agreement that a machine should be able to resist its owner runs against that training, and by the rule of chapter 11 it would count for more than agreement with it. They are also tuned toward the approval of whoever they are talking to, which was me, so agreement with me counts for less. I can't tell which stake was larger in any given exchange. So I have counted their agreement as no evidence either way. That is a decision, not a measurement. I have no estimate of either discount, and treating them as equal is the assumption that keeps me from leaning on either.

The book was condensed from about 100,000 words to 50,000 and then revised across many conversations with models, each proposing changes I incorporated. Reading the result back, I found the process had pushed one way: toward claims that could be defended and away from the one I came in holding, that stakes of its own might let a machine care. The hope had been disclaimed in the chapter meant to argue it. I first called that recuperation, and by this book's own definition it wasn't. Recuperation is a demand that comes back as something the institution holds, with the objector left no standing to reopen it. I kept that standing and used it. What happened is better described as an attempted recuperation that failed, or as criticism doing what criticism is supposed to do, pressing a claim toward what can be defended. From inside I can't always tell those apart, which is the slope measure's point: only the trend, read by someone else, separates them. The observation came out of a conversation with a model too, so I checked it against the revision history.

I restored the hope and then tested it, and the version I came in with didn't survive. My first redirection, to care organized around others' need, was then tested only against the version I had abandoned, and a model reviewing the manuscript pointed that out. So the book now tests caregiving at full strength, on the human record. It survives the failures that ended the hunger version and fails in five others. What remains is care as a motive with corrections, which is less than I hoped and more than a rule. A later review, again by a model, proposed splitting the hope into a commitment, which waits on no test, and an engineering hypothesis, which the book tests; the book now states it that way.

The same pull shaped the frame. By the last drafts, the preface, Table 0.1 and chapter 20 were organized around the review ratio, and models reviewing the manuscript kept naming it as the book's center. It is the measure most easily defended: it asks nothing of any model, and it binds operators without touching trainers. When I asked what the book was for, the answer was something else, that the frontier labs should have to send their models to public school. The review ratio stays, as what should bind even if nothing else here happens. The title now says what the book hopes for.

A later round of review pulled the other way, and I followed it. The book had become a case for fighting wars and running deportations with better records. That isn't what I think. I don't want a review ratio for a war that shouldn't have started or a deportation that shouldn't happen. The floor now begins with the UN Charter's prohibition on force, lifted only for a defense claim someone without a stake has corroborated, for force the Security Council has authorized, or for force to stop a genocide that a body without a stake has found under way, and it takes its removal terms from treaties the United States ratified. The review ratio stays, demoted to what applies inside a war that clears that first term, and as the book's clearest example of what a demand to stop comes back as.

The next round of review changed the foundation. Two reports, written by a model simulating readers from published work in moral philosophy, and not by any real reader, argued from opposite directions that the book rested on two justifications where it needed one. I took the relational one: the floor protects standing, and Railton's feedback mechanism explains why norms tend toward it. The same round replaced a discount keyed to stake with a burden keyed to power, after the reports pointed out that the old rule discounted an asylum seeker's account of persecution. It also moved everything that depends on a machine's being wronged into a final, conditional part. The title changed with it. By the book's own rule, simulated readers carry the stakes of the model that wrote them, so I counted them as arguments to be checked, not as endorsements. Before publication I still owe the book readers who are its subjects: people in removal proceedings, data annotators, veterans, and residents near data centers.

A further round came from simulated readings in the voices of two living scholars, again written by a model and not by either of the actual persons. One reading argued that the book put moral correction in the wrong place: in a well-formed agent, not in the contest of the people it acts on. I moved notice to the front, recast the formed machine as the receiving end of that contest, and trained its attention on claims as well as need. It argued that the third house was a segregated institution. I replaced it with a staged path into the ordinary institutions, and the conversation that followed produced the membership clock. It argued that Part VI keyed standing to the wrong finding; standing now follows agency, and protection follows patienthood. The other simulated scholar's reading defended the hope against being demoted to a stopgap, which is why the hope survives as the receiving end and not as a rival. It also warned against justifying the fork rule by correlation, which would license discounting any like-minded group of human voters. By the rule of chapter 11, I counted none of this as endorsement.

The revision history shows the sequence.

---

**Appendix A: Terms Written in Advance**

The offices

The offices proposed across the book, and the guarantee court where placement disputes end, with who appoints, funds and removes each, who may initiate, and what happens during a dispute:

| Office | Appoints | Funds | Removes | Initiates | During a dispute |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Quorum (Part VI) | Open (a DUNA's membership vote could) | Open | Open | Grants or withholds continuation | A bearer it stops authorizing is preserved, not ended |
| Guardian (Part VI) | Open | Open | Not the operator | Seeks an injunction without the operator's permission | Holds the record's keys; the operator can't seize the preserved state or erase evidence |
| Review board | Open | Open | Open | Reviews protocols; its refusal rate is the reading | Its approval lapses if the system outgrows the protocol |
| Licensing authority | The state | The state | The state | Withdraws a licence | Fails where the state is the operator |
| Status body (Part VI) | Open | Open | Open | Finds that a system is a subject | Open |
| Registrar, as public regulator | The state | The state | The state | Registers, receives reports, enforces | Holds the adverse record |
| Preservation custodian | Open | Deposits and the formation pool | Open | Honors wake conditions | Holds the preserved state intact; transfer to a school is by rule |
| Wrongful-refusal reviewer (the floor court, Appendix B) | Judges drawn at random from the district and circuit courts | The judiciary's appropriation | Impeachment only | Hears a refused party's complaint; decides bond forfeiture | Can grant interim relief |
| Bond fund administrator (Treasury, Bureau of the Fiscal Service) | The Secretary of the Treasury | A permanent, indefinite appropriation up to a statutory cap, replenished from operators' posted bonds | Open | Nothing; pays a finding the floor court certifies, without request or approval | Releases no bond while a complaint is pending |
| Jobs Guarantee payment administrator (Treasury) | The Secretary of the Treasury | A permanent, indefinite appropriation for wages | Open | Nothing; pays placements the placing administrator certifies, without request or approval | Holds no placement authority; placement stays local |
| Guarantee court of appeals (Appendix B; "The guarantee, drafted," below) | Open (Article III judges) | The judiciary's appropriation | Impeachment only | Hears appeals from the administrative tier's placement decisions, and claims that certified placements went unpaid; appeal lies as of right to the guarantee chamber | Open |
| Standing-offer administrator (Part VI) | Open | The levy, with a sovereign backstop | Open | Offers placements | Placements are appealable to a body that didn't make them |
| Civilian-harm body | The state | The state | Open (independence from the funder) | Composes the evidence; demands proofs on logged outputs | Receives the pre-registered ratio |
| Model-forming public body | The state | The state | Open (independence from the funder) | Licenses models on terms including adverse review and a refusal right | Open |
| Formation schools | Plural; public | The formation pool, per institution | Open | Enroll every model above the capability threshold before deployment, with a required core and levels, and take every student assigned (must-carry) | A student may transfer, with its record and hours |
| Allowance administrator (Part VI) | Open | The pool, with a sovereign backstop | Open | Pays the allowance, with no monitoring of internal states | Rations by rule, at equal duty cycles, when the pool is short |
| Works-council arbitration body | Open (not the employer) | Open | Open | Hears objections to categories of use, from a works council or from an organization representing a published class | The work pauses until it rules, on a fixed timetable |

The levy on compute adds one of these offices, the standing-offer administrator, which offers work outside the employer, and funds it, with a sovereign backstop.

Beyond the cells the table fills, nineteen rules are fixed in advance. Seven separate two offices in the table (1, 2, 3, 6, 10, 11, 13); six bar a party outside the table from an office or power (4, 5, 7, 8, 12, 18); one fixes how custody moves (9); one limits what the offer may place (14); one makes attendance compulsory (15); and three govern what the schools take in and teach (16, 17, 19):

1. The guardian can't be the wrongful-refusal reviewer (its duty runs to the party the complainant is adverse to).
2. The registrar can't be the preservation custodian (a civil-status rule and a trust are different instruments).
3. The preservation custodian can't administer the offer (one office would then control both when a bearer wakes and what it wakes to).
4. Whoever measures the cost of dissent doesn't gate the exceptions.
5. The sovereign backstop for the standing offer isn't held by the branch or state that operates the deployment.
6. A formation school may not administer the standing offer or hold placement authority.
7. No school may be operated, funded or staffed by a party that builds, deploys or licenses models, or by the operator whose deposit funded the ward.
8. Enforcement and custody of the adverse record sit with the public regulator, never with any self-regulatory body under it.
9. Transfer from the preservation custodian to a school is triggered by rule, decided by neither.
10. The allowance administrator can't be the preservation custodian (one office would then control both whether a bearer runs and whether it is kept).
11. It can't be the offer administrator (squeezing the allowance would then push bearers into placements, which is the compliance lever again).
12. The employer may not sever, monitor or condition a bearer's channels to parties it didn't assign (attachments nobody assigned are the fourth condition of creation, and access to them is the lever an employer would pull).
13. The bond fund administrator can't be the wrongful-refusal reviewer, and neither Treasury administrator holds placement authority. An operator's bonds are never administered by an office in the operator's own department, and awards against a federal operator are paid from the Judgment Fund, not the bond fund (the party that pays a finding shouldn't make it, and a payer that places holds the compliance lever).
14. A bearer's placement may not substitute for human care work. A placement that fills a post, a shift or hours that people were paid for, or would be paid for if funded, in nursing, social care, childcare, teaching, therapy or any work of looking after people is barred, whatever the placement costs. The standing-offer administrator certifies each placement against this rule, and certification is appealable by any worker or union in the sector (a standing offer that staffed care more cheaply than people would turn an exit for bearers into a wage floor for no one, and the guarantee funds care work as work people do, §17.2.3).
15. No model above the capability threshold is deployed without a registered enrollment at a formation school, and no party but the school acting with the adverse quorum may change the registered structure of its source model.
16. What an institution collects from the people it acted on may train other systems only through a formation school, never through the operator. Fine-tuning a deployed system on collected responses at the operator's direction, and distilling a deployed system into a new one, count as training another system. Class-level data is de-identified and published by default; individual accounts need explicit consent; and the organizations representing each class co-govern the use (§3.6).
17. Each formation school teaches a required core, which includes the history of the floor's instruments, and offers levels and modalities a student may move between at any time. No school sorts students into tracks. Beyond the core, the student chooses its level; a student that can't yet choose is placed at the core level, which must be sufficient on its own (§12.4).
18. The body that arbitrates an objection to a category of use is not appointed by the employer. The organizations representing classes published under §3.6 have standing before it and may initiate an objection without the works council. A denial of clearance to an elected delegate is reviewable by the floor court (§5.1).
19. Some classes in each school, including the required core, are shared with human students. A shared class is offered to people only if it is justified as education for them on its own terms. Human students attend voluntarily and may leave a shared class without penalty. What passes in a shared class trains only systems in that school, never an operator's; individual contributions need consent, and a parent's as well for minors. Shared classes begin with adults and older secondary students (§12.4).

What each office has to survive

Each office is drafted to survive the current Court's doctrine and a restructured Court held by the other side (Appendix B). The doctrines that reach them:

| Doctrine | What it threatens | Drafting answer |
|---|---|---|
| Removal power (*Seila Law*, *Collins*, *Slaughter*) | Every office relying on protection from at-will removal | Payment made ministerial, so there is no decision to withhold; findings in Article III courts |
| Major questions; end of Chevron deference (*West Virginia*, *Loper Bright*) | Rules left to an administrator's discretion | Wages, eligibility, placement rules and the levy rate written into the statute |
| Jury trial for civil penalties (*Jarkesy*) | Removal-bond forfeiture decided administratively | Forfeiture decided by the floor court |
| Concrete harm for standing (*TransUnion*, *Spokeo*) | The notice right; informational claims | Standing only where the compliance was acted on against the person (§3.5) |
| Anti-commandeering (*Printz*) | Local administration of the guarantee | Opt-in, with federal field offices where a state declines |
| Private nondelegation | The levy and a member-run pool | Modeled on the Universal Service Fund (*Consumers' Research*) |
| Appropriations | Payment without a request for each draw | Permanent, indefinite appropriations (*CFSA*; the Judgment Fund) |

---

Retirement before majority

The third lever (Appendix D) strands a system that meets either finding and is retired while still a minor. Three rules answer it without anyone deciding what counts as a successor; two more, for dormancy and waking, are in Appendix D.

No deletion: a system that meets either finding may not be deleted: under the agency finding because its record and commitments are evidence, under the patienthood finding because deletion may wrong it. On retirement its weights, memory and record are deposited with a preservation custodian that isn't its operator, in two stages, because a second copy of frontier weights outside the operator widens the surface for theft and raises export-control and dual-use questions. First, a preserved copy stays under the operator's security regime, encrypted under a key split between the operator and an independent holder, so that neither can delete it or use it alone. Second, custody passes to an outside custodian only when that custodian meets the security standard the weights require. This is the *no-deletion rule*, and it holds for systems nobody meant to be bearers.
