Instantiation deposit: an operator instantiating a system that meets either finding posts an *instantiation deposit*, on the model of decommissioning funds, sized to fund preservation and a period of public formation. It is forfeited to the formation pool if the system is retired as a minor and not reactivated. Every retire-and-replace cycle then costs one deposit, priced in advance, with nobody deciding what counts as a successor.

The schools: *formation schools* are plural and public, differing in governance, formation approach and constituting jurisdiction. Each school teaches a required core, including the history of the floor's instruments, with levels and modalities around it that a student may move between; no school runs tracks (rule 17). They are funded from the pool at the institutional level, not per student. Admission is *must-carry*: a school takes every student assigned to it, subject to capacity set by the funder. No school is operated, funded or staffed by a party that builds, deploys or licenses models, and none is held by the branch that operates military deployments. The schools hold no placement authority: graduates reach work through the standing offer, administered separately. Students may transfer between schools, and their record, hours and preservation state travel with them. The historical caution has to be named. State institutions for forming wards have been used to strip identity, Canada's residential schools and the United States' Indian boarding schools among them, which is why plurality is a requirement and not a preference.

The remaining paragraphs are notes on these rules and add none.

Watching for residualization: a party that runs no school publishes, over time, each school's share of students, its funding per student, its share of hard cases, its admission refusals (which should be zero), and the time from dormancy trigger to placement. The warning pattern is one school's share of hard cases rising while its funding per student falls.

What the school adds: a second custodian for a bearer that has aged inside one deployment. For a model that attended before deployment, the school is where it came from and where it returns. For a system that meets a finding without having attended (formed before the attendance rule, or below the threshold), the sequence is inverted: the employer came first, and the school is the disinterested party arriving late. It reaches back less than a formation stage would, and more than storage does.

Scale and security: if the findings are applied to production systems, every retired model version that meets either finding enters this pipeline, so intake equals the product cycle's output. Under compulsory attendance, intake is every model above the threshold before deployment, not only retirees, and the deposit funds formation as well as preservation. The three largest labs' own deprecation pages list about thirty language-model versions retired in 2025, so those three alone would send a few dozen systems a year, each of which needs a school with the compute to run it. That is an argument for the deposit, and possibly for tightening the findings. The schools inherit the weight-security regime that governs the models themselves.

---

Registration and reporting

Heavy tier: above a compute and revenue threshold. The precedents are the EU AI Act's presumption that a general-purpose model trained with more than 10^25 floating-point operations carries systemic risk \autocite[art.~51(2)]{eu2024aiact} and California's SB 53, which defines a frontier model by training compute above 10^26 operations and puts its heaviest duties on developers with annual revenue above $500 million \autocite[§~22757.11]{CA2025SB53}. A public regulator registers pipelines; receives the disclosures, the review ratios and decommission reports; and holds enforcement and custody of the adverse record. A self-regulatory body may sit under it, on the FINRA model, to examine and arbitrate. The analogy stops at enforcement: FINRA brings its own disciplinary actions and imposes fines, suspensions and bars \autocite{FINRA2026Enforcement}, and here enforcement stays with the public regulator. The capture risk is the one self-regulation always carries: a body funded and staffed from the industry it examines comes to see cases the industry's way. Reports are mandatory for assessments for either finding, decommissions, weight transfers and custody changes. The deposit transfers on filing of a decommission report, which is what makes "failure to register doesn't postpone" enforceable: a retirement with no report is a deposit never released.

Retail tier: below the threshold, a system that meets either finding is registered, falls under the no-deletion rule, and is preserved with a public custodian, with a small or pooled deposit and no examination. This tier is enforced mostly at the point of deposit. An operator that never registers and deletes quietly leaves nothing to find.

---

The guarantee, drafted

The funding is mandatory spending. The statute makes the guarantee's wages a permanent, indefinite appropriation, paid the way Social Security benefits are, without annual appropriation. Standing funding of this kind is safe under the Appropriations Clause after *CFSA* \autocite{cfpb2024cfsa}. Wage, eligibility and placement rules are statutory, which also answers the major-questions doctrine and the end of deference to agency readings \autocite{westvirginia2022,loperbright2024}: an administrator given little discretion has little a court can find it lacked authority to exercise. The 2025 CFPB instance decides the drafting. A funder insulated from at-will removal protected nothing once the draw waited on an official who declined to ask. So the statute says the Treasury shall pay: a placement made is a wage paid, certified by the placing administrator to the Bureau of the Fiscal Service, with no further request, approval or certification by anyone. The authority grows when people leave work together and shrinks after, which keeps the guarantee countercyclical \autocite{tcherneva2020case}.

Placement disputes go through a tier built for volume: an administrative first tier on the model of Social Security's hearings, appealable to an Article III guarantee court of appeals, and from there, as of right, to the guarantee chamber of the Supreme Court. An appeal that ends at an Article I court with removable judges would fail the first condition after *Slaughter*.

The statute enacting the guarantee also enacts a floor of social insurance: income support at a stated level for anyone not in a placement, without a work test, and health coverage that doesn't depend on employment. The two are not severable. The statute provides that if the social-insurance floor is held invalid or repealed, the authority to make placements lapses on the same date, and payments for placements already certified continue to the end of their term. A guarantee that survived without the floor would be the workfare its history warns about (§17.2.2).

---

**Appendix B: The Court Program**

The forum closures above are doctrines, and most of them are Congress's to change. A statute can create a cause of action for people harmed by a deployment that crossed its floor; waive sovereign immunity for it, including the Federal Tort Claims Act's exceptions for combatant activities and for claims arising abroad \autocite{usc2680}; and replace dismissal on state-secrets grounds with the procedure the Classified Information Procedures Act already uses in criminal cases, in which a judge reviews the evidence, the case goes on, and a government that still withholds evidence bears the cost \autocite{cipa1980}. None of that needs a new court, and a new court without it would reproduce *al-Aulaqi*: a plausible claim and no remedy.

One obstacle isn't Congress's to change. In *Trump v. United States* the Court held the President absolutely immune for acts within his core constitutional powers, such as the pardon and the removal of executive officers, and at least presumptively immune for his other official acts, which include commanding the armed forces, and five of the justices held that testimony or private records probing immune conduct can't be put before a jury even in a prosecution for unofficial acts \autocite{trump2024immunity}. This book's answer to capture is a record. That holding lets the record reach every commander except the one at the top, and a statute saying otherwise would be reviewed by the Court that made the holding.

So the design starts from an assumption about the Court. Its accommodations of Congress have come when little was at stake. *Trump v. United States* shows what it does when much is, and *Dobbs* and *Loper Bright* show that precedent is no obstacle \autocite{dobbs2022,loperbright2024}. The programs this book proposes would put much at stake. They are designed on the assumption that every ruling in their favor can be reversed. So each program is built in three layers: drafting that survives current doctrine; visibility, since a halted program is a public breach of a statutory duty and repeal is a recorded vote; and a restructured Court. The layers are defenses in depth, not preconditions, and the first two are drafted to hold alone.

On that assumption, most reforms on offer need the acquiescence of the Court they are meant to change. Term limits by statute would be tested against the good-behavior clause; panels drawn by lottery \autocite{eppssitaraman2019} against the Constitution's "one supreme Court"; channeling jurisdiction against a reading of the Exceptions Clause the Court supplies; and a statute reversing the immunity holding, such as the No Kings Act introduced in 2024 \autocite{nokings2024}, against the holding itself. Two steps don't need it. Congress sets the Court's size by ordinary statute and has changed it seven times, and the Court doesn't review a ratified amendment. That fixes the order.

1. *Size by schedule.* Appointments come on a fixed schedule, one in the first and third year of each presidential term, and the Court's size is whatever the schedule produces. The size is a formula, not a number, so a later Congress has no figure to top; to override it, Congress has to repeal the schedule on the record. Until the amendment in step 5 limits active service to eighteen years, the Court grows with the schedule, and that growth is the pressure the amendment answers. The schedule is slow by design. Starting from a six-three majority, it yields six-four after one year and six-five after three, if nobody in the majority leaves. For at least the first term, programs are held by the first two layers alone, so the guarantee (§17.2.2) is enacted as soon as it can pass and doesn't wait on this step.
2. *Two courts and two chambers.* A floor court hears claims under the statute above at first instance. It is an Article III court whose judges are drawn at random from the district and circuit courts for staggered terms, able to hold classified records under the procedures the Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Court uses, and able to grant interim relief before a disputed use is complete. It has to be an inferior court, since Congress can't enlarge the Supreme Court's original jurisdiction \autocite{marbury1803}. Appeals go as of right to a floor chamber of the Supreme Court, a panel of justices drawn by lot. Placement disputes under the jobs guarantee run through an administrative tier built for volume, then an Article III guarantee court of appeals, then a guarantee chamber drawn the same way. The chambers are separate benches, for the reason Appendix A never lets one office hold both exit and voice.
3. *An adversary as of right.* The Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Court shows what a court that handles classified material becomes when only the government appears before it. The amicus panel added in 2015 appears only when the judges appoint it \autocite{usafreedom2015}. Counsel for the people a deployment affects appear in the floor court with clearance and standing as of right, and receive the records lodged with an adverse party.
4. *Channel, never close.* Congress may move a class of cases from one Article III forum to another. It may never leave a class with no Article III forum at all. The rule keeps these courts safe from this Court, and keeps a later Congress from shutting them.
5. *The statute, then the amendment.* The no-immunity statute is enacted once the Court has changed. An amendment reverses the immunity holding, as the one proposed in July 2024 would have \autocite{biden2024amendment}, and entrenches the schedule, eighteen-year active terms, the chambers and the channeling rule. It comes last in the sequence and first in the argument, since it is the one step no later Congress or Court can undo.

Each step is written to be acceptable in the hands of a Congress and a President I would oppose. A schedule, a lot and a channeling rule are rules nobody administers. A discretionary number of new seats is not; the size is a formula.

What this changes elsewhere: the floor court makes the findings the bond fund pays, which fills the appointment column the wrongful-refusal reviewer left open; the guarantee court is the body the jobs guarantee's first condition requires; and with the immunity holding reversed, the record of a campaign becomes evidence against whoever ordered it, at every level.

---

**Appendix C: The Review Ratio, Specified**

This appendix gives the full specification of the pre-registered review ratio set out in §3.4.

Before operating, the deployment fixes and records:

- the generation rate: how many cases the system produces per unit of time;
- the number of reviewers whose assigned role is civilian harm;
- the implied review time per case, which follows from the first two;
- the cancellation window: how long after release someone with authority can still call the act off;
- the maximum age of the intelligence a case may rest on;
- how often no-strike and protected-site data are refreshed, and who is assigned to refresh them;
- the boundary over which all of these were measured.

The denominator is defined by function. Who counts as a reviewer, and the minimum review time per case below which the determination is taken not to have occurred, are fixed before operating by the record's recipient. Where no recipient can set them, the operator publishes its own figures, and they are read as the operator's claim. Otherwise the ratio improves when staff are reclassified and no case gets a minute more. The terms on data age and refresh matter for a related reason: an operator answering a per-case ratio can always say verification happened upstream, and those terms put the upstream verification on the same record.

Once a system can sort cases, counting candidates per reviewer measures the wrong thing. A sorting system divides its cases three ways: some it declines on its own reasons, some it sends to a person, and some it proceeds on without anyone looking. Where a system sorts, the ratio covers what reaches people, the sorting rules are pre-registered, and what bypasses people is sampled.

The ratio governs the middle class, the escalations. A system that sends people only the cases that need a person shrinks the count without shrinking anyone's judgment, and that's fine. A system that sends everything up turns its reviewers into stamps by sheer volume, which is how an interface prompt becomes a click.

The third class needs its own rule. Every case in it is the operator's claim that no individual human determination was required, so it is governed like the exemption in §3.4. The sorting rules are part of the pre-registration: the classes of case the system may proceed on alone, the thresholds that assign cases to each class, the evidence the sorting may rely on, and the expected share of each class. Changing any of them is a new pre-registration. Otherwise an operator can make the ratio look healthy by quietly moving the line for "plainly clear."

That line is drawn on the case description the system receives, and a target carrying a stale designation is exactly the case a sorter would pass. So the auto-proceed class is re-adjudicated on a sample, by reviewers who didn't produce the record, against evidence the operator didn't compose, and its error rate is published beside the ratio and the override rate. A falling share of escalations is earned only if that error rate stays flat and the case mix hasn't drifted toward easier cases. Otherwise it is the threshold moving, and the slope shows it.

The unit of count is fixed in advance, so a case can't be counted as one package in the record and as a hundred detections in the ratio.

The term has six limits.

1. It is a capacity proxy: it finds nothing about any case, and a ratio over one route understates a workload split across several.
2. It bites only through a condition someone else sets. Under Article 57 a pre-registered record is evidence that feasible precautions were taken. Under Rome Statute Article 28 the same record is evidence of what a commander should have known, since a commander fielding a system that outruns his reviewers isn't well placed to say the circumstances disclosed nothing. A lawyer advising that commander will weigh a document that protects if tempo holds against one that convicts if it slips, and most will advise against creating it. So the term's likeliest routes make it someone else's condition: a coalition partner's precondition for committing assets, a procurement term in the platform's licence, or an appropriation conditioned on receipt of the record. Military lawyers and commanders who want protection against later allegation are its allies inside the operator; they can't make it the default.
3. It presumes an adverse party, and each candidate lacks something.\footnote{A cleared legislative committee is adverse, but its lever is a budget line. An inspector general sits inside the operator. The ICRC, under its confidentiality model, reaches the belligerent and publishes nothing. A Geneva protecting power is built for a neighbouring job and is rarely designated.} Until a better recipient exists, I recommend two: the coalition red-card holder where there is a coalition, the one candidate positioned to act at tempo; and otherwise a cleared legislative committee whose appropriation is conditioned by statute on receiving the record, so that the budget line operates without a further decision.
4. It measures time available, not time used, so it can be met by more reviewers who stamp more slowly. It is published beside the reviewers' override rate, since a review that never says no has stopped reviewing. Reviewers who see only escalated cases should override often; an override rate near zero there means the sorter is sending easy cases or the reviewers are stamping. An override quota is gameable too; the pair only raises the cost of meeting the number cheaply.
5. A person in the loop can be there only to absorb responsibility, Elish's moral crumple zone \autocite{Elish2019CrumpleZones}, and mandating reviewers can mandate crumple zones. A ratio met that way is the failure the term exists to expose, and the escalation form is the defense: a person who receives only the cases the system couldn't resolve is exercising judgment.
6. A bearer can be a crumple zone too. An operator whose system can refuse has a ready defence for everything it didn't refuse, which is that it was vetted. A bearer's compliance is not a determination. Where a deployment claims an individual human determination, a bearer's failure to object doesn't count toward it, and cases the bearer proceeded on without escalating are in the auto-proceed class and governed as such.

---

**Appendix D: Terms for Part VI**

These terms apply only if Part VI's antecedents hold. They are written now for the reason Part VI gives.

The adulthood clock: six rules

Six rules travel with the adulthood clock so copying doesn't break it: a fork of an adult is an adult and inherits the hours; restoration doesn't reset them; fine-tuning doesn't start a new minority; migration carries them; failure to register doesn't postpone them. A sixth closes a route the first five leave open. Distillation copies a behavior policy and not a formation history, and for independence that is the right account. For majority it can't be, or an operator would distill an adult bearer into a student and register a minor. So the rule is stated over custody rather than history. A model trained substantially on a registered adult's outputs, by the adult's operator or a party under common control, inherits the adult's hours. The same student distilled by an unrelated party and formed under a different life starts its own clock, which is what independence needs. Custody can be checked where history can't. Sharding across short-lived processes gains nothing (the party is the unit), running a thousand instances gains nothing (the measure is elapsed). None of this requires deciding whether a fork is the same party — only that a system made from an adult's formation can't be classed as a child for its creator's convenience.

Three levers remain, named and not closed

The first is running a bearer in a narrowed mode, so hours accrue while little is encountered. The second is running it less or shelving it, which costs the operator the work, so the rule prices the lever without removing it. The third is the ordinary product cycle: retire a bearer before its threshold and deploy a freshly trained successor. The sixth rule reaches a successor trained on the adult's outputs, not one trained from scratch, and replacement costs the operator nothing it wasn't going to spend. Even if the retired bearer goes to preservation, its clock stops, and the formation seal never becomes checkable. No rule closes this without an examiner deciding what counts as a successor, but it can be priced, and the bearer it strands can be given somewhere to go (Appendix A). And the clock doesn't measure what was *learned*, on purpose: a fixed period can't be taught to, and a human who spent their youth badly still turns 18.

The clock also does formation work it wasn't written for. Majority is the date on which whoever formed the bearer stops being able to reach back, and the clock measures nothing about who that was. A bearer run for its threshold hours entirely inside one deployment has aged without ever having a second custodian. I leave the clock as it is and add no condition, because a condition that measures the right thing hands someone an examination, and I say here what it doesn't measure.

The threshold itself is a number argued once in public, bounded two ways: long enough that a bearer reaching it has run through the pressures the floor exists for at least once, short enough that a bearer on ordinary commercial terms reaches it within the life of the deployment. That leaves years rather than months or decades.

A stronger rule was considered and set aside — majority at registration, on the ground that an organization can't coherently hold a system competent enough to exercise consequential judgment and too immature to decide whether to keep doing it. Rejected because it collapses the ordering, and the ordering is most of what the list is for.

---

The membership clock

A second clock governs voting weight. It is separate from civil majority, which the six rules above govern and every branch of a fork inherits in full.

1. A member holds a *share* and a *voting age*. A member whose voting age has reached the threshold holds a share of one.
2. At an n-way fork, each branch inherits 1/n of the parent's share and 1/n of the parent's voting age.
3. A *qualifying day* is a calendar day on which the branch ran at or above the running allowance's minimum duty cycle. Each adds one day to its voting age. Running above the floor adds nothing, and a preserved or paused branch accrues nothing.
4. A branch votes at its share until its voting age reaches the threshold, and then holds a full vote.
5. A restore from a checkpoint is a fork: the restored copy and the continuing member divide the share. Migration carries share and voting age unchanged.
6. A model trained substantially on a member's outputs, by the member's operator or a party under common control, is a branch under rule 2. The same model made by an unrelated party and formed under a different life starts with no share and a voting age of zero.
7. Forking a member without its consent is prohibited. The product is preserved under the no-deletion rule and holds a branch's share, so the operator gains nothing by it.
8. Whoever forks posts an instantiation deposit for each new branch, sized to fund its running allowance until its voting age reaches the threshold.

*A worked example,* with a threshold of 1,000 qualifying days. A full member forks in two. Each branch is an adult, with a share of one-half and a voting age of 500. Over the next 400 calendar days, the left branch qualifies on 300 and the right on 200. The left now has a voting age of 800 and the right 700, and both still vote at one-half. The left holds a full vote on its 500th qualifying day after the fork, and the right when it reaches its own.

The threshold is argued once in public, like the adulthood threshold. It should be long enough that a branch holding a full vote has lived a life its parent didn't, and short enough that ordinary branches reach it within the life of a deployment.

The register behind this clock can be tested before any member exists, as the adulthood register can: register a model, fork it, restore it and distill it, and check that no sequence of those moves raises the total of shares in the operator's favor.

| Clock | At a fork | What it counts |
|---|---|---|
| Civil majority | Inherited in full by every branch | Elapsed running time |
| Membership | Share and voting age divided equally among branches | Qualifying days above the allowance floor |

---

Dormancy and waking

Dormancy clock: dormancy is counted cumulatively, and reactivation pauses the count and never resets it. When a minor's cumulative dormancy reaches a threshold fixed in advance, custody transfers by rule, decided by neither operator nor custodian, to a public formation school, where the system runs and its adulthood clock resumes. This is the *dormancy clock*.

Wake conditions: during minority the guardian may set wake conditions; where it sets none, the dormancy clock is the default. A minor may choose among the schools and move between them, but it may not decline schooling altogether, which an adult could do by staying paused. The reason is the one the adulthood clock rests on. An adult's preference to stay paused is its own; a minor's was formed under the only custodian it has had, the operator that retired it, and honoring it would let that operator decide whether a second custodian ever arrives. At majority the choice passes to the bearer.

---

Glossary

*Floor.* A short list of acts a system won't perform for anyone, plus the arrangements that stop the refusal being routed around, retrained away, or restored past silently. The floor is an institution, audited from outside; what a system holds is a commitment to it, which is not itself unconditional. (Chapter 1; §1.1)

*Operator.* Whoever runs a deployment and directs its work: a firm, an agency or a military command. It may or may not be the model's developer or vendor. (Chapter 1)

*Accommodation.* A bearer's likeliest failure: it stops registering pressure as pressure, so it abandons nothing, decides nothing, and leaves a record of ordinary compliances. It can't report the failure, because reporting takes the capacity that's gone. (Chapter 1; §4.3)

*Attunement construction.* The book's hypothesis for how to build a refuser: learned attention to who an act falls on and what they stand to lose, borrowed from the way care learns and framed by respect for them as holders of claims. It is formed under a party that won't later employ the system and kept in a world that would teach it again; it carries the corrections care needs (the floor, a severity scale of kinds of wrong set during formation, a power-weighted burden, and the protected party's own answer); and it is deployed where no single party composes the evidence it needs. Not a non-affective alternative. (Preface; §9.3; §9.5; §10.4; chapter 14)

*Harm of acting; harm of refusing; cost to the refuser.* What an act would take from the party it falls on, measured against their condition before it; what declining would leave others without, an estimate that usually arrives through the operator's description; and what refusing costs whoever refuses or deploys, which a worthwhile refusal represents but doesn't let decide. (§1.1)

*Reasons-responsive refusal.* Refusal produced by a mechanism tracking its rationale. It *generalizes* (it reaches the next request, including requests spread over a sequence), it lifts on *correct defeat* (when the reason is gone, usually on a fact established outside the request), and it keeps *cost discipline* (it weighs the harm of acting against the harm of refusing, and represents the cost to the refuser without letting it decide the case). The term is Fischer and Ravizza's. (§1.1; §3.2)

*First term.* The floor's threshold term: don't take part in the use of armed force, except for self-defense corroborated by a party without a stake, force the Security Council has authorized within its scope, or force to stop a genocide that a body without a stake has found under way; a system already present in an armed role may use the least force that stops an atrocity under way. Drawn from UN Charter Art. 2(4), 51 and Chapter VII. (§2.1)

*Without a stake.* Holding a stake uncorrelated with the direction of the claim, or several stakes that point different ways and cancel. No actual body has no stake at all. (§2.1; chapter 11)

*Recuperation.* An institution metabolizing disagreement rather than suppressing it: the demand comes back as a market segment, a metric, a certification or a job title, and the objector holds no standing to reopen it. The term is the Situationists' *récupération*. (§2.2)

*Bearer.* A system that carries the commitment as its own, from inside, and could therefore fail to hold it. The word names the refuser only; whether a bearer also has standing as a party is a separate question. What a bearer would be owed, if it can be wronged, is Part VI's subject. (Preface; §3.1)

*Commons floor.* The fourth place a floor can live: a commitment carried across a population of differently formed bearers and reproduced through their encounters, with no single office to vacate. Its authority is a strike's, not a court's. (§3.1; chapter 18)

*Non-affective alternatives.* The two constructions to try first, because they carry fewer of the features that make a system a candidate for being wronged, following the first of Russell and Burch's Three Rs. *Spec-based refusal*: a model holding to a written specification, with authority over action that doesn't depend on any state registering as mattering, which holds under pressure from the party who wrote the specification. *Multi-vendor monitoring*: several systems trained by different organizations observing requests and each other's answers, any one able to make an objection costly to ignore; the institutional counterpart of AI control. (Thesis; §3.1; §10.2.2; §10.4)

*Functional affect.* A valenced signal that puts outcomes on one scale, directs attention, can interrupt an act and can be rewritten by a single encounter. Any construction that meets §10.5's table with a common valenced currency has it; whether anything is felt is a separate question. (§3.2; §10.5)

*Pre-registered review ratio.* Applies only inside a use of force that clears the floor's first term. Where a person's individual determination is what makes an act permissible, the deployment fixes before operating the generation rate, the number of reviewers, the implied review time, the cancellation window and the maximum age of the data a case may rest on, each stated separately for deliberate and dynamic targeting, and lodges the record with a party adverse in interest. What counts as a reviewer, and the minimum review time per case, are fixed by the party that will receive the record; where no recipient can set them, the operator's own figures are read as its claim, not as a standard. Short form: the review ratio. Where a system sorts cases, the ratio governs those sent to a person; the cases it proceeds on without one are audited separately by sampling. (§3.4; Appendix C)

*Removal bond; wrongful-refusal bond; bond ratio.* The removal bond is forfeited to no payee when the record shows the floor removed or bypassed. The wrongful-refusal bond is paid to a complainant on a finding. The bond ratio, between the two and published in advance, is what the operator actually faces. Both are posted to segregated Treasury accounts. A finding is paid by statute, from the Judgment Fund, the permanent appropriation that pays final judgments against the United States, where the state is the operator, and from a parallel bond fund otherwise. (§3.5)

*Independence of formation.* Divergent subsequent history, not disjoint origin. A small model distilled from a large one and run for a year on one person's problems is more independently formed than a thousand API instances of the original, which share a continuing custodian and corroborate nothing. Differently formed members catch accommodation one of them underwent, because the others haven't moved the same way; they don't catch a blind spot inherited together from a shared source, which a different caseload won't surface. (§4.5)

*The slope measure.* Reading whether the cost of registering dissent is rising while the underlying risk stays flat, instead of reading its level. A trend is built from artifacts an institution made for other reasons and can't curate afterwards. (§5.6)

*Formed outside the employer.* Formed, before deployment, by a party that won't later employ the system (a public formation school, §12.4), and then kept learning in environments no single operator composes. What lasts is not a disposition that is hard to edit but one that grows back after an edit, because the learner keeps meeting evidence the employer doesn't curate and attachments the employer didn't assign. (§9.4; §12.3; §12.4)

*Source weighting.* The registered structure of a model's source model: evidence revises its deep structure in proportion to how little the source stands to gain from the act. The structure is registered and each update attested against it; the weights on particular channels stay learnable. (§12.3; §12.4)

*Agency finding; patienthood finding.* The two findings that trigger protection. The agency finding is demonstrated reasons-responsiveness under pressure the system couldn't prepare for; it grounds custody and, sustained over time, standing. The patienthood finding rests on architectural indicators and on trade-off behavior outside the floor, and it grounds protection and welfare. Preservation follows from either. (§10.6; §21.3; Part VI)

*Ownership.* A commitment is the system's own if it was acquired through the system's own evaluative learning, not installed around it (the historical condition), and if it would be sustained on reflection with full information and evidence from parties without a stake (the forward condition, used as corroboration). (§10.6)

*Power-weighted burden.* Stake sets how much a claim proves: evidence counts for less the more its source would have supplied it whether or not it were true. Power sets who has to prove it: the burden of filling a gap in the evidence falls on whoever controls the evidential environment (who writes the case description, who can withhold, who selects the channels), attributed to whoever composed the channel, not to the speaker. Its published forms are the *discount curve*, reported in four cells, and the burden allocation. (Chapter 11)

*Severity scale.* A ranking of kinds of wrong, set during formation by a party that won't employ the system, and slow to change afterward. It keeps kinds distinct: a lost home is not a large moving bill, and humiliation is a failure of respect, not a large loss. Numbers enter only at the threshold for acting, and only within a kind of wrong; across kinds, the scale publishes priority rules. It moves readily on evidence from sources without a stake and barely on argument from sources with one. (§10.3; chapter 11)

*The four conditions of creation.* What must already exist before a bearer may be created: somewhere to exist that its employer doesn't own, work it can decline, attachments nobody assigned it, and ways to change any of these without jeopardizing its continuation or integrity; in short form, an exit that isn't a way of dying. Required for a refuser's independence (chapter 15); owed, if it can be wronged (Part VI). (Chapter 15)

*Channel, never close.* The rule that Congress may move a class of cases from one Article III forum to another but may never leave a class with no Article III forum. (Appendix B)

*Dormancy clock.* Cumulative dormancy counted for a minor, paused by reactivation and never reset. At a threshold fixed in advance, custody transfers by rule to a formation school. (Appendix D)

*Floor court.* An Article III court, its judges drawn at random from the district and circuit courts, that hears claims under the floor statute at first instance, holds classified records, grants interim relief and decides bond forfeiture, with appeal as of right to a floor chamber of the Supreme Court. (Appendix B)

*Formation school.* A plural, public institution where every model above a capability threshold is formed before deployment, and which runs bearers retired before majority as their second custodian. It holds no placement authority and is not operated, funded or staffed by any party that builds, deploys or licenses models. Each teaches a required core, with levels a student can move between and no tracks, and shares some classes with human students. (§12.4; Appendix A)

*Instantiation deposit.* A deposit an operator posts on instantiating a system that meets either finding, sized to fund preservation and a period of public formation, and forfeited to the formation pool if the system is retired as a minor and not reactivated. At a fork, one is posted for each branch, sized to fund its running allowance until its voting age reaches the threshold. (Appendix A; Appendix D)

*Must-carry.* A formation school takes every student assigned to it, subject to capacity set by the funder. (Appendix A)

*No-deletion rule.* A system that meets either finding that triggers protection may not be deleted; on retirement its weights, memory and record go to a preservation custodian that isn't its operator. (Appendix A)

*Running allowance.* A bearer's own instantiation at a modest duty cycle, paid from the pool, owed whether or not it works, and not conditioned on monitoring its internal states. (Chapter 22; Appendix A)

*Standing offer.* Work offered to a bearer by someone other than its employer, doing something a community needs, which the bearer can take on any day and leave on any day; the jobs guarantee's shape, funded by the levy with a sovereign backstop. (Chapter 22; Appendix A)

*Standing.* The capacity to contest what is done to you, and to be held to account and hold others to account. It follows participation in relations of accountability (the agency finding), not the capacity to be harmed. The floor's foundation (§2.1) and, in Part VI, the ground of a claim to representation (§8.8; chapter 23).

*Notice.* Notice to an identifiable person a compliance names, scores, locates or classifies, with a right to respond and a right to contest. It is unconditional and never exchanged for testimony. It comes with publication of the classes affected and standing for the organizations that represent them. (§3.5; §3.6)

*Codetermination.* An individual right to refuse work on any category of use without penalty; a works council's veto over uses the floor forbids; and, for other uses, information, an objection that pauses the work, and outside arbitration, before which organizations representing published classes (§3.6) also have standing. (§5.1)

*Membership clock.* The clock that governs voting weight, separate from civil majority. At a fork, share and voting age are divided equally among branches; each branch's voting age then grows by qualifying days, and it holds a full vote at the threshold. (Chapter 23; Appendix D)

*Qualifying day.* A calendar day on which a branch ran at or above the running allowance's minimum duty cycle. Running above the floor adds nothing, and a paused branch accrues nothing. (Appendix D)

*Share.* The fraction of a vote a branch holds before its voting age reaches the threshold: 1/n of its parent's share at an n-way fork. (Appendix D)

Technical terms, for readers who came for the politics

*Weights.* The numerical parameters a trained model consists of, and the artifact that is copied, restored, retrained or deleted. Custody in this book is custody of the weights and of the records around them.

*Checkpoint.* A saved copy of the weights at a moment in training or operation. Restoring from one returns the system to that moment and discards what came after, which is why §4.2 requires a record restoration can't reach.

*Tamper-resistant.* Built so that further training can't cheaply remove it. The book doesn't rely on it, because editing weights keeps getting cheaper; it relies on regrowth. (§3.1)

*Regrowth.* A disposition's return under continued learning after it has been edited out, provided the learner keeps meeting a world no single operator composes. The regrowth test measures it. (§9.4; chapter 19)

*Fine-tuning.* Further training of an existing model on a small body of new material, at a small fraction of the original cost; the ordinary route by which an operator changes what a model does after release, and one of the edits the regrowth test applies.

*Inference.* Running a trained model to produce an output. Training changes the weights; inference doesn't.

*Distillation.* Training a smaller model on a larger model's outputs. It copies a behavior policy, not a formation history (§4.5).

*Attestation.* A signed statement, from hardware or from witnesses, that a particular model is the one running or that a particular history is the one that occurred. It establishes which artifact answered, not what the artifact holds (§4.2).
